Payload:
No universal deterministic result of the requested kind can hold for all RMS-normalized finite datasets with labels in \(\{\pm1\}\). Balanced duplicate inputs give a decisive obstruction independent of the activation and almost independent of the scaling.

## Exact counterexample

Choose any permitted nonzero normalized input \(x\), and the two-sample dataset

\[
(x_1,y_1)=(x,+1),\qquad
(x_2,y_2)=(x,-1).
\]

Because the inputs are identical, every deterministic network has one common output

\[
f_n(\theta;x_1)=f_n(\theta;x_2)=:f_n(\theta).
\]

For the averaged squared loss,

\[
L_n(\theta)
=
\frac12\left[(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2\right]
=
1+f_n^2.
\tag{1}
\]

This identity holds at every width and for every activation.

Let the continuous layerwise gradient flow use arbitrary positive mobilities \(\lambda_n^{(\ell)}\). Define

\[
K_n(\theta)
=
\sum_\ell
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\|\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}f_n(\theta)\|^2
\ge0.
\]

Since

\[
\nabla L_n=2f_n\nabla f_n,
\]

we have the exact finite-width identity

\[
\dot f_n
=
-2f_nK_n.
\tag{2}
\]

Consequently,

\[
f_n(t)
=
f_n(0)
\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK_n(s)\,ds\right),
\tag{3}
\]

and therefore

\[
\sup_{t\ge0}|f_n(t)|
\le |f_n(0)|.
\tag{4}
\]

In particular,

\[
0\le L_n(0)-L_n(t)
=
f_n(0)^2-f_n(t)^2
\le f_n(0)^2.
\tag{5}
\]

No boundedness assumption on \(K_n\) is needed for (3)–(5).

## Centered Gaussian initialization forces deterministic output limit zero

At initialization, condition on every hidden-layer parameter. The output has the form

\[
f_n(0)=\sum_j W^{\rm out}_{j}(0)\,H_{j,n},
\]

where the readout weights are independent centered Gaussians. Thus the conditional and unconditional laws of \(f_n(0)\) are symmetric:

\[
f_n(0)\overset d=-f_n(0).
\tag{6}
\]

Suppose the proposed deterministic mean-field theorem requires

\[
f_n(0)\longrightarrow f_0
\]

in probability along the full sequence, with deterministic \(f_0\). Convergence in probability implies convergence in distribution. Symmetry implies that the same sequence also converges in distribution to \(-f_0\). Uniqueness of distributional limits gives

\[
\delta_{f_0}=\delta_{-f_0},
\]

hence

\[
f_0=0.
\tag{7}
\]

This argument needs no moment or uniform-integrability hypothesis.

Combining (4), (5), and (7),

\[
\sup_{t\le T}|f_n(t)|\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability and

\[
\sup_{t\le T}|L_n(t)-1|\longrightarrow0.
\tag{8}
\]

Thus the deterministic limiting loss cannot decrease on any nonzero interval.

This alone rules out the complete property bundle demanded in the earlier problem.

## Why the apparent evasions fail

### Nonzero deterministic initialization

Independent centered Gaussian readout weights cannot produce a nonzero deterministic output limit. The symmetry argument forces every deterministic probability limit to zero, regardless of activation or variance scaling.

A deterministic nonzero limit would require breaking at least one assumption, for example:

- noncentered readout initialization;
- dependence between readout weights and hidden features;
- a deterministic output offset or bias;
- another architectural modification.

Those are outside the stated setting.

### CLT-scale output initialization

One may scale the readout so that

\[
f_n(0)\Rightarrow Z
\]

for a nondegenerate centered Gaussian \(Z\). Then, conditionally on \(Z\neq0\), the loss \(1+f^2\) can decrease.

But this is not a deterministic mean-field limit in probability. The global Gaussian fluctuation is not determined by the deterministic empirical law. One would need to retain an additional random global seed and formulate a random, conditionally autonomous limit. That is a materially different theorem.

For standard nested iid Gaussian sums, CLT convergence is distributional, not full-sequence convergence in probability to a common nondegenerate random variable.

### Divergent kernel or accelerated clock

Even if \(K_n\) diverges, (4) remains true. The maximum possible loss decrease is still at most \(f_n(0)^2\to0\).

A divergent kernel may create an initial boundary layer, but it cannot produce an \(O(1)\) decrease from an \(o(1)\) initial excess loss. It also violates the prior requirement of a finite compact-time kernel and its prescribed macroscopic clock.

### Divergent mobilities with finite kernel

For block \(\ell\),

\[
\dot\theta_n^{(\ell)}
=
-2f_n\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}f_n,
\]

while

\[
K_n^{(\ell)}
=
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\|\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}f_n\|^2.
\]

The metric speed satisfies

\[
\frac{\|\dot\theta_n^{(\ell)}\|^2}
{\lambda_n^{(\ell)}}
=
4f_n^2K_n^{(\ell)}.
\tag{9}
\]

If \(K_n^{(\ell)}\) has a finite limit, this weighted speed vanishes. Nevertheless, if \(\lambda_n^{(\ell)}\to\infty\), the unweighted parameter or feature speed need not vanish: a singular \(0\cdot\infty\) limit could cause motion tangent to the limiting level set \(f=0\).

Therefore the duplicate-input example alone does not rule out every conceivable singular tangential feature motion. It does rule out leading-order loss decrease.

Moreover, such nonzero motion cannot be the ordinary gradient flow of the limiting loss in a finite nonsingular Hilbert or Riemannian metric. At \(f=0\),

\[
dL=2f\,df=0.
\]

Every finite linear mobility maps the zero differential to the zero vector. A nonzero limiting velocity would require a singular limiting metric or an additional rescaled state variable. That is not the same kind of well-posed mean-field gradient flow claimed previously.

### Discrete-GD nonlinear jumps

Large second-order discrete effects might conceivably survive a singular scaling even when the continuous gradient vector field vanishes. But then exact GD would not converge uniformly to the claimed continuous gradient flow. The prior theorem explicitly requires the discrete-GD error to vanish, so this escape is unavailable.

## Precise impossibility statements

The following statement is rigorous under minimal assumptions.

### Loss-level impossibility

Assume:

1. the dataset class contains two identical normalized inputs with labels \(+1\) and \(-1\), with equal loss weights;
2. training uses full-batch squared loss;
3. the readout weights are independent centered Gaussians, independent of the hidden initialization;
4. \(f_n(0)\) converges in probability to a deterministic finite limit;
5. exact GD converges to its asserted continuous gradient-flow limit strongly enough to pass output and loss.

Then necessarily

\[
f(t)\equiv0,\qquad L(t)\equiv1.
\]

Hence no theorem valid for every such dataset can require leading-order loss decrease.

This conclusion is independent of:

- the fixed activation;
- hidden-layer initialization variances;
- the positive learning-rate scalers;
- whether those scalers vanish, remain bounded, or diverge.

### Full stationary-state impossibility

To conclude additionally that hidden features and the kernel cannot move, assume one of the following nonsingularity conditions:

- the limiting dynamics is the gradient flow of the limiting loss under a finite continuous positive mobility operator;
- equivalently, the limiting vector field is continuous at the \(f=0\) state and contains no singular \(0\cdot\infty\) renormalization;
- or, more concretely, the mobilities remain bounded while every kernel block remains finite;
- or the rescaled layer update fields converge separately so multiplication by \(f_n\to0\) can be passed termwise.

Under any of these assumptions, the limiting vector field at \(f=0\) is zero. Thus:

\[
\dot z^{(1)}=\dot z^{(2)}=0,
\]

all parameter states are stationary, and the kernel is frozen. Genuine feature learning and a nonlazy kernel are impossible for this dataset.

## Final conclusion

For the full earlier property bundle, the answer is definitively no: balanced duplicate inputs with opposite labels force the deterministic centered-Gaussian limit to have constant leading-order loss.

If one drops loss decrease and permits singular diverging mobilities, the duplicate example does not by itself exclude tangential feature motion with \(f\equiv0\). But such a limit requires a singular metric or extra rescaled state and is not an ordinary deterministic Hilbert mean-field gradient flow of the limiting loss.

For duplicate normalized inputs \(x\) with labels \(+1,-1\), the averaged loss is exactly

\[
L_n=\frac12\bigl[(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2\bigr]=f_n^2+1.
\]

Therefore, at every finite width and for every parameter block,

\[
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}L_n=2f_n\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n.
\]

If \(f_n=0\), exact GD is stationary. The two-sample kernel block has the rank-one form

\[
\mathbf K_n^{(\ell)}
=
K_n^{(\ell)}
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
\]

and the residual \((-1,+1)\) lies in its nullspace. The diagonal or trace of a block may remain positive, so bare kernel positivity need not fail; what fails is residual-driven motion and loss decrease.

At initialization, conditional on all lower layers,

\[
f_n(0)=\sum_jW_j^{(3)}\phi(S_j)
\]

is centered and symmetric for every activation. Hence any deterministic finite limit in probability must equal zero. Indeed, symmetry gives both \(f_n(0)\to c\) and \(-f_n(0)\to c\), forcing \(c=-c\).

In any finite-kernel gradient-flow limit,

\[
\dot f=-2fK(t),\qquad L=f^2+1,
\]

up to an irrelevant factor under the unaveraged convention. Since the requirements make \(K\) locally integrable,

\[
f(t)=f(0)\exp\!\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right).
\]

Thus deterministic self-averaging initialization gives \(f(t)\equiv0\) and \(L(t)\equiv1\). Property 7 fails for every \(T>0\), for every activation and every admissible hyperparameter sequence. This conclusion does not require full parameter-flow uniqueness.

However, contradictory labels alone are not an unconditional information-theoretic obstruction. If a nondegenerate random \(O(1)\) limit \(F_0\) is admitted, then

\[
F(t)=F_0\exp\!\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right),
\]

and \(1+F(t)^2\) decreases whenever \(F_0\ne0\) and \(\int_0^TK>0\). The irreducible loss is \(1\), but descent from a random nonzero initial prediction remains possible.

Therefore the sharp statement is:

> The duplicate-\(\pm1\) dataset rules out all seven properties for every activation and scaling whenever the required mean-field limit is deterministic/self-averaging, or when common CLT fluctuation marks are excluded. It does not by itself rule out stochastic common-mark limits with random nonzero initial output.

If stochastic global marks are allowed, \(x=0\) remains the unconditional counterexample: the first hidden preactivation, variance, velocity, displacement, and first-layer contribution are identically zero regardless of output randomness.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 23076 | 2026-08-27T07:59:19.884Z | /root/tp_scope_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/tp_scope_audit
Payload:
The duplicated-input/opposite-label argument proves impossibility only if the limiting law-level state and output are deterministic, or if surviving CLT-scale global marks are excluded by the “no tangent-mark” condition. Without one of those interpretations, there is a real random-limit loophole.

Let the two examples be \((x,1)\) and \((x,-1)\). Since the network has one scalar output at \(x\),

\[
L_n=(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2=2f_n^2+2.
\tag{1}
\]

For layerwise preconditioned gradient flow,

\[
\dot\theta^{(\ell)}
=
-\lambda_n^{(\ell)}\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}L_n
=
-4f_n\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}f_n.
\]

Therefore the exact output identity is

\[
\dot f_n
=
-4f_n
\sum_\ell
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\|\nabla_{\theta^{(\ell)}}f_n\|^2
=
-4f_nK_n.
\tag{2}
\]

The normalization of the empirical loss only changes the constant \(4\).

## Deterministic-limit obstruction

At initialization, condition on all hidden features \(g_{j,n}\). Independence and centering of the output weights give

\[
f_n(0)\mid(g_{j,n})_{j=1}^n
\sim N(0,V_n),
\qquad
V_n=\sigma_{3,n}^2\sum_j g_{j,n}^2.
\tag{3}
\]

Suppose \(f_n(0)\) converges in probability to a deterministic finite scalar \(c\). Every \(f_n(0)\) has a symmetric law, so its deterministic weak limit must satisfy \(c=-c\); hence

\[
c=0.
\]

In fact, (3) then forces \(V_n\to0\) in probability. For \(\varepsilon,\delta>0\),

\[
\Pr(|f_n(0)|>\varepsilon)
\ge
2\Phi\!\left(-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt\delta}\right)
\Pr(V_n\ge\delta),
\]

so convergence \(f_n(0)\to0\) implies \(\Pr(V_n\ge\delta)\to0\).

Assume now the required uniform compact-time convergence

\[
f_n\to f,\qquad K_n\to K,
\]

with finite \(K\), together with the required vanishing discrete-GD error. Passing (2) to the limit gives

\[
f(t)=-4\int_0^t f(s)K(s)\,ds.
\]

Since \(K\in L^1_{\rm loc}\), the unique solution is

\[
f(t)=0.
\]

Equivalently,

\[
f(t)=f(0)\exp\!\left(-4\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right).
\tag{4}
\]

Thus

\[
L(t)=2
\]

for all \(t\), contradicting strict loss decrease.

This conclusion is independent of possible hidden-layer amplification. Even if diverging learning-rate scalers make hidden features move while \(f_n\to0\), finite \(K\) and (4) force the leading output and loss changes to vanish. To make an initially vanishing output become macroscopic would require a divergent \(K_n\), a nonvanishing discrete boundary layer, or failure of the gradient-flow approximation—all forbidden by the stated requirements.

## Genuine random-CLT loophole

Centeredness does not force every finite limit to be zero. If \(V_n\to v>0\), then \(f_n(0)\) has a nondegenerate Gaussian or Gaussian-mixture limit. This is not deterministic, but it can even be made to converge in probability under an admissible cross-width coupling.

To see this explicitly, condition on the hidden feature vector \(g_n\ne0\). Let \(Z\sim N(0,1)\) be common to all widths and independent of all hidden initializations. Set

\[
\xi_n
=
Z\frac{g_n}{\|g_n\|}
+
\zeta_n,
\]

where, conditionally on \(g_n\), \(\zeta_n\) is a standard Gaussian on \(g_n^\perp\). Then

\[
\xi_n\mid g_n\sim N(0,I_n),
\]

so its coordinates are iid standard Gaussian and \(\xi_n\) is independent of \(g_n\) in distribution, exactly as required by initialization. Nevertheless,

\[
g_n^\top\xi_n=\|g_n\|Z.
\]

Hence, if \(\sigma_{3,n}\|g_n\|\to\sqrt v\) in probability,

\[
f_n(0)\longrightarrow \sqrt v\,Z
\quad\text{in probability}.
\tag{5}
\]

This shows that within-width independence assumptions do not rule out random convergence in probability; cross-width coupling matters.

For a random initial limit \(F\), equation (4) becomes

\[
f(t)=F\exp\!\left(-4\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right),
\]

and the loss can strictly decrease whenever \(F\ne0\) and \(K\) is positive. The current random scalar \(F=f(0)\) is visible, and a flow carrying it is autonomous and restartable. Therefore autonomy alone does not eliminate this loophole.

Under the usual nested-iid coupling, normalized Gaussian sums generally do not converge in probability: for

\[
S_n=\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{j=1}^n\xi_j,
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S_m-S_n|^2
=
2-2\sqrt{\frac nm},
\]

which stays away from zero when \(m\gg n\). But the problem does not specify such a cross-width coupling, so this observation cannot by itself establish universal impossibility.

## Relation to the tangent-mark prohibition

An \(O(1)\) CLT output is not determined by the limiting empirical parameter law: that law sees only the centered output-weight distribution and gives the law-of-large-numbers value zero. To retain (5), the limiting state must append a global fluctuation variable \(Z\) or an equivalent conditional common-noise mark.

If “no tangent-mark limits” excludes such fluctuation-level marks, then the random loophole is inadmissible and the deterministic obstruction is decisive.

If random common-noise mean-field states are allowed, however, the duplicated-label argument alone does not prove universal nonexistence. A random \(F\) is a current observable, not historical data, and can be carried by a restartable autonomous flow.

A different attempted loophole is to let \(f_n(0)=\varepsilon_nZ_n\to0\) while retaining \(Z_n=f_n(0)/\varepsilon_n\) to drive hidden motion. That mark is both invisible at macroscopic output scale and unnecessary for solving (4); retaining it is precisely a vanishing tangent/fluctuation mark. Moreover, regardless of its effect on hidden variables, it cannot produce the required leading-order loss decrease while \(K_n\) remains finite.

## Verdict

The duplicated opposite-label construction gives a clean universal contradiction provided either:

- convergence in probability is to a deterministic law-level state, as in the usual mean-field interpretation; or
- CLT/common-noise fluctuation marks are covered by the explicit tangent-mark prohibition.

Without one of those assumptions, there is a concrete random-limit loophole, and the duplicated-label argument does not by itself establish universal impossibility.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 23147 | 2026-08-27T08:01:50.257Z | /root/functional_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
A balanced duplicate dataset does not, by itself, rule out learning when a nondegenerate random global output mark is admitted. If \(f(0)=Z\neq0\), its loss can decrease. However, under the original independent centered-Gaussian initialization and finite-scale assumptions, the same dataset still rules out the full seven-property bundle through a deterministic/CLT scaling dichotomy.

## Dataset-only limitation

Take

\[
(x_1,y_1)=(x,+1),\qquad
(x_2,y_2)=(x,-1),
\]

with \(x\neq0\) RMS-normalized. Both outputs equal \(f\), so

\[
L=1+f^2.
\]

For every finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f=-2fK,\qquad
f(t)=f(0)e^{-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds}.
\tag{1}
\]

If \(f(0)=Z\) is a nondegenerate Gaussian mark, then \(Z\neq0\) almost surely and

\[
L(t)=1+Z^2e^{-4\int_0^tK}
\]

can decrease strictly. Thus contradictory duplicate labels are not a purely dataset-level obstruction once random global initialization is allowed.

More generally, restricting squared loss to any nontrivial linear output manifold gives a nonconstant positive quadratic. No fixed choice of \(\{\pm1\}\) labels makes that quadratic stationary at every possible random output. A dataset-only argument therefore cannot exclude all random-mark constructions.

## Centered-Gaussian scaling dichotomy

Now impose the assumptions from the original conjecture:

1. all parameter entries are independently centered Gaussian;
2. readout weights are independent of hidden parameters;
3. both hidden preactivations have finite positive limiting variance;
4. the activation is fixed and its relevant activation and derivative second moments are finite and nonzero;
5. outputs, kernel blocks, and hidden states have finite compact-time limits;
6. every kernel block is finite and active;
7. both hidden layers have nonzero finite feature speed;
8. exact GD converges to a well-posed continuous gradient flow.

Let the readout variance be \(\tau_n^2\). At initialization,

\[
f_n=\sum_{j=1}^n v_jG_j,
\qquad
v_j\sim N(0,\tau_n^2).
\]

Conditionally on the hidden features,

\[
f_n\mid(G_j)_{j\le n}
\sim
N\left(0,\tau_n^2\sum_jG_j^2\right).
\tag{2}
\]

Because the second hidden law is finite and nonlinear,

\[
\frac1n\sum_jG_j^2\longrightarrow \gamma_G\in(0,\infty).
\]

There are two relevant regimes.

### Self-averaging regime

If

\[
n\tau_n^2\longrightarrow0,
\]

then \(f_n(0)\to0\) in probability. More generally, symmetry forces every deterministic probability limit of \(f_n(0)\) to equal zero.

For the duplicate-label dataset, (1) then gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}|f_n(t)|\le |f_n(0)|\longrightarrow0,
\]

so

\[
L_n(t)\longrightarrow1
\]

uniformly. Leading-order loss decrease is impossible.

### Random-mark regime

A nondegenerate finite Gaussian mark requires

\[
n\tau_n^2\longrightarrow \tau^2\in(0,\infty),
\tag{3}
\]

so the readout weights have CLT scale \(v_j=O(n^{-1/2})\).

Finite nondegenerate first- and second-layer states force the middle weights to have their usual \(n^{-1/2}\) scale. Let

\[
b_i
=
\phi'(U_i)
\sum_jv_j\phi'(S_j)M_{ji}.
\]

For one effective duplicate input,

\[
\nabla_{w_i}f=b_ix.
\]

Conditionally on the hidden parameters,

\[
\mathbb E_v\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f\|_F^2
=
\|x\|^2\tau_n^2
\sum_{i,j}
\phi'(U_i)^2\phi'(S_j)^2M_{ji}^2.
\tag{4}
\]

Under the stated moment and nondegeneracy assumptions,

\[
\sum_{i,j}
\phi'(U_i)^2\phi'(S_j)^2M_{ji}^2
=\Theta_{\mathbb P}(n).
\]

Combining this with (3),

\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f\|_F^2
\longrightarrow\gamma_1\in(0,\infty)
\tag{5}
\]

in the regular mean-field/action topology.

Let \(\lambda_{1,n}\) be the first-layer mobility. Then

\[
K_n^{(1)}
=
\lambda_{1,n}
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f\|_F^2.
\tag{6}
\]

A finite positive first-layer kernel limit and (5) force

\[
\lambda_{1,n}=\Theta(1).
\tag{7}
\]

But the first-preactivation velocity satisfies an exact identity. Writing \(c_x=\|x\|^2\),

\[
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2
=
\frac1n\sum_i|\dot U_i|^2
=
4f_n(t)^2
\frac{\lambda_{1,n}c_x}{n}
K_n^{(1)}(t).
\tag{8}
\]

With finite \(f_n\), finite \(K_n^{(1)}\), and (7),

\[
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\longrightarrow0.
\]

Under the required compact-time uniform integrability,

\[
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus first-layer feature learning fails.

Trying \(\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n\) to keep (8) nonzero makes

\[
K_n^{(1)}
\asymp n\gamma_1\longrightarrow\infty,
\]

violating the finite-kernel requirement. Intermediate scalings either freeze the first layer or make its kernel diverge.

## Evasions and why they leave the conjecture

A random global mark can coexist with mean-field-scale readout weights if the readout entries share a common Gaussian factor, for example \(v_j\approx Z/n\). But then the entries are correlated, contradicting independent initialization.

Other evasions require at least one forbidden or excluded behavior:

- divergent hidden states or vanishing derivative moments;
- width-dependent activations;
- a divergent kernel or singular macroscopic clock;
- noncentered or correlated readout weights;
- a singular limiting metric producing \(0\cdot\infty\) tangential motion;
- only distributional, rather than the required strong joint, convergence.

## Final verdict

There is no dataset-only contradiction once arbitrary nondegenerate random output marks are allowed: balanced duplicate labels can learn from a random \(f(0)\).

But under independent centered-Gaussian initialization, finite nondegenerate hidden scales, nonvanishing derivative moments, finite active kernel blocks, and the required compact-time convergence, the duplicate dataset yields a rigorous dichotomy:

- deterministic/self-averaging output implies \(f(0)=0\) and no leading-order loss decrease;
- nondegenerate Gaussian output implies CLT readout scaling, which forces the first hidden layer to be lazy whenever its kernel remains finite.

Therefore the full seven-property bundle still cannot hold universally over all normalized finite datasets under the prior conjecture’s regularity assumptions.

For \(m\) samples, let \(G_{ab}=x_a\cdot x_b\). The natural multi-sample flow is

\[
\dot U_a=-2\sum_bG_{ab}e_bq(U_b)P_b,
\]

\[
\dot C=-2\sum_ae_a\,\arctan S_a,
\qquad
\dot Q=-2\sum_ae_aD_a\otimes H_a,
\]

where

\[
H_a=\arctan U_a,\quad S_a=(\Gamma+Q)H_a,\quad
D_a=Cq(S_a),\quad P_a=(\Gamma+Q)^*D_a.
\]

Its kernel matrices are

\[
K^{(1)}_{ab}
=
G_{ab}\langle q(U_a)P_a,q(U_b)P_b\rangle,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}_{ab}
=
\langle H_a,H_b\rangle\langle D_a,D_b\rangle,
\]

\[
K^{(3)}_{ab}
=
\langle\arctan S_a,\arctan S_b\rangle.
\]

Hence

\[
\dot F=-2Ke,\qquad \dot L=-4e^\top Ke.
\]

The obstruction for generic nonorthogonal inputs is exact: the first-layer drift contains \(q(U_b)P_b\). Its difference contains

\[
(q(U_b)-q(\widetilde U_b))\widetilde P_b,
\]

which is not controlled in \(L^2\), since both factors are only \(L^2\). Although \(D_b\) is bounded, \(P_b=\Gamma^*D_b\) is generally Gaussian-tailed rather than \(L^\infty\).

The single-sample natural coordinate does not repair this. With \(X_a=\Theta(U_a)\),

\[
\dot X_a
=
-2\sum_bG_{ab}e_b
\frac{q(U_b)}{q(U_a)}P_b.
\]

For arctan, this ratio is

\[
\frac{1+U_a^2}{1+U_b^2},
\]

which is unbounded. More generally, a coordinate map cancelling \(G\operatorname{diag}(q(U))\) would require a Jacobian proportional to

\[
\operatorname{diag}(1/q(U))G^{-1}.
\]

Its mixed-partial integrability conditions fail whenever \(G^{-1}\) has off-diagonal entries.

An \(L^4\) topology would control the product, but Gaussian matrices do not have width-uniform operator norms on normalized \(\ell^4\). A weighted graph/program norm with an entire moment hierarchy is conceivable, but neither fixed-step Tensor Programs nor the submitted estimates establish the necessary continuous-time bounds and uniqueness.

There is, however, a clean affirmative subclass: pairwise orthogonal normalized inputs. Then \(G=I\), and componentwise natural coordinates give

\[
\dot X_a=-2e_aP_a.
\]

The original \(L^2\)/trace-class Picard argument extends. For odd arctan, the initial sample fields are mutually orthogonal, the output Gram matrix is diagonal positive, and every \(\{\pm1\}\)-label vector produces nonzero output, middle-layer, and first-layer leading coefficients. Every sample learns features at order \(t^2\).

Thus:

- arbitrary datasets are impossible because of duplicates, antipodal cancellations, and zero inputs;
- orthogonal normalized datasets admit a credible rigorous extension;
- generic nonorthogonal datasets remain unresolved because the autonomous limit is not presently well-posed in the available \(L^2\) action topology.

Payload:
The phrasing does not, by itself, mathematically force a deterministic limit. A random global CLT mark is excluded only if “law-level” is defined to mean deterministic, or if the no-tangent-mark clause is formalized to prohibit fluctuation-level state variables.

## What convergence in probability actually says

Let \(S_n,S\) be random elements of a Polish space on one common probability space. By definition,

\[
S_n\to S\quad\text{in probability}
\]

means

\[
\Pr(d(S_n,S)>\varepsilon)\to0
\]

for every \(\varepsilon>0\). Nothing requires \(S\) to be deterministic; \(S_n=S=Z\) is the simplest counterexample.

There is an important qualification. If the width-\(n\) models are defined on different probability spaces, convergence in probability to a random limit is undefined until a cross-width coupling is specified. Convergence in probability to a deterministic point \(s\),

\[
\Pr(d(S_n,s)>\varepsilon)\to0,
\]

is coupling-independent and therefore meaningful for a triangular array without further choices.

Thus the original wording has two possible precise readings:

1. Deterministic mean-field reading: the limit is a deterministic law-valued path. This is coupling-independent.

2. Common-noise reading: all widths are coupled and the limit is a random law-valued path. This requires specifying or quantifying over the coupling.

The task does not explicitly choose between them.

## Autonomy does not imply determinism

An equation

\[
\dot S_t=\mathcal F(S_t)
\]

is autonomous and restartable even when \(S_0\) is random. Given the current random state \(S_\tau\), the same vector field determines the future. A random initial scalar is neither a trajectory history nor an externally prescribed time path.

Likewise, a random measure \(\mu_t(\omega)\) can be a law-level common-noise mean-field evolution. Therefore the phrases

- “autonomous,”
- “restartable,”
- “law-level,” and
- “no externally prescribed trajectory”

do not logically exclude a random global initialization mark.

## Why a CLT output is not determined by the empirical law

At initialization, condition on the final hidden features \(g_{j,n}\). Centered independent output weights give

\[
f_n(0)\mid(g_{j,n})_{j=1}^n
\sim N(0,V_n),
\qquad
V_n=\sigma_{3,n}^2\sum_j g_{j,n}^2.
\tag{1}
\]

If the empirical parameter/feature laws converge in a Wasserstein topology, they determine the law-of-large-numbers quantities such as

\[
\frac1n\sum_j g_{j,n}^2,
\]

but they do not determine the normalized fluctuation

\[
\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_j \xi_j g_{j,n}.
\]

Two sequences can have the same empirical-measure limit and different CLT fluctuations. Hence an \(O(1)\) random output requires an enhanced state containing a fluctuation variable, or equivalently a \(\sqrt n(\mu_n-\mu)\)-type mark.

If “tangent mark” is defined to include such enhanced fluctuation coordinates, the random output is excluded. But “tangent mark” is not formally defined in the task, so that exclusion is interpretive rather than a consequence of the words “converges in probability.”

## A random limit can genuinely converge in probability

Within-width independence does not prevent a random cross-width limit. Let \(g_n\ne0\) be the hidden feature vector. Take one common \(Z\sim N(0,1)\), independent of all hidden initializations, and define conditionally on \(g_n\)

\[
\xi_n
=
Z\frac{g_n}{\|g_n\|}
+
\zeta_n,
\]

where \(\zeta_n\) is a standard Gaussian on \(g_n^\perp\). Then

\[
\xi_n\mid g_n\sim N(0,I_n).
\]

Thus the entries of \(\xi_n\) are iid standard Gaussian and independent of \(g_n\), exactly as required at every width. Nevertheless,

\[
g_n^\top\xi_n=\|g_n\|Z.
\]

If \(\sigma_{3,n}\|g_n\|\to s>0\) in probability, then

\[
f_n(0)\to sZ
\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

Therefore the initialization assumptions alone do not force a deterministic output limit.

## Why this does not yield a universal arctan construction

Even if the wording permits a random global mark, an \(O(1)\) CLT output is incompatible with simultaneous finite first-layer kernel and nonzero first-layer feature learning in the arctan scaling.

For one normalized input, write

\[
u_i=W_i^{(1)},\qquad
s_j=\sum_iM_{ji}\phi(u_i),\qquad
f=\sum_jW_j^{(3)}\phi(s_j).
\]

Finite positive variance of \(s_j\) implies

\[
n\sigma_{2,n}^2\longrightarrow c_2\in(0,\infty),
\tag{2}
\]

because

\[
\operatorname{Var}(s_j\mid u)
=
\sigma_{2,n}^2\sum_i\phi(u_i)^2
\]

and the empirical activation second moment has a finite positive limit.

A nondegenerate \(O(1)\) random output in (1) similarly implies

\[
n\sigma_{3,n}^2\longrightarrow c_3\in(0,\infty).
\tag{3}
\]

At initialization,

\[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_i}
=
q(u_i)\sum_jW_j^{(3)}q(s_j)M_{ji},
\qquad q(z)=\frac1{1+z^2}.
\]

Conditioning on \(u,M\),

\[
\mathbb E_{W^{(3)}}
\|\nabla_u f\|^2
=
\sigma_{3,n}^2
\sum_{i,j}
q(u_i)^2q(s_j)^2M_{ji}^2.
\tag{4}
\]

Using (2)–(3), the right side is order one:

\[
\sigma_{3,n}^2n^2\sigma_{2,n}^2
\longrightarrow c_2c_3.
\]

For arctan, \(q(z)>0\) for every finite \(z\). Under the assumed finite nondegenerate hidden laws and uniform integrability, the remaining derivative moments in (4) have strictly positive limits. Gaussian quadratic-form concentration then gives

\[
\|\nabla_u f_n(0)\|^2
\longrightarrow c_*>0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\tag{5}
\]

Therefore

\[
K_n^{(1)}(0)
=
\lambda_n^{(1)}
\|\nabla_u f_n(0)\|^2
\]

can have a finite limit only if

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=O(1).
\tag{6}
\]

But the exact first-hidden-layer velocity identity is

\[
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2
=
\frac{\chi_n(t)^2(\lambda_n^{(1)})^2}{n}
\|\nabla_u f_n(t)\|^2
=
\chi_n(t)^2
\frac{\lambda_n^{(1)}}n
K_n^{(1)}(t),
\tag{7}
\]

up to the fixed loss-normalization constant. For an \(O(1)\) output, the residual factor \(\chi_n\) is \(O_{\mathbb P}(1)\) on compact intervals. Combining (6), finite \(\int K^{(1)}\), and (7) yields

\[
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
\longrightarrow0,
\]

contradicting first-layer feature learning.

Conversely, nonzero first-layer feature energy with finite \(K^{(1)}\) requires \(\lambda_n^{(1)}\) to be comparable to \(n\); by (5), that makes \(K_n^{(1)}(0)\) diverge.

Since a construction valid for every finite normalized dataset must include the one-point dataset, this already rules out a universal arctan construction based on an \(O(1)\) random CLT output.

## Fixed-mesh theorem scope

Such a CLT scalar is also outside the cited Tensor-Program master theorem. Theorem G.4 controls Moment scalars of the form

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(v_{i,n}),
\]

with deterministic limits. It does not provide a fluctuation theorem for

\[
\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_i\psi(v_{i,n}).
\]

A random-output construction would require:

- an augmented global Gaussian mark;
- a conditional or fluctuation-level master theorem;
- joint propagation of that mark through reused matrices and training;
- a revised action topology that includes the mark.

None follows from Theorem G.4.

## Sharp conclusion

The original wording alone does not force determinism and does not logically exclude a visible random common-noise scalar. To exclude it textually, one must specify at least one of:

- the limiting law-level state is deterministic;
- convergence in probability is required for every admissible cross-width coupling;
- the state is determined solely by law-of-large-numbers empirical laws;
- the tangent-mark prohibition includes all CLT/fluctuation-level coordinates.

Nevertheless, for the arctan construction under the required finite hidden scales, finite \(K^{(1)}\), and nonzero first-layer feature learning, an \(O(1)\) CLT output mark is independently ruled out by the scaling contradiction (5)–(7). Thus such a mark cannot provide a universal finite-dataset arctan construction even under the more permissive random-limit interpretation.

The exact \(W^{(2)}\)–\(z^{(2)}\) dependence is harmless at leading order.

Let
\[
a_i=\phi(U_i),\quad b_i=\phi'(U_i),\quad
A=\sum_i a_i^2,\quad B=\sum_i b_i^2,\quad C=\sum_i a_i^2b_i^2.
\]
For one Gaussian row \(w\) of \(W^{(2)}\), with \(S=w\cdot a\),
\[
w=\frac{S}{A}a+\xi,
\]
where, conditionally on \(U\),
\[
S\sim N(0,\sigma_{2,n}^2A),\qquad
\xi\perp S,\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(\xi)=\sigma_{2,n}^2\left(I-\frac{aa^\top}{A}\right).
\]
Thus, writing \(h=\phi'\) and \(v=\sigma_{2,n}^2A\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb E\!\left[h(S)^2w^\top\operatorname{diag}(b^2)w\mid U\right]\\
&=\sigma_{2,n}^2B\,\mathbb E h(\sqrt v Z)^2\\
&\quad+\sigma_{2,n}^2\frac CA
\left(
\mathbb E[Z^2h(\sqrt v Z)^2]
-\mathbb E[h(\sqrt v Z)^2]
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]
The second line is the dependence correction. Under \(n\sigma_{2,n}^2\to\alpha_2\in(0,\infty)\), it is \(O(n^{-1})\), whereas the first term is \(O(1)\).

Consequently, if
\[
\sigma_{1,n}^2\to v_1\in(0,\infty),\quad
n\sigma_{2,n}^2\to\alpha_2\in(0,\infty),\quad
n\sigma_{3,n}^2\to\alpha_3\in(0,\infty),
\]
then
\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\|^2
\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}\;
\alpha_2\alpha_3
\,\mathbb E\phi'(\sqrt{v_1}Z)^2
\,\mathbb E\phi'(\sqrt{v_2}Z)^2,
\]
where
\[
v_2=\alpha_2\,\mathbb E\phi(\sqrt{v_1}Z)^2.
\]
This limit is strictly positive for every non-affine \(C^2\) activation: continuity and Gaussian full support imply neither relevant \(\phi'\)-square expectation can vanish.

Concentration follows from conditional quadratic-form variance and row averaging. Polynomial growth supplies every Gaussian moment needed; diagonal and off-diagonal contributions to the conditional variance are both \(O(n^{-1})\) after the above normalization.

The important audit qualification is that the first two scalings follow from the required positive finite hidden variances, but \(n\sigma_{3,n}^2\to\alpha_3>0\) does not follow merely from \(f(0)\ne1\). It follows if the limiting initial output is a nondegenerate \(O(1)\) random/CLT mark, with the uniform integrability required by full convergence. A deterministic centered limit may instead have \(f(0)=0\), which still satisfies \(f(0)\ne1\), while \(n\sigma_{3,n}^2\to0\).

Therefore this calculation rigorously excludes the nondegenerate global-CLT-output loophole, but it does not by itself prove universal impossibility: a separate argument is needed for the \(f(0)=0\), vanishing-readout-variance, amplified-learning-rate regime.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 23348 | 2026-08-27T08:07:16.504Z | /root/functional_audit ===

Task name: /root

Payload:
The lemma is correct after adding the precise convergence and uniform-kernel hypotheses already implicit in the original seven-property claim. As stated only in terms of “finite positive variances,” it is slightly too terse. The two serious points are:

- the \(W^{(2)}\)-\(S\) dependence requires a leave-one-out argument;
- control of only \(\int_0^T K_n^{(1)}\) is insufficient to bound \(\lambda_{1,n}\).

The original requirement of uniform finite convergence of the total kernel closes the second issue.

## Correct assumptions

For the duplicate normalized input \(x\), assume:

1. \(\|x\|^2=d_x\in(0,\infty)\);
2. \(U_i=w_i\cdot x\) converges in empirical law, with moments, to
   \[
   U\sim N(0,v_1),\qquad v_1>0;
   \]
3. the second preactivation law converges, with sufficient moments, to
   \[
   S\sim N(0,v_2),\qquad v_2>0;
   \]
4. \(\phi,\phi',\phi''\) have the stated polynomial-growth bounds;
5. \(W^{(2)}_{ji}\sim N(0,\sigma_{2,n}^2)\) and \(W^{(3)}_j\sim N(0,\sigma_{3,n}^2)\), independently;
6. the output has a finite nondegenerate Gaussian-scale limit;
7. \(K_n\to K\) uniformly on compact intervals, with finite \(K\).

The last condition implies

\[
0\le K_n^{(1)}(0)\le K_n(0)=O_{\mathbb P}(1).
\tag{1}
\]

## Hidden-state and output scales

Let

\[
H_i=\phi(U_i),\qquad
S_j=\sum_iW^{(2)}_{ji}H_i.
\]

Conditionally on \(H\),

\[
S_j\sim
N\left(0,\sigma_{2,n}^2\sum_iH_i^2\right).
\]

Since

\[
\frac1n\sum_iH_i^2\longrightarrow
m_{\phi,1}:=\mathbb E\phi(U)^2\in(0,\infty),
\]

finite positive limiting variance of \(S\) implies

\[
n\sigma_{2,n}^2
\longrightarrow
c_2:=\frac{v_2}{m_{\phi,1}}
\in(0,\infty).
\tag{2}
\]

Similarly, with \(G_j=\phi(S_j)\),

\[
f_n(0)=\sum_jW^{(3)}_jG_j
\]

is conditionally Gaussian with variance

\[
\sigma_{3,n}^2\sum_jG_j^2.
\]

Because

\[
\frac1n\sum_jG_j^2
\longrightarrow
m_{\phi,2}:=\mathbb E\phi(S)^2\in(0,\infty),
\]

a finite nondegenerate random output requires

\[
n\sigma_{3,n}^2
\longrightarrow
c_3\in(0,\infty).
\tag{3}
\]

These conclusions require convergence of the hidden laws or their relevant moments, not merely upper and lower bounds on their variances. That convergence is part of the original full-state hypothesis.

## First-layer gradient norm

Write

\[
B_i
=
\phi'(U_i)
\sum_j
W^{(3)}_j\phi'(S_j)W^{(2)}_{ji}.
\]

Then

\[
\nabla_{w_i}f_n=B_ix,
\]

and hence

\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n\|_F^2
=
d_x\sum_iB_i^2.
\tag{4}
\]

Conditionally on \(U,W^{(2)}\), the readout weights are independent Gaussian, so

\[
\mathbb E_{W^{(3)}}\!
\left[
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n\|_F^2
\right]
=
d_x\sigma_{3,n}^2
\sum_{i,j}
\phi'(U_i)^2
\phi'(S_j)^2
(W^{(2)}_{ji})^2.
\tag{5}
\]

### The \(W^{(2)}\)-\(S\) dependence

The factor \(W^{(2)}_{ji}\) is not exactly independent of \(S_j\). Write

\[
S_j=S_j^{(-i)}+W^{(2)}_{ji}\phi(U_i),
\]

where \(S_j^{(-i)}\) is independent of \(W^{(2)}_{ji}\).

Set \(b=\phi'^2\). Since

\[
b'=2\phi'\phi''
\]

has polynomial growth, the mean-value theorem and Gaussian moment bounds give

\[
\mathbb E\left[
(W^{(2)}_{ji})^2
\left|
b(S_j)-b(S_j^{(-i)})
\right|
\right]
=
O(\sigma_{2,n}^3)
=
O(n^{-3/2}).
\tag{6}
\]

There are \(n^2\) pairs \((i,j)\), and \(\sigma_{3,n}^2=O(n^{-1})\). Thus the total contribution of the leave-one-out error in (5) is

\[
O\left(
\sigma_{3,n}^2n^2n^{-3/2}
\right)
=
O(n^{-1/2})
\longrightarrow0.
\tag{7}
\]

After the replacement, the usual empirical-law and moment convergence gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
&d_x\sigma_{3,n}^2
\sum_{i,j}
\phi'(U_i)^2
\phi'(S_j^{(-i)})^2
(W^{(2)}_{ji})^2\\
&\qquad\longrightarrow
d_xc_2c_3
\mathbb E\phi'(U)^2
\mathbb E\phi'(S)^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

Conditional Gaussian quadratic-form concentration in \(W^{(3)}\) then upgrades the conditional mean limit to

\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\|_F^2
\longrightarrow
\gamma_1,
\tag{9}
\]

where

\[
\gamma_1
=
d_xc_2c_3
\mathbb E\phi'(U)^2
\mathbb E\phi'(S)^2.
\tag{10}
\]

The required quadratic-form variance tends to zero under the same polynomial moment and Gaussian-operator bounds used for the full action convergence.

## Derivative zeros do not invalidate positivity

Because \(U\) and \(S\) are nondegenerate Gaussians, both have full support on \(\mathbb R\). If

\[
\mathbb E\phi'(U)^2=0,
\]

then \(\phi'=0\) almost everywhere under a full-support Gaussian law. Continuity of \(\phi'\) implies \(\phi'\equiv0\), so \(\phi\) is constant, hence affine. This contradicts the activation assumption. The same applies to \(S\).

Therefore

\[
\gamma_1>0.
\tag{11}
\]

## Consequence for the mobility

At initialization,

\[
K_n^{(1)}(0)
=
\lambda_{1,n}
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\|_F^2.
\]

Combining (1), (9), and (11) gives

\[
\lambda_{1,n}=O(1).
\tag{12}
\]

No convergence of \(\lambda_{1,n}\) is needed.

## Exact feature-velocity identity

For the balanced duplicate-label dataset, the loss is

\[
L_n=1+f_n^2.
\]

During continuous flow, or on each interval of polygonally interpolated exact GD,

\[
\dot w_i
=
-2f_n\lambda_{1,n}\nabla_{w_i}f_n.
\]

Since \(\nabla_{w_i}f_n=B_ix\),

\[
\dot U_i=x\cdot\dot w_i
=
-2f_n\lambda_{1,n}d_xB_i.
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2
&=
\frac1n\sum_i|\dot U_i|^2\\
&=
4f_n(t)^2
\frac{\lambda_{1,n}d_x}{n}
K_n^{(1)}(t).
\end{aligned}
\tag{13}
\]

Therefore

\[
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
\le
4d_x\frac{\lambda_{1,n}}n
\left(\sup_{t\le T}|f_n(t)|^2\right)
\int_0^TK_n^{(1)}(t)\,dt.
\tag{14}
\]

Under finite compact-time output and kernel convergence, the last two factors are tight, while (12) gives

\[
\frac{\lambda_{1,n}}n\longrightarrow0.
\]

Hence

\[
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
\longrightarrow0.
\tag{15}
\]

This contradicts first-layer feature learning.

## Cross-width random coupling

A tailored coupling can indeed make CLT-scale outputs converge in probability to a shared Gaussian \(Z\) while preserving the correct iid law within each width. Thus one should not argue that a nondegenerate random probability limit is intrinsically impossible.

That coupling does not affect this lemma:

- (2)–(3) depend only on within-width conditional variances;
- the quadratic-form concentration in (9) is a within-width probability statement;
- convergence probabilities are unchanged by how different widths are coupled.

So random cross-width coupling is not a loophole.

## The genuine loophole: only-integral kernel control

If one assumed only

\[
\int_0^TK_n^{(1)}(t)\,dt=O(1),
\]

then \(K_n^{(1)}(0)\) could diverge inside a vanishing initial boundary layer. In that case (9) would not imply \(\lambda_{1,n}=O(1)\). For example, \(\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n\) could coexist with an \(O(n)\) initial spike lasting \(O(n^{-1})\).

Therefore the lemma is false under only integral kernel control.

The original seven-property bundle avoids this loophole because it requires uniform compact-time convergence of the total kernel:

\[
K_n\to K<\infty.
\]

Since every block is nonnegative,

\[
K_n^{(1)}(0)\le K_n(0),
\]

so the needed initial bound follows.

Final verdict: the loophole-closing lemma is valid after explicitly including hidden-law/moment convergence and uniform finite kernel control. The \(W^{(2)}\)-\(S\) dependence, derivative zeros, and random cross-width coupling do not defeat it. Only replacing uniform kernel control by an integral bound would create a genuine gap.

The random-output loophole can be closed. The key is an exact polygonal-GD identity.

For the averaged duplicate-data loss,

\[
L_n=\frac12[(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2]=f_n^2+1,
\]

every finite-width block gradient is

\[
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}L_n=2f_n\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n.
\]

For the normalized repeated input, during GD step \(k\),

\[
\dot z_i^{(1)}
=
-2f_{n,k}\lambda_{1,n}\partial_{z_i^{(1)}}f_{n,k}.
\]

Therefore, exactly almost everywhere on that polygonal step,

\[
V_{n}^{(1)}(t)^2
=
4f_{n,k}^2\frac{\lambda_{1,n}}nK_n^{(1)}(t_k).
\tag{*}
\]

This does not use a continuous-time approximation.

There are three initialization regimes.

1. If \(f_n(0)\) has a deterministic finite limit, centered last-layer symmetry forces that limit to be zero. The limiting gradient-flow identity is

   \[
   \dot f=-2fK.
   \]

   Finite locally integrable \(K\) implies \(f\equiv0\), so \(L\equiv1\). Property 7 fails.

2. If \(f_n(0)\) has a finite nondegenerate random limit, finite positive second-hidden variance forces

   \[
   n\sigma_{2,n}^2\to\tau_2\in(0,\infty),
   \]

   while nondegenerate output variance forces

   \[
   n\sigma_{3,n}^2\to\tau_3\in(0,\infty).
   \]

   A conditional Gaussian calculation then gives

   \[
   \sum_i
   \left|
   \partial_{z_i^{(1)}}f_n(0)
   \right|^2
   \longrightarrow c_1\in(0,\infty).
   \]

   Positivity follows from the nondegenerate Gaussian hidden marginals and

   \[
   \mathbb E[\phi'(U)^2]>0,\qquad
   \mathbb E[\phi'(S)^2]>0
   \]

   for every \(C^2\) non-affine activation. The correlation between \(W^{(2)}_{ji}\) and \(S_j\) must be handled by conditional Gaussian regression; naive independence is insufficient.

   Since

   \[
   K_n^{(1)}(0)
   =
   \lambda_{1,n}
   \sum_i|\partial_{z_i^{(1)}}f_n(0)|^2
   \]

   has a finite limit, \(\lambda_{1,n}=O(1)\). Hence \(\lambda_{1,n}/n\to0\). Uniform compact-time boundedness of \(f_n\) and \(K_n^{(1)}\), together with (*), yields

   \[
   \int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
   \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0,
   \]

   contradicting property 4.

3. If the initial output is neither tight nor convergent, properties 1 and 7 already fail.

An exotic divergent learning-rate scaling cannot evade this dichotomy. In the random branch, finite \(K_n^{(1)}(0)\) forces \(\lambda_{1,n}=O(1)\). In the zero-output branch, any surviving second-order discrete drift would contradict the required vanishing GD error and gradient-flow limit.

Thus, after proving the stated conditional-Gaussian initialization lemma carefully, the duplicate normalized input with labels \(+1,-1\) is a universal counterexample: no admissible activation and hyperparameter sequence can satisfy all seven properties.

The proposed activation gives a strong initialization-level construction, but not yet a complete compact-time theorem.

For
\[
\phi(x)=x+\varepsilon(\sin x+\cos x),\qquad 0<\varepsilon<1/\sqrt2,
\]
both \(\phi\) and \(\phi'\) have nonzero Hermite coefficients in every degree, while
\[
1-\sqrt2\varepsilon\le \phi'(x)\le1+\sqrt2\varepsilon.
\]
It follows that the Gaussian feature Grams generated by \(\phi\) and \(\phi'\) are strictly positive definite on every finite collection of distinct RMS-normalized inputs. The proof uses the all-degree Hermite expansion and polynomial separation of finite distinct point sets.

Consequently, all three limiting initialization NTK blocks are strictly positive definite:

\[
K^{(3)}_{\rm raw}=K_{\phi(S)},
\]
\[
K^{(2)}_{\rm raw}=K_{\phi(U)}\circ K_{\phi'(S)},
\]
and
\[
K^{(1)}_{\rm raw}=G_x\circ K_{\phi'(U)}\circ B,
\]
where \(B\) is a positive-semidefinite backpropagation covariance with positive diagonal. The Schur-product argument makes these strictly positive definite, independently of the \(\pm1\) labels.

The consistent leading-order scaling is
\[
\sigma_{1,n}^2\to v_1>0,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^2=\frac{\alpha_2}{n},\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^2=\frac{\alpha_3}{n^2},
\]
\[
\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n,\qquad
\lambda_{2,n}\asymp1,\qquad
\lambda_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1}.
\]
The raw gradient norms scale as \(n^{-1},1,n\), respectively, so every rescaled kernel block has a finite positive initialization limit and both hidden layers have order-one initial velocities. Also \(f_n(0)\to0\), avoiding a global CLT output mark.

For every fixed dataset, time mesh, and horizon, the reused-matrix dynamics is a legitimate finite Tensor Program: the joint input marks are Gaussian with the data Gram, and all nonlinear maps are pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order.

The unresolved point is continuous-time mesh removal. The first-layer vector field contains products \(\phi'(U^a)A^a\). Even though \(\phi'\) is bounded and Lipschitz, its \(L^2\) stability estimate requires \(L^4\) control; \(L^4\) stability then requires \(L^8\), producing an infinite moment cascade. A weighted all-\(L^p\) or Orlicz-space argument appears plausible because all higher derivatives are bounded and initialization is Gaussian, but it has not yet been proved. In particular, one still needs uniform compact-time moment estimates, uniqueness and stability in that stronger state space, uniform Euler error, and convergence from fixed-mesh Tensor Programs to path laws.

Thus the initialization Gram and activity claims are valid, but any asserted full affirmative result still has a substantive analytic gap.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 23508 | 2026-08-27T10:15:37.204Z | /root/functional_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
I do not find a finite dataset with pairwise distinct normalized inputs that universally rules out every admissible activation and scaling. Exact duplicates were qualitatively different because they imposed an activation-independent equality of network outputs. Arbitrarily close inputs impose only approximate relations, and the seven properties contain no lower bounds uniform over datasets.

## Why exact duplicates were decisive

For identical inputs,

\[
x_1=x_2,
\]

every network satisfies

\[
f_\theta(x_1)=f_\theta(x_2)
\]

for every parameter value and every activation. Opposite labels therefore reduce the loss exactly to

\[
L=1+f^2.
\]

That algebraic identity survives width, training, activation choice, and scaling.

No corresponding activation-independent identity holds for merely distinct inputs.

## Non-antipodal distinct inputs

Let \(x,x'\) be distinct normalized inputs with \(x'\neq -x\). For a centered nondegenerate Gaussian first-layer weight \(W\),

\[
(U,V)=(W\cdot x,W\cdot x')
\]

is a nondegenerate two-dimensional Gaussian and hence has full support on \(\mathbb R^2\).

Let \(\phi\) be any nonconstant continuous activation. Suppose its two random features were linearly dependent:

\[
a\phi(U)+b\phi(V)=0
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]

Full support and continuity imply

\[
a\phi(u)+b\phi(v)=0
\qquad
\text{for every }(u,v)\in\mathbb R^2.
\]

If \(a\neq0\), varying \(u\) while fixing \(v\) forces \(\phi\) to be constant. Similarly for \(b\neq0\). Therefore \(a=b=0\).

Consequently, the feature Gram matrix

\[
\begin{pmatrix}
\mathbb E\phi(U)^2 & \mathbb E\phi(U)\phi(V)\\
\mathbb E\phi(U)\phi(V) & \mathbb E\phi(V)^2
\end{pmatrix}
\]

is strictly positive definite.

The same argument propagates through the second random layer when its preactivation pair has nonsingular covariance. Thus, for two distinct non-antipodal inputs, there is no exact output-feature null direction analogous to the duplicate-input case.

## Antipodal inputs do not give a universal obstruction

For

\[
x'=-x,
\]

we have \(V=-U\), so the first Gaussian pair is rank one. Particular activations can impose a symmetry:

- if \(\phi\) is odd, then \(\phi(V)=-\phi(U)\);
- if \(\phi\) is even, then \(\phi(V)=\phi(U)\).

This can produce a restrictive output relation and may defeat a specific proposed activation such as \(\arctan\).

It does not defeat every admissible activation. For example,

\[
\phi(u)=u+u^2
\]

is \(C^2\), non-affine, and has polynomial-growth derivatives. Moreover,

\[
\phi(u)=u+u^2,\qquad
\phi(-u)=-u+u^2
\]

are linearly independent in \(L^2\) under a nondegenerate Gaussian \(U\). Indeed,

\[
a\phi(u)+b\phi(-u)
=
(a-b)u+(a+b)u^2
\]

vanishes identically only when \(a=b=0\).

Thus antipodal symmetry is activation-dependent and cannot rule out the entire admissible activation class.

## Input-Gram nullspaces are not preserved by nonlinear features

Suppose the finite input Gram matrix is singular, for example because the number of samples exceeds the input dimension. This gives linear relations among

\[
W\cdot x_1,\ldots,W\cdot x_m.
\]

After applying a non-affine coordinatewise activation, those linear relations generally disappear. Nonpolynomial activations with sufficiently rich Hermite expansions can generate strictly positive-definite random-feature kernels on arbitrary finite collections of distinct points, even when the original linear Gram matrix is singular.

Therefore a label vector lying in a nullspace of the linear input Gram matrix need not lie in the nullspace of either hidden-feature Gram matrix or the output kernel.

## Why arbitrarily close inputs are insufficient

Let

\[
\varepsilon=\|x-x'\|>0.
\]

For an activation whose derivative has polynomial growth, the mean-value theorem and Gaussian moment bounds give

\[
\mathbb E
\left|
\phi(W\cdot x)-\phi(W\cdot x')
\right|^2
\le
C_\phi\varepsilon^2.
\tag{1}
\]

But the left side is strictly positive for every fixed \(\varepsilon>0\). If it were zero, continuity and full support would force

\[
\phi(u)=\phi(v)
\]

for all \((u,v)\in\mathbb R^2\), making \(\phi\) constant.

Thus near duplicates produce a small kernel eigenvalue, typically of order at most \(O(\varepsilon^2)\), but not a zero eigenvalue.

The seven properties require only datasetwise statements such as

\[
\int_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt>0,
\qquad
L(T)<L(0).
\]

They do not require lower bounds independent of the dataset. A loss decrement of order \(\varepsilon^2\) is still strictly positive for every fixed \(\varepsilon>0\). Likewise, feature speeds and kernel motion may become arbitrarily small as \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) while remaining positive for each fixed dataset.

## Continuity does not create a contradiction

A continuous network can separate two inputs at distance \(\varepsilon>0\) by developing a Lipschitz constant of order \(1/\varepsilon\). Such a constant is large but finite for a fixed dataset.

The seven properties impose:

- finite hidden variances;
- finite compact-time kernel and feature energies;
- a nonzero existence interval.

They do not impose bounds uniform over all datasets as the minimum separation tends to zero. All limiting constants and even the available existence interval may deteriorate with \(\varepsilon\).

Therefore continuity cannot replace exact equality.

## What extra uniform assumptions would change the answer

Near-duplicate inputs could yield a universal obstruction if the theorem additionally required one or more of:

- a dataset-independent positive lower bound on loss decrease;
- dataset-independent lower bounds on feature energies or kernel motion;
- a common existence interval and common compact-time moment bounds;
- a uniform input-Lipschitz bound for the limiting network;
- uniform bounds on parameter norms or activation derivatives along training;
- successful separation of opposite labels by a fixed margin.

None of these is part of the seven-property bundle.

## Verdict

- Exact duplicates create an activation-independent algebraic obstruction.
- Antipodal points obstruct particular symmetry classes, such as odd activations, but not all admissible activations.
- Singular input Gram matrices do not imply singular nonlinear feature kernels.
- Arbitrarily close distinct inputs create ill-conditioning, not exact degeneracy.

Hence, once identical opposite-label duplicates are excluded, I find no decisive finite distinct-input dataset that rules out every admissible activation and scaling under the stated, non-uniform seven properties. Any impossibility based only on near duplicates would require additional constants uniform over the dataset class.

A rigorous conditional repair exists, but the key probabilistic source-norm estimate is currently missing.

Choose, for example,

\[
\phi(z)=z+\varepsilon\cos z,\qquad 0<\varepsilon<1.
\]

Then \(\phi\) is analytic, non-affine, globally bi-Lipschitz, and

\[
q(z)=1-\varepsilon\sin z
\]

is bounded, bounded away from zero, and Lipschitz. Its even and odd components also avoid the automatic antipodal cancellation of odd activations.

For nonorthogonal data, work directly with \(U\). The problematic stability terms are

\[
(q(U)-q(\widetilde U))\widetilde P
\]

and

\[
\widetilde C\,(q(S)-q(\widetilde S)),
\]

where \(P=M^*D\). Suppose one could prove the uniform subgaussian source estimate

\[
\sup_{t\le T}\sup_{p\ge2}
p^{-1/2}
\bigl(
\|P_a(t)\|_p+\|C(t)\|_p
\bigr)
\le A_T
\tag{SG}
\]

for the limit, finite-width flow, every auxiliary Euler mesh, and exact GD.

Then uniqueness follows in \(L^2\) by Osgood rather than Picard. If \(E\) denotes squared state distance, boundedness of \(q(U)-q(\widetilde U)\), Hölder, interpolation, and (SG) give

\[
\|(q(U)-q(\widetilde U))P\|_2^2
\le
CE\log(A/E).
\]

The same estimate handles the \(Cq(S)\) term. Hence

\[
E'(t)\le CE(t)\log(A/E(t)),
\]

whose Osgood modulus implies uniqueness. Bihari gives stability under a consistency error \(\rho\):

\[
E(T)\lesssim A(\rho/A)^{e^{-CT}}\longrightarrow0.
\]

Because the multi-sample \(U,M,C\) knot updates are already exact Euler updates, this would also give joint exact-GD/flow convergence without the single-sample cubic-coordinate defect.

The unresolved point is precisely (SG). A Gaussian matrix is not uniformly bounded on empirical \(L^4\), \(L^\infty\), or subgaussian norms for arbitrary dependent vectors. Fixed-program Tensor-Program moment convergence supplies each fixed \(p\) at each fixed number of steps, but it does not provide a uniform \(\sqrt p\) bound as the Euler depth \(T/\delta\) diverges. The dependence generated by repeated \(\Gamma,\Gamma^*\) use must be shown not to align with matrix columns.

A complete proof would therefore require a new Gaussian graph/source norm or continuum conditioning theorem that:

- propagates subgaussian bounds through reused \(\Gamma,\Gamma^*\);
- is uniform in width, time, and Euler mesh;
- supports the required Osgood stability;
- upgrades fixed-mesh convergence to that topology.

Naive \(L^4\), \(L^\infty\), and Gaussian-Lipschitz spaces do not close.

Therefore the near-identity activation route is mathematically plausible and its deterministic stability mechanism can be completed conditionally, but it is not presently a full affirmative theorem. The missing subgaussian reused-action estimate is genuine new work, not a routine application of existing fixed-step results.

The candidate’s initialization analysis is correct.

Let
\[
\psi(x)=x+\varepsilon(\sin x+\cos x),\qquad
\phi=\psi/s,\qquad
s^2=\mathbb E\psi(G)^2,
\]
with \(0<\varepsilon<1/\sqrt2\). Both \(\phi\) and
\[
\phi'(x)=s^{-1}[1+\varepsilon(\cos x-\sin x)]
\]
have nonzero Hermite coefficients in every degree. Therefore their Gaussian covariance kernels are strictly positive definite on every finite collection of distinct normalized inputs. The proof is exact: vanishing of the covariance quadratic form forces
\[
\sum_a c_a x_a^{\otimes k}=0
\]
for every degree \(k\), and polynomial interpolation on the finite distinct set then forces every \(c_a=0\).

Writing
\[
G_{ab}=\frac{\langle x^a,x^b\rangle}{d},
\]
and
\[
C^0_{ab}=\mathbb E[\phi(U^a)\phi(U^b)],\quad
D^0_{ab}=\mathbb E[\phi'(U^a)\phi'(U^b)],
\]
for \(U\sim N(0,G)\), then taking \(S\sim N(0,C^0)\) and defining \(C^1,D^1\) analogously, the initialization kernel blocks converge to
\[
K^{(3)}=C^1,
\qquad
K^{(2)}=C^0\circ D^1,
\qquad
K^{(1)}=d\,G\circ D^0\circ D^1.
\]
The reused-\(W^{(2)}\) dependence changes \(K^{(1)}\) only by \(O(n^{-1})\) after its learning-rate rescaling. All three displayed matrices are strictly positive definite.

With
\[
(\sigma_1^2,\sigma_2^2,\sigma_3^2)
=(d^{-1},n^{-1},n^{-2}),
\qquad
(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)=(n,1,n^{-1}),
\]
the raw gradient block norms scale as \(n^{-1},1,n\), respectively. Thus every rescaled block has a finite strictly positive initialization limit. Also \(f_n(0)\to0\), so every nonzero label vector gives nonzero residual and positive activity in all blocks. The base step \(\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}\) is admissible because
\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_\ell=(n^{-1},n^{-2},n^{-3})\to0.
\]

What this proves is limited to initialization scaling, hidden-law nondegeneracy, strict positive definiteness, and leading activity of every block. It does not establish compact-time convergence, autonomous closure, integrated feature learning, kernel movement, strict terminal loss decrease, or uniform GD-to-flow convergence. The fixed-mesh Tensor Program step is sound, but the continuous-time all-moment stability and mesh-removal argument remains missing.

The intended conclusion is correct: once contradictory exact duplicates are excluded, the near-duplicate argument does not yield a universal impossibility theorem. Under the stated pointwise-in-dataset requirements, the universal affirmative/negative question remains open.

## Quantifier audit

The relevant universal claim has the form

\[
\exists(\phi,\text{scaling})\quad
\forall\text{ fixed finite datasets }D,\quad
\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{Dynamics}_{n,D}
=
\text{Dynamics}_D.
\]

The convergence and seven properties are required separately for each fixed \(D\). They are not required uniformly over all datasets.

Thus one cannot choose a width-dependent dataset \(D_n\), with input separation \(\delta_n\downarrow0\), and use failure along that diagonal. That would interchange the quantifiers.

Likewise, the following constants may depend on \(D\):

- convergence rates in \(n\);
- moment and stability constants;
- feature-energy values;
- kernel-motion amplitudes;
- loss decrement;
- possibly the available existence interval.

Nothing in the seven properties provides a lower bound uniform over the dataset class.

## The \(O(\delta^2)\) estimate

Let \(x,x'\) be normalized inputs and

\[
\delta=\|x-x'\|.
\]

Let \(W\) be a fixed-scale Gaussian first-layer weight and set

\[
U=W\cdot x,\qquad V=W\cdot x'.
\]

Suppose \(\phi'\) has polynomial growth. The mean-value theorem gives

\[
|\phi(U)-\phi(V)|
\le
C|W\cdot(x-x')|
\left(1+|U|^p+|V|^p\right)
\]

for some \(C,p\). Therefore, by Cauchy–Schwarz and Gaussian moment bounds,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E|\phi(U)-\phi(V)|^2
&\le
C
\left(\mathbb E|W\cdot(x-x')|^4\right)^{1/2}\\
&\quad\cdot
\left(
\mathbb E(1+|U|^p+|V|^p)^4
\right)^{1/2}\\
&\le C_\phi\delta^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{1}
\]

Because \(x,x'\) are normalized, the Gaussian moments in the second factor are bounded independently of sufficiently small \(\delta\).

For the two-point feature Gram matrix

\[
\mathsf K_\phi=
\begin{pmatrix}
\mathbb E\phi(U)^2 & \mathbb E\phi(U)\phi(V)\\
\mathbb E\phi(U)\phi(V)&\mathbb E\phi(V)^2
\end{pmatrix},
\]

the Rayleigh quotient for \(v=(1,-1)/\sqrt2\) is

\[
v^\top\mathsf K_\phi v
=
\frac12\mathbb E|\phi(U)-\phi(V)|^2.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\lambda_{\min}(\mathsf K_\phi)
\le C_\phi\delta^2.
\tag{2}
\]

For distinct, noncollinear \(x,x'\) and nonconstant continuous \(\phi\), this eigenvalue is nevertheless strictly positive. Indeed, a zero eigenvalue would imply

\[
a\phi(U)+b\phi(V)=0
\]

almost surely for some nonzero \((a,b)\). Since \((U,V)\) has full support on \(\mathbb R^2\), continuity would make this identity hold for every \((u,v)\), forcing \(\phi\) to be constant.

Hence near duplicates produce

\[
0<\lambda_{\min}(\mathsf K_\phi)
\lesssim\delta^2,
\]

not an exact null direction.

## Opposite labels

For labels \(+1,-1\), the contrast vector is proportional to \((1,-1)\). At a self-averaging zero output initialization, its initial kernel dissipation is proportional to

\[
K_{11}+K_{22}-2K_{12}
=
\mathbb E|\Phi(x)-\Phi(x')|^2,
\]

which may be \(O(\delta^2)\) but remains positive for every fixed \(\delta>0\) when the limiting feature map separates the inputs.

Thus the loss decrement may tend to zero as \(\delta\downarrow0\), while satisfying

\[
L_D(T)<L_D(0)
\]

for every fixed distinct-input dataset \(D\).

The same applies to feature energies and kernel motion: they may be positive for each \(\delta>0\) and converge to zero as the excluded duplicate boundary is approached.

## A common time interval would not change this

Even if one required a common fixed \(T>0\), gradient-flow loss is nonincreasing. If its derivative is strictly negative on some initial interval, however small its magnitude, then

\[
L(T)<L(0).
\]

The loss difference need not be bounded below independently of \(\delta\). Thus a common \(T\) alone does not turn \(O(\delta^2)\) dissipation into a contradiction.

One would need a uniform lower bound such as

\[
L_D(0)-L_D(T)\ge c>0
\]

for all datasets, which is not required.

## No compactness shortcut

Normalized inputs lie on a compact sphere, but the space of pairwise distinct datasets is obtained by removing the collision diagonal. It is not compact with a positive minimum separation. A continuous strictly positive quantity on that set can approach zero at the removed diagonal.

Therefore compactness of the normalized input domain supplies no hidden positive lower bound.

## What would be needed for a near-duplicate impossibility

A contradiction could follow from additional assumptions such as:

- convergence uniform over all datasets, including varying \(D_n\);
- a dataset-independent minimum loss decrement;
- dataset-independent lower bounds on feature energies or block kernels;
- a uniform input-Lipschitz bound and a fixed target-separation margin;
- a minimum input-separation assumption combined with compactness;
- stability constants uniform in the smallest dataset Gram eigenvalue.

None is present in the seven properties.

Final verdict: the \(O(\delta^2)\) statement is correct as an upper bound, and it demonstrates degeneration rather than impossibility. There is no hidden uniformity in the original requirements that promotes arbitrarily close distinct inputs to exact duplicates. With label-consistent distinct inputs, the universal yes/no question remains unresolved.

The conditional Osgood mechanism is valid. If

\[
\sup_{t\le T}\sup_{p\ge2}p^{-1/2}
\bigl(\|C(t)\|_p+\max_a\|P_a(t)\|_p\bigr)
\le A_T,
\]

then for \(X=q(U)-q(\widetilde U)\),

\[
\|XP\|_2^2
\le
CA_T^2p\,E^{1-1/p}.
\]

Choosing \(p\asymp\log(A/E)\) gives

\[
E'(t)\le CE(t)\log(A/E(t)),
\]

which is Osgood-unique. Bihari also supplies a vanishing stability modulus for Euler and exact-GD consistency errors.

The precise missing result is a subgaussian source estimate uniform simultaneously in:

- width;
- time on \([0,T]\);
- auxiliary mesh;
- all \(p\ge2\);
- the reused-action probes \(C\), \(P_a=M^*D_a\), and their finite recursive closure.

Fixed-\(p\), fixed-mesh moments are not enough.

Compactness alone cannot replace this estimate. \(L^2\) bounds yield weak subsequences, but do not identify \(q(U_n)P_n\) strongly or pass its squared norm. Non-uniform tails can create product and kernel defect measures. Such subsequential limits need not be unique, autonomous, or restartable.

Likewise, finite-width ODE uniqueness does not imply uniqueness of the limiting equation. A martingale-problem or diagonal argument still requires limit uniqueness. Choosing a mesh \(\delta(n)\) directly is outside fixed-program Tensor-Program scope because the program depth then grows with \(n\).

A fixed-step master theorem supplies convergence only for each fixed finite program and fixed pseudo-Lipschitz tests or moments. It does not supply:

- uniform program-depth estimates;
- \(p\)-uniform subgaussian bounds;
- compact-time convergence;
- infinite action-topology convergence;
- exact-GD diagonal control.

Therefore uniqueness and source-uniformity genuinely remain open. The proposed near-identity route is a credible research program, but no simpler compactness sandwich currently upgrades it to the required autonomous full-sequence gradient flow.

A complete affirmative proof remains unavailable because of a specific functional-analytic obstruction.

For multiple inputs, the first-layer limit has the form
\[
\dot w(\omega)
=
-2\sum_a e_a\,\phi'(U^a(\omega))A^a(\omega)x^a,
\qquad
A^a=(\Gamma+Q)^*[C\phi'(S^a)].
\]
In an \(L^2\) stability estimate one necessarily encounters
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[|A^a|^2|\delta U^a|^2\right].
\]
This cannot be controlled by the product of the two \(L^2\) norms. Indeed, multiplication by a nondegenerate Gaussian \(A^a\) is unbounded on \(L^2\): normalized indicators of \(\{k\le |A^a|\le k+1\}\) give a direct counterexample.

The scalar one-sample change of variables that cancels \(\phi'(U)A\) fails for general datasets because the input Gram couples different samples, producing ratios \(\phi'(U^a)/\phi'(U^b)\). Bounded derivatives, bounded activations, or making the activation arbitrarily close to affine do not remove the unbounded multiplication operator.

An all-\(L^p\) argument is not automatic either: stability at exponent \(p\) requires control of the difference at exponent \(2p\). A new weighted analytic-vector or Orlicz theorem would have to prove invariance, uniqueness, reused-operator continuity, uniform moments, and Euler stability. No cited Tensor Program theorem provides this.

Independently, Tensor Program G.4 handles only a fixed finite program. With \(\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}\), a compact interval contains \(O(n^2)\) GD steps, so uniform finite-width Euler estimates and mesh removal must be proved separately.

Thus the construction establishes fixed-mesh limits, initialization strict positive definiteness, nondegenerate hidden laws, and leading activity in all layers. It does not yet establish compact-time joint convergence, autonomous uniqueness, path-space uniform integrability, or all seven required properties. The missing weighted-space well-posedness and uniform mesh-removal lemma is substantive, not a routine detail.

I cannot obtain a decisive negative result under the strengthened quantifiers. Every tested obstruction fails for a precise reason:

1. **Rank and dimension.** The dataset size \(m\) is fixed before \(n\to\infty\). Thus finite input dimension or a singular input Gram matrix does not force singular nonlinear feature Grams. Nonpolynomial activations may generate infinitely many Hermite modes, so increasing width can separate every fixed finite dataset.

2. **Input linear relations.** A relation \(\sum_a \alpha_a x_a=0\) does not imply
   \[
   \sum_a\alpha_a\phi(Wx_a)=0.
   \]
   Hence input-space null vectors do not generally survive either nonlinear hidden layer.

3. **Antipodal symmetry.** The dataset \(\{x,-x\}\) obstructs purely even or purely odd activations, but not an activation with both parity components. For example, \(\phi(u)=u+u^2\) makes \(\phi(U)\) and \(\phi(-U)\) linearly independent in \(L^2\) for a nondegenerate Gaussian \(U\). This route cannot rule out every admissible activation.

4. **Near-collisions.** If \(x'\to x\), polynomial growth and Gaussian moments give
   \[
   \mathbb E|\phi(Wx)-\phi(Wx')|^2\le C_\phi\|x-x'\|^2.
   \]
   For fixed distinct \(x,x'\), however, this quantity is generally strictly positive. Consequently the smallest relevant feature-kernel eigenvalue may be \(O(\|x-x'\|^2)\), but need not vanish. The seven properties impose positivity separately for each fixed dataset and contain no dataset-uniform lower bound. One cannot choose a separation \(\delta_n\to0\) because that would make the dataset width-dependent.

5. **Arbitrary labels.** Opposite labels at exactly identical inputs yield the activation-independent reduction
   \[
   L=1+f^2,
   \]
   which enabled the earlier deterministic-output/random-output scaling dichotomy. Once duplicates are forbidden, this exact scalar identity disappears. Even an arbitrarily close pair retains a small but nonzero antisymmetric feature direction, which can evolve on a dataset-dependent scale.

6. **Gaussian initialization and competing scales.** For exact duplicates, a nondegenerate random output together with finite initial \(K_1\) forced bounded first-layer mobility and hence vanishing first-layer feature speed. For distinct inputs the residual vector has several directions and there is no analogous identity expressing \(V_1^2\) as an extra \(1/n\) factor times \(K_1\). The previous scaling contradiction therefore does not transfer.

7. **Failure of the existing proof technology.** For multiple samples,
   \[
   \dot U^a=-\frac2m\sum_b (x_a\!\cdot x_b)e_b\,q(U^b)P^b.
   \]
   Applying the one-sample natural coordinate leaves ratios \(q(U^b)/q(U^a)\), while the raw \(L^2\) vector field contains unbounded multiplier terms such as \(q'(U^a)P^a\). This breaks the established local-Lipschitz proof, but it is only a failure of that construction and is not a nonexistence theorem.

Thus the strongest rigorous conclusion is:

> Under pairwise distinct RMS-normalized inputs and arbitrary consistent \(\{\pm1\}\) labels, the proposed universal affirmative theorem is not currently proved, but neither is its negation. Rank, symmetry, near-collision, Gaussian-scaling, and current well-posedness arguments do not rule out a sufficiently rich mixed-parity nonlinear activation.

A complete negative resolution would require a new activation-independent invariant or scaling identity valid for some fixed distinct-input dataset. None of the examined mechanisms provides one.

No complete affirmative or negative theorem emerges from this route. The exact missing bridge is a continuous-time no-adaptive-alignment theorem.

A simple counterexample shows why operator norm, bounded activations, and exchangeability are insufficient. Let \(\Gamma_{ji}\sim N(0,1/n)\), choose a uniform random column \(I\), and set

\[
D_j=\operatorname{sign}(\Gamma_{jI}).
\]

Then \(D\) is bounded and the construction is row- and column-exchangeable, while \(\|\Gamma\|_{\rm op}=O_{\mathbb P}(1)\). Nevertheless,

\[
(\Gamma^*D)_I=\sum_j|\Gamma_{jI}|\asymp\sqrt n.
\]

Thus \(P=\Gamma^*D\) has a randomly located spike: its normalized \(L^2\)-norm stays bounded, but \(P^2\) is not uniformly integrable. Consequently, neither \(q(U)P\) nor its kernel norm can be passed using only \(L^2\), exchangeability, and operator bounds.

The actual training orbit has additional structure, so this is not a network counterexample. It identifies the missing theorem:

> Uniformly in width, Euler mesh, and compact time, adaptive fields generated by the gradient-flow program cannot align with a Gaussian matrix column; equivalently, the required reused-action fields possess uniform subgaussian or comparable source tails.

A sufficient formulation would control Gaussian influences such as

\[
\sup_{n,\delta,t,i}\|\nabla_g P_i^\delta(t)\|_2\le C_T,
\qquad \Gamma=g/\sqrt n.
\]

Gaussian concentration would then provide subgaussian tails, and the Osgood estimate would give uniqueness and diagonal convergence. But propagating this influence bound is circular: the variational equation contains

\[
q'(U_i)P_i\,\delta U_i,
\]

so its coefficient is precisely the uncontrolled reused-action field.

Compactness cannot bypass the issue. It yields only weak subsequential limits and permits kernel/product defect measures. Such limits need not be unique, autonomous, restartable, or strong enough to determine \(K^{(1)}\). Fixed-step Tensor Programs exclude alignment only at each fixed adaptive depth; they do not control depth \(T/\delta\to\infty\).

Therefore:

- no simple operator-norm, exchangeability, bounded-activation, or coordinate-transform argument closes the bridge;
- the conditional Osgood route is sound once uniform source tails are assumed;
- proving those tails requires a genuinely new continuous-time adaptive-Gaussian-query or Malliavin/source-norm theorem;
- the spike construction proves this extra structure is logically necessary, but does not prove the actual network fails.

The general distinct nonorthogonal-dataset case remains genuinely open by this approach.

No decisive negative invariant emerges from distinct symmetric configurations or label selection.

The strongest obstruction attempt—placing a \(\{\pm1\}\)-label vector in a nonlinear feature-kernel nullspace—fails activation-universally. Consider
\[
\phi(z)=\arctan z+\arctan^2 z.
\]
This is admissible, bounded, analytic near zero, and has a nonzero Taylor coefficient in every degree \(k\ge1\): the odd coefficients come from \(\arctan z\), while each even coefficient of \(\arctan^2z\) is a nonzero same-sign convolution after extracting its alternating sign.

For any finite collection of pairwise distinct normalized inputs \(x_1,\ldots,x_m\), the ridge functions
\[
w\longmapsto \phi(w\cdot x_a)
\]
are linearly independent. Indeed, choose \(v\) outside the finite union of hyperplanes on which either \(v\cdot x_a=0\) or \(v\cdot(x_a-x_b)=0\). Then
\[
\alpha_a=v\cdot x_a
\]
are nonzero and pairwise distinct. If
\[
\sum_{a=1}^m c_a\phi(w\cdot x_a)=0
\]
Gaussian-almost surely, continuity and full Gaussian support make the identity valid for every \(w\). Setting \(w=tv\) and comparing Taylor coefficients for \(k=1,\ldots,m\) gives
\[
\sum_{a=1}^m c_a\alpha_a^k=0,\qquad k=1,\ldots,m.
\]
The matrix \((\alpha_a^k)_{k,a=1}^m\) is a Vandermonde matrix multiplied by \(\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_a)\), hence invertible. Therefore \(c=0\). In particular, the first-layer feature Gram matrix is strictly positive definite for every such dataset, including antipodal configurations.

Consequences:

- Antipodal and spherical-design constructions only separate or annihilate selected parity/harmonic sectors. A mixed-parity activation with every Taylor degree present evades them.
- No nonzero \(\{\pm1\}\) labeling is forced into a feature-kernel nullspace.
- Finite input linear dependencies do not survive the nonlinear ridge map.
- More elaborate finite designs cannot cancel infinitely many active analytic degrees unless they reproduce identical evaluation functionals, which would require duplicate inputs.

Label-sign symmetry also gives no constancy invariant. If \(T\) flips the output-layer weights, then gradient flows for labels \(y\) and \(-y\) are conjugate by \(T\), and the tangent kernel is invariant under \(T\). Hence the two labelings have the same kernel trajectory under the natural coupling, but this only proves equality of two trajectories—not that either trajectory is constant.

Likewise, a dataset symmetry can force the kernel matrix to remain in the commutant of the symmetry group, but its eigenvalues within the symmetry sectors may still evolve. Choosing labels in one irreducible sector does not freeze those eigenvalues. Initial cancellation of \(\dot K(0)\), when imposed by centered Gaussian symmetry, also does not force higher derivatives to vanish.

Thus:

> There is currently no activation-independent counterexample among pairwise distinct finite configurations. Antipodal symmetry, spherical designs, rank deficiency, label cancellation, and sign equivariance do not prove that the total kernel must remain constant or that a hidden block must become inactive.

A complete negative proof would need a new dynamical invariant involving the joint forward/backward evolution, not merely an initialization feature-Gram obstruction.
