The April 2026 Celentano–Cheng–Montanari theorem does not apply exactly to the jointly trained two-hidden-layer network.

Their flow has the fixed-disorder form
\[
\dot\theta_t=-\theta_t\Lambda_t+\delta^{-1}X^\top\ell_t(X\theta_t;z),
\]
where:

- \(X\) is one fixed iid sub-Gaussian rectangular matrix;
- \(\theta\in\mathbb R^{d\times k}\) with \(k\) fixed;
- \(\ell_t\) acts coordinatewise and is globally Lipschitz with globally Lipschitz Jacobian;
- \(\Lambda_t\) is externally prescribed, deterministic, symmetric, and Lipschitz.

Our trained middle matrix satisfies
\[
B(t)=B_0-2\int_0^t\sum_a e_a(s)\,D^a(s)\otimes H^a(s)\,ds.
\]
Eliminating \(B(t)\) produces endogenous memory terms involving overlaps such as
\[
D^a(s)\langle H^a(s),H^b(t)\rangle,\qquad
H^a(s)\langle D^a(s),D^b(t)\rangle.
\]
These are finite-width empirical, trajectory-dependent coefficients. They are neither a coordinatewise \(\ell_t\) nor a prescribed deterministic \(\Lambda_t\). Replacing them by limiting deterministic overlaps would be circular and would not represent the finite-width flow to which the theorem applies.

Block augmentation also fails: it either requires \(k=n\), destroys the iid rectangular disorder structure, or treats a dynamically independent left state as though it were the algebraic field \(X\theta\). Path-dependent losses and trainable disorder are outside the theorem.

Dandi et al. is narrower still for this purpose. Its theorem concerns a fixed number of discrete GFOM iterations with coordinatewise finite-history maps. The network recurrence uses empirical Moment scalars—the output, residual, and hidden/backprop overlaps—which are not available to those coordinatewise maps. Thus exact fixed-mesh inclusion is not established, and the paper supplies no continuous-time or uniform mesh-removal theorem anyway.

Therefore neither result closes the proposed proof. CCM has a rigorous continuous-time theorem, but for the wrong structural flow class; Dandi provides fixed-time discrete asymptotics for a class that does not contain the exact network recurrence without an unproved extension.

The zero output initialization improves the first few orders, but does not close the continuous-time theorem.

For a bounded analytic non-odd activation such as

\[
\phi(z)=c+\arctan z,\qquad c\ne0,
\]

one has locally

\[
C(t)=O(t)\quad\text{in }L^\infty,
\qquad
D_a(t)=O(t)\quad\text{in }L^\infty,
\qquad
P_a(t)=M(t)^*D_a(t)=O(t)\quad\text{in }L^2.
\]

At leading order, \(P_a(t)/t\) is \(\Gamma^*Y_a\), where \(Y_a\) is a bounded row-wise function of finitely many initial Gaussian projections. Conditional Gaussian regression shows that this leading field is a finite combination of the initial right fields plus a Gaussian innovation, hence uniformly subgaussian. The same is true at every fixed Picard order.

The unresolved issue is uniformity in Picard order. Smallness \(P(t)=O(t)\) does not itself imply uniqueness. With only \(P\in L^2\), multiplication has modulus

\[
\omega_P(x)
=
\inf_{R>0}
\left\{
R^2x+4\mathbb E[P^2\mathbf1_{\{|P|>R\}}]
\right\}.
\]

For admissible heavy \(L^2\) tails, \(\omega_P(x)\asymp x^\alpha\) with \(\alpha<1\). An estimate

\[
E'(t)\le t^2E(t)^\alpha
\]

is non-Osgood and allows delayed nonunique solutions. Thus vanishing of the bad coefficient at \(t=0\) is insufficient on any nonzero interval.

A sufficient missing local theorem would be a uniform Picard-depth estimate

\[
\sup_{r,n,\delta}\sup_{t\le T_0}\sup_{p\ge2}
p^{-1/2}
\left(
\|C_{n,\delta}^{(r)}(t)\|_p+
\max_a\|P_{a,n,\delta}^{(r)}(t)\|_p
\right)
\le A.
\]

Together with a leave-one-column-out or Malliavin influence bound, this would yield the Osgood estimate, local uniqueness, uniform Euler stability, and exact-GD diagonal convergence.

But the leave-one-out variational recursion contains

\[
q'(U_i)P_i\,\delta U_i,
\]

so controlling it is circular without the desired source-tail estimate. Fixed-order Tensor Programs and fixed-order Gaussian regression give no bound uniform in Picard depth. Bounded \(D\), exchangeability, and operator norm are insufficient because adaptive column alignment can create square-tail defects.

Therefore \(C_0=0\) makes a local subgaussian theorem plausible and supplies the necessary small-time factors, but it does not currently prove it. Full closure still requires a new uniform-in-depth no-alignment/source estimate. Arbitrary distinct datasets additionally require a separate strict-positive-definiteness and noncancellation theorem for all block coefficients.

I find no universal dynamical obstruction under the exact datasetwise quantifiers.

The main candidate routes fail as follows.

- **Fixed near-duplicates.** If \(x\neq x'\) are not antipodal, \((W\cdot x,W\cdot x')\) has full support in \(\mathbb R^2\). Hence
  \[
  \mathbb E|\phi(W\cdot x)-\phi(W\cdot x')|^2=0
  \]
  would imply \(\phi(u)=\phi(v)\) for all \(u,v\), contradicting non-affinity. Thus the antisymmetric feature direction is strictly positive for every fixed distinct pair, although it can be arbitrarily small as \(x'\to x\). The properties contain no uniform-in-dataset lower bound. A choice \(x'_n\to x\) is inadmissible because it makes the dataset width-dependent.

- **Near-antipodal and antipodal data.** Purely even or odd activations have an exact degeneracy, but mixed-parity activations do not. Larger symmetry orbits only decompose the kernel into representation sectors. They do not force any sector eigenvalue to remain zero or constant for an activation carrying sufficiently many harmonic degrees.

- **Arbitrarily many points.** The number \(m\) is arbitrary but fixed before \(n\to\infty\). Width-independent scaling exponents are unaffected by \(m\); stability constants and smallest kernel eigenvalues may depend arbitrarily badly on the dataset. Therefore large finite \(m\) does not yield an \(n\)-scaling contradiction.

- **Dimension and linear dependence.** A singular input Gram matrix does not imply a singular nonlinear feature Gram. Dimension-dependent factors are finite constants for each fixed dataset and need not be controlled uniformly over all dimensions.

- **Label symmetries.** Flipping all labels conjugates the flow by an output-weight sign flip and leaves the kernel invariant. This shows \(K_y(t)=K_{-y}(t)\) under the corresponding coupling, not that \(K_y(t)\) is constant. More general dataset symmetries constrain the kernel to a commutant but permit its sector eigenvalues to evolve.

- **Kernel-motion cancellation.** Centered initialization can make \(\dot K(0)=0\) by symmetry, but no argument forces all higher time derivatives to vanish. A single initial cancellation is insufficient for
  \[
  \sup_{t\le T}|K(t)-K(0)|=0.
  \]

- **All-block activity.** For normalized nonzero inputs and a non-affine \(C^2\) activation under a full-support Gaussian, the relevant derivative-square expectations are positive unless the derivative vanishes identically. Thus no activation-independent initialization identity forces \(K_1\), \(K_2\), or \(K_3\) to vanish. The exact duplicate/opposite-label identity was special because it inserted an additional \(1/n\) factor into the feature-speed relation; no counterpart survives for merely close inputs.

- **Scaling.** For every fixed finite \(m\), summing the sample gradients introduces only \(m\)-dependent constants, not new powers of \(n\). Consequently the single-sample scaling balance does not become inconsistent merely because the construction must cover all finite datasets.

- **Analytic well-posedness.** The failure of the one-sample natural-coordinate argument for generic datasets—off-diagonal \(q(U^b)/q(U^a)\) factors and unbounded \(q'(U^a)P^a\) multipliers—shows that the proposed affirmative proof does not extend as written. It does not prove that no stronger state space or estimate can work.

Therefore a complete negative theorem is not presently justified. Exact contradictory duplicates provide an activation-independent invariant, but after excluding them every examined obstruction either depends on the activation or produces only an arbitrarily small, still positive dataset-dependent constant. A genuine negative resolution would require a new joint forward/backward dynamical identity valid for at least one fixed distinct-input dataset and every admissible activation and scaling.

The theorem audit finds no external result that closes the required joint limit.

- Celentano–Cheng–Montanari, arXiv:2112.07572v3:
  - Theorem 1 gives existence and uniqueness of its DMFT.
  - Theorem 2 gives weak empirical path-law convergence for the fixed-disorder flow
    \[
    \dot\theta=-\theta\Lambda_t-\delta^{-1}X^\top\ell_t(X\theta;z),
    \]
    with fixed column number, iid fixed \(X\), globally Lipschitz \(\ell_t\) and Jacobian, and prescribed deterministic \(\Lambda_t\).
  - Lemma 6.1 gives the valuable width-uniform Euler-to-flow estimate, but only for that exact flow.
  - The trained middle matrix, two independently evolving populations, empirical residual/overlap scalars, and endogenous rank-one history cannot be represented in this class. Block or path augmentation violates fixed-column, iid-disorder, rowwise-loss, or prescribed-coefficient hypotheses.

- Dandi et al., arXiv:2603.14573:
  - Definition 1.1 and Theorem 1.3 concern fixed-\(T\) discrete GFOMs with coordinatewise Lipschitz history maps.
  - The network’s residual and overlap Moments are global empirical scalars unavailable to those coordinatewise maps.
  - There is no continuous-time or iteration-uniform result.

- Agazzi–Mosig García–Trevisan, arXiv:2607.06290:
  - Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.3 give \(O(n^{-1/2})\) Wasserstein/kernel bounds for a fixed finite Netsor program.
  - NetsorK Theorem 2.4 allows restricted kernel scalars but requires nondegenerate limiting Gaussian variables.
  - The constant depends on the entire program as a structural constant, including its line count; no usable bound for \(L=L_n\asymp n^2\) is stated.
  - Its language also does not provide the exact tied \(W,W^\top\) two-sided computation needed here.

- Yang–Hu Theorem G.4, together with TP III Theorem E.15:
  - This is the exact fixed-mesh match: finite programs may reuse \(W,W^\top\) and empirical Moment scalars, without rank stability.
  - It is qualitative and fixed-program only, with no rate or program-length-uniform estimate.

Thus Yang–Hu closes fixed meshes, while CCM closes continuous-time mesh removal for a structurally different model. No reviewed theorem bridges both. The compact-time trained-random-operator limit still requires a new uniform stability and mesh-removal theorem.

A self-contained Picard/leave-one-out proof does not presently close.

The key failure is that factorial time coefficients do not imply uniform subgaussian source tails. At adaptive depth \(r\), Gaussian-program estimates naturally grow like \(p^{r/2}\). Time ordering gives \(t^r/r!\), so the summed bound is

\[
\sum_{r\ge0}\frac{(Ct\sqrt p)^r}{r!}
=
e^{Ct\sqrt p},
\]

not \(C\sqrt p\). This tail growth is too large for the Osgood uniqueness estimate.

The issue is genuine even locally. The scalar equation

\[
Y'(t)=GY(t),\qquad Y(0)=1,\qquad G\sim N(0,1),
\]

has the factorial Picard expansion

\[
Y(t)=\sum_{r\ge0}\frac{t^rG^r}{r!}=e^{tG},
\]

which is lognormal and not subgaussian for every \(t>0\). The network leave-one-out variational equation contains the analogous multiplicative term

\[
q'(U_i)P_i\,\delta U_i.
\]

Thus \(C_0=0\), \(P(t)=O(t)\), and factorial Picard suppression do not by themselves propagate subgaussian influence on any nonzero interval.

A leave-one-column decomposition separates \(P_i\) into:

- a conditionally Gaussian term using a column-removed field;
- an Onsager linear-response term;
- a remainder requiring a uniform influence estimate.

The remainder estimate is circular because its variational recursion contains \(q'(U_i)P_i\delta U_i\), the same uncontrolled field.

For exact \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) GD, moment bounds only up to \(p\asymp\log n\) might suffice for the diagonal approximation. But compact-time uniqueness of the limiting autonomous equation requires control at arbitrarily small state distances, effectively all source moments or a different strong topology. Fixed-\(p\) compactness yields only selected weak subsequences and possible product/kernel defect measures.

Therefore:

- fixed-order Gaussian regression is valid;
- factorial Picard bounds give fixed-\(p\) existence information;
- they do not yield uniform source subgaussianity or Osgood uniqueness;
- no self-contained compact-time full-sequence theorem follows without an additional no-adaptive-alignment theorem or a new analytic-source Banach-scale construction.

The obstruction is a rigorous circularity, not merely missing exposition.

The seven requirements determine the macroscopic mobility balance, but they do not produce a contradiction. Under the usual nondegeneracy implicit in the required positive limiting integrals, the unique compatible effective scaling is
\[
\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n,\qquad
\lambda_{2,n}\asymp1,\qquad
\lambda_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1},
\]
with trained output weights of order \(n^{-1}\).

For the original one-sample problem there is an exact identity
\[
V_{1,n}^2
=
4(f_n-1)^2\,\frac{\lambda_{1,n}}n\,K_{1,n}.
\]
Because \(f(0)\neq1\), continuity gives a sufficiently short interval on which \(|f_n-1|\) is uniformly bounded above and away from zero. On that interval, convergence of both
\[
\int V_{1,n}^2,\qquad \int K_{1,n}
\]
to finite strictly positive limits forces
\[
\frac{\lambda_{1,n}}n\asymp1.
\]
Thus \(\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n\) is not merely an ansatz.

Similarly,
\[
K_{3,n}
=
\lambda_{3,n}\sum_{j=1}^n\phi(z_j^{(2)})^2.
\]
The limiting second-hidden law and its surviving nonlinearity imply that the empirical second moment of \(\phi(z^{(2)})\) is finite and nonzero on a positive-time set. Hence finite positive \(K_3\) forces
\[
\lambda_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1}.
\]
The output update then has componentwise speed
\[
\dot W_j^{(3)}
=
-2(f_n-1)\lambda_{3,n}\phi(z_j^{(2)})
=
O(n^{-1}),
\]
so over macroscopic time the trained output weights acquire scale \(n^{-1}\), even if their initialization is smaller.

With \(W^{(2)}_{ji}=O(n^{-1/2})\) and \(W_j^{(3)}=O(n^{-1})\),

\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(2)}}f_n\|^2=O(1),\qquad
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n\|^2=O(n^{-1}).
\]
Therefore finite positive \(K_2\) requires \(\lambda_{2,n}\asymp1\), while \(\lambda_{1,n}\asymp n\) makes \(K_1\) order one. The same powers persist for every fixed finite dataset; sample sums introduce dataset-dependent constants but no new powers of \(n\).

If the output-weight standard deviation is parametrized as \(n^{-\gamma}\), then \(\gamma=1\) is the unique generic macroscopic trained scale. This does not force the initialization to be exactly \(n^{-1}\): an initialization \(o(n^{-1})\), such as variance \(n^{-4}\), is compatible because training creates \(n^{-1}\)-scale weights immediately. Conversely, a CLT initialization \(W^{(3)}=O(n^{-1/2})\) is incompatible with simultaneous first-layer feature motion and finite \(K_1\): after \(\lambda_1\asymp n\) is imposed, the initial \(K_1\) generically diverges.

There is no conflict with the discrete-GD assumptions. They reduce at the largest mobility to
\[
\eta_{0,n}n\longrightarrow0,
\]
which is satisfied, for example, by \(\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}\).

Finally, two-sided reuse of the trained middle matrix does not itself force non-tightness. Its Gaussian background has entries \(n^{-1/2}\) and order-one operator norm. Each macroscopic training increment has rank-one form with entries \(O(n^{-1})\); over finite time these form a finite trace-class perturbation. The coherent contraction generated by reuse is order one:
\[
\sum_j c_jQ_{ji}=O(1),
\]
rather than divergent. For a fixed finite dataset one obtains a finite sum of such rank-one increments. Proving well-posedness of the resulting multi-sample operator dynamics remains difficult, but no activation-independent blow-up or loss of tightness follows from reuse alone.

Thus the scaling theorem supports, rather than excludes, the balance
\[
(n,1,n^{-1}).
\]
It narrows any affirmative construction substantially but supplies no negative resolution.

The broader continuous-time DMFT literature still does not contain an applicable theorem.

The closest rigorous results are:

- Ben Arous–Dembo–Guionnet, Theorem 1.2: continuous spherical mixed-\(p\)-spin Langevin dynamics, but only for symmetric Gaussian polynomial Hamiltonians. It proves uniform convergence of correlations and responses, not the required full joint empirical network state.

- Ben Arous–Guionnet and successors: empirical path-law convergence for soft-spin SK Langevin systems with either iid directed couplings or symmetric SK couplings. Their models have fixed pairwise disorder, one population, confining one-body drift, and usually Brownian noise.

- Dembo–Lubetzky–Zeitouni, Theorem 1.1: full empirical path-law convergence for confined spins with fixed iid directed disorder and linear interaction.

- Arnese–Hu, arXiv:2604.05234: quantitative quenched propagation of chaos for the same fixed iid directed linear-interaction model.

None permits the required combination:

\[
B_0\text{ rectangular iid},\qquad B_0\text{ and }B_0^\top\text{ both reused},
\]
two independently evolving typed populations, nonlinear messages, and endogenous residual/overlap feedback.

Embedding the two populations gives
\[
\begin{pmatrix}
0&B_0\\
B_0^\top&0
\end{pmatrix},
\]
which is neither iid directed disorder nor GOE: it has paired transpose entries and deterministic zero within-population blocks. Moreover, after eliminating the trained matrix, its rank-one history becomes adaptive empirical path feedback, whereas the reviewed theorems require fixed disorder and fixed local drift structure.

Gerbelot et al. allows broad nonseparable discrete updates but explicitly does not prove a continuous-time limit. Recent recurrent-network and bidirectionally correlated DMFT papers located in the search are heuristic, physics/path-integral derivations, or begin directly with an already-postulated continuum model.

Therefore no reviewed theorem supplies an exact mapping. A new multi-species continuous-time DMFT theorem for bipartite transpose-correlated disorder with adaptive empirical feedback would still be required.

The natural analytic Fock/Ovsyannikov construction fails at a precise ordinary-product estimate.

Let

\[
\|F\|_r=\|r^NF\|_{L^2},\qquad r>1,
\]

where \(N\) is the Gaussian number operator. For the Wick exponential

\[
\mathcal E_a=e^{aG-a^2/2},
\]

one has

\[
\|\mathcal E_a\|_r=e^{r^2a^2/2},
\qquad
\mathcal E_a^2=e^{a^2}\mathcal E_{2a}.
\]

Therefore a product estimate

\[
\|FG\|_{r'}\le C_{r,r'}\|F\|_r\|G\|_r
\]

would require

\[
1+2r'^2\le r^2.
\]

Thus ordinary multiplication cannot map \(H_r\times H_r\) into \(H_{r'}\) for \(r'\) arbitrarily close to \(r\). Each nonlinear multiplication consumes a fixed fraction of the chaos radius, so infinitely many Picard iterations exhaust every positive radius.

Even bounded analytic multiplication is too costly. For \(W_b=e^{ibG}\), the multiplier \(F\mapsto W_bF\) maps \(H_r\) to \(H_{r'}\), but its norm grows like

\[
\exp\!\left(\frac{Cb^2}{r^2-r'^2}\right).
\]

Allocating total radius loss \(\Delta\) over \(k\) Picard steps produces growth approximately \(e^{Ck^2/\Delta}\), which is not controlled by \(k!\). Hence bounded \(q(S)\) does not make the ordinary products tame enough for Ovsyannikov theory.

Creation and annihilation operators themselves admit dimension-free radius-loss bounds on normalized vector Fock spaces, so Gaussian matrix action is not the primary obstruction. The failures are:

- ordinary products and Nemytskii composition;
- loss of a common positive chaos radius under iteration;
- absence of a finite-width-to-limit strong Fock identification.

Hida/Kondratiev Fréchet spaces move the problem to seminorm-index loss; they do not automatically supply a common positive existence time. Slower Gevrey weights might help bounded multipliers, but all matrix-action, product, restartability, and finite-width convergence estimates would need to be rebuilt.

Even a source-space limit would not automatically imply finite-width convergence: fixed-program Wick moments do not yield strong convergence of typed Fock kernels, and low-chaos contractions may contain arbitrarily long matrix words.

Therefore the proposed \(r^N\)-weighted Fock/Ovsyannikov route cannot produce the required theorem. The decisive failure is the explicit product inequality above, not merely missing technical detail.

For \(\phi=\sin+\cos\), the formal DMFT closes only at the level of an unproved response system; bounded phases do not supply the estimate needed for a rigorous continuous-time limit.

Writing
\[
H_t=\phi(U_t),\qquad
S_t=M_tH_t,\qquad
G_t=\phi(S_t),\qquad
D_t=C_t\phi'(S_t),
\]
the deterministic low-rank part of the middle matrix is exactly
\[
Q_t=-2\int_0^t e_r\,D_r\otimes H_r\,dr,\qquad M_t=\Gamma+Q_t.
\]
A cavity decomposition of the adaptive Gaussian terms \(\Gamma H_t\) and \(\Gamma^*D_t\) formally produces two Gaussian processes \(\Xi,\mathcal Z\) satisfying
\[
\mathbb E[\Xi_t\Xi_s]=\mathbb E[H_tH_s],\qquad
\mathbb E[\mathcal Z_t\mathcal Z_s]=\mathbb E[D_tD_s],
\]
together with Volterra memory terms determined by the causal response kernels
\[
R_H(t,s)=\frac{\delta H_t}{\delta\mathcal Z_s},
\qquad
R_D(t,s)=\frac{\delta D_t}{\delta\Xi_s}.
\]
The remaining equations are
\[
\dot U_t=-2e_t\phi'(U_t)P_t,\qquad
\dot C_t=-2e_tG_t,\qquad
e_t=\mathbb E[C_tG_t]-1,
\]
where \(S_t\) and \(P_t\) equal their Gaussian cavity fields plus the explicit \(Q_t\)-terms and the Onsager terms generated by \(R_H,R_D\). This is an autonomous formal covariance-response system, but its definition already assumes existence and boundedness of those functional derivatives.

The exact analytic obstruction is the first-layer drift. For two states,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\phi'(U)P-\phi'(\widetilde U)\widetilde P\|_2
&\le
\|\phi'\|_\infty\|P-\widetilde P\|_2\\
&\quad+
\|P(\phi'(U)-\phi'(\widetilde U))\|_2 .
\end{aligned}
\]
There is no estimate
\[
\|P(\phi'(U)-\phi'(\widetilde U))\|_2
\le C(\|P\|_2)\|U-\widetilde U\|_2.
\]
For example, take \(U=0\), let \(\widetilde U-U=a\,1_{A_N}\) with \(\mathbb P(A_N)=N^{-1}\), and take
\[
P=\sqrt N\,1_{A_N}.
\]
Then \(\|P\|_2=1\) and \(\|\widetilde U-U\|_2=aN^{-1/2}\), whereas, since
\(\phi''(0)=-1\),
\[
\|P(\phi'(\widetilde U)-\phi'(U))\|_2
\asymp a
\]
for fixed sufficiently small \(a\). The Lipschitz ratio therefore grows like \(\sqrt N\).

Thus bounded \(\phi,\phi',\phi''\) prevents polynomial moment explosions but does not make the response map locally Lipschitz on the natural \(L^2\) state space. Unlike the arctangent construction, no scalar natural coordinate removes \(P\): here \(\phi'=\cos-\sin\) has zeros.

At finite width, the same defect appears through
\[
\|\operatorname{diag}(P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_i|P_i|.
\]
If the adaptive fields remained uniformly sub-Gaussian, this maximum would be \(O(\sqrt{\log n})\), leading to a stability factor
\[
\exp(C T\sqrt{\log n})=n^{o(1)}.
\]
Such growth would still be compatible with a mesh \(\eta=n^{-2}\). The unresolved step is proving the required sub-Gaussian and response bounds uniformly over \(O(n^2)\) adaptive GD steps. Gaussian conditioning does not automatically do this: the query vectors depend on the same matrix, their span grows with the mesh, and finite-step tensor-program guarantees are not uniform in this regime.

Therefore I cannot certify a rigorous local DMFT theorem for \(\sin+\cos\). The precise missing ingredient is a uniform adaptive-response estimate strong enough to control the multiplication operator \(P\,\delta U\), either in an Orlicz/\(L^p\) hierarchy or directly through finite-width concentration. Without it, the covariance/response Volterra equations remain formal.

The standard action-convergence/graphop route does not complete the proposed theorem.

The primary source defines action profiles using only bounded probes \(v_i\in[-1,1]\), with convergence measured weakly by Lévy–Prokhorov and Hausdorff metrics. Its main results are compactness and representation of already convergent subsequences—not continuity or uniqueness for nonlinear flows. [Backhausz–Szegedy, Definitions 2.2–2.5 and Theorems 2.9–2.10](https://real.mtak.hu/215464/1/action-convergence-of-operators-and-graphs.pdf).

Two decisive issues remain:

1. Full-sequence Ginibre convergence is unavailable. For normalized iid sign matrices, the paper proves only almost-sure convergence along a further subsequence of every infinite index set, and explicitly leaves full-sequence convergence open. Gaussian Ginibre is not proved there. Moreover, simultaneous convergence of \(A\) and \(A^*\), essential for backpropagation, is only proposed as a generalization; the paper says its relation to ordinary action convergence is unclear. [Random-matrix proposition](https://real.mtak.hu/215464/1/action-convergence-of-operators-and-graphs.pdf), [simultaneous \(A,A^*\) discussion](https://real.mtak.hu/215464/1/action-convergence-of-operators-and-graphs.pdf).

2. The nonlinear flow is not continuous in action topology, even for \(\phi=\sin+\cos\). On the \(n\)-atom probability space, set
   \[
   p_n=\sqrt n\,e_1,\qquad d_n=\mathbf1,\qquad
   A_nx=\langle x,p_n\rangle d_n
   \]
   with normalized inner product. Then
   \[
   \|A_n\|_{2\to2}=1,\qquad A_n^*d_n=p_n,\qquad
   \|A_n^*d_n\|_2=1.
   \]
   Nevertheless, \(A_n\), \(A_n^*\), and even the self-adjoint block encoding both actions converge in the ordinary action topology to zero: bounded probes produce either an \(O(n^{-1/2})\) constant or a spike of mass \(1/n\), both invisible to weak profile convergence.

   Let \(q=\phi'=\cos-\sin\), \(U_n=0\), and \(\widetilde U_n=\delta e_1\), where \(q(\delta)\ne q(0)\). Then
   \[
   \|U_n-\widetilde U_n\|_2=\frac{|\delta|}{\sqrt n}\to0,
   \]
   but
   \[
   \bigl\|(q(U_n)-q(\widetilde U_n))A_n^*d_n\bigr\|_2
   =|q(0)-q(\delta)|>0.
   \]
   Thus the exact multiplier appearing in the hidden-layer velocity is discontinuous even under uniform \(2\to2\) bounds and bounded smooth activation. Kernel and velocity energies can likewise disappear in the action limit.

This is not merely an artificial tail concern. For a normalized Ginibre matrix \(B_n\), choose a random column \(I\) and bounded adaptive field
\[
D_j=\operatorname{sign}(B_{jI}).
\]
Then
\[
(B_n^*D)_I=\sum_j|B_{jI}|\asymp\sqrt n,
\]
so the coordinate-law squares of \(B_n^*D\) are not uniformly integrable, despite \(\|B_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb P}(1)\). This does not prove that the training orbit realizes that field, but it proves that action convergence, boundedness, exchangeability, and operator-norm control cannot exclude the obstruction.

Therefore, action convergence alone cannot justify the claimed full-sequence, compact-time mean-field gradient flow. A repair would require genuinely new results: simultaneous forward/adjoint convergence in a stronger norm-sensitive topology, orbit-specific square-uniform-integrability or higher-tail estimates for every generated source, nonlinear-flow existence and uniqueness in that topology, and a uniform Euler-to-flow theorem. None is supplied by the cited action-convergence theory.

Audit conclusion: both cited mean-field frameworks are in the wrong scaling regime.

Nguyen–Pham use \(1/n\) averaging in every higher-layer preactivation. Mapping their middle weight \(w_{ji}\) to the raw network requires
\[
w_{ji}=nW^{(2)}_{ji}.
\]
Nondegenerate raw \(z^{(2)}\) with centered independent weights requires \(n\sigma_{2,n}^{2}\to\alpha>0\), hence
\[
\operatorname{Var}(w_{ji})=n^{2}\sigma_{2,n}^{2}\sim \alpha n.
\]
This violates their width-independent neuronal embedding and uniform sub-Gaussian moment assumption. Keeping their weights \(O(1)\) instead gives \(\operatorname{Var}(z^{(2)}_j)=O(n^{-1})\to0\). Their simultaneous-width trajectory theorem therefore does not apply to the required regime, nor does it establish two-sided action convergence of a reused \(n^{-1/2}\)-Gaussian matrix.

Sirignano–Spiliopoulos likewise use
\[
\frac1{N_1}\sum_j W_{2,ij}\sigma(W_{1,j}\!\cdot x)
\]
and thus have the identical middle-layer scaling obstruction. Additionally, their assumptions require bounded \(C^2\) activation and compactly supported parameter initialization, excluding exact Gaussian initialization, and their width limits are explicitly sequential rather than simultaneous.

Output scaling can be matched in both papers; the middle layer cannot. Learning-rate reparameterization does not repair a theorem-hypothesis failure already present at initialization. Their fluctuation/trajectory-stability follow-ups remain in the same normalized neuronal-embedding regime. I found no theorem in this line proving the missing compact-time, two-sided random-operator action convergence.

Primary sources: [Nguyen–Pham](https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.11443), [Sirignano–Spiliopoulos](https://arxiv.org/abs/1903.04440), [Nguyen–Pham trajectory stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.15954).

No external theorem located covers the exact trained random-operator dynamics.

The closest families all miss an essential hypothesis:

- AMP handles repeated \(B,B^\top\), but requires prescribed Onsager corrections and generally proves state evolution only for fixed iteration count.
- Tensor-program theorems handle each fixed finite computation graph, not uniformly \(O(\eta_n^{-1})\) adaptive steps.
- Rigorous neural-network DMFT treats fixed quenched connectivity, not a matrix acquiring endogenous rank-one history.
- Spin-glass dynamical theorems concern GOE/Gaussian-Hamiltonian covariance structures, not this bipartite trained operator.
- Panahi’s 2026 Gaussian-comparison result fixes algorithmic depth and analyzes a perturbed perceptron/Gaussian-mixture system; it supplies neither growing-step nor path-energy convergence.

The block symmetrization
\[
A=\begin{pmatrix}0&B^\top\\ B&0\end{pmatrix}
\]
does not unlock GOE results: \(A\) has a bipartite variance profile, mirrored dependencies, and becomes time-dependent under training. Replacing the blocks by independent matrices destroys the exact \(B/B^\top\) feedback.

Thus the affirmative route still requires a genuinely new quantitative lemma: compact-time, width-uniform concentration/stability of adaptive Gaussian operator actions, strong enough to pass from fixed meshes to \(\eta_n\to0\), including path energies and uniform integrability. Fixed-step tensor-program or state-evolution results cannot legitimately supply that bridge.

The proposed orbit energy gives a genuine compactness estimate, but it does not close uniqueness, response control, or the finite-width Euler argument.

For a dataset with Gram matrix \(R\), each neuron carries
\[
U_i=(W_i\cdot x_a)_{a=1}^m.
\]
Since \(\operatorname{Ran}R=\operatorname{Ran}X\), both initialization and every first-layer update keep \(U_i(t)\in\operatorname{Ran}R\). With
\[
q(u)=\frac1{1+u^2},\qquad
\dot U_i=-\frac2m R A_i,\qquad
(A_i)_a=e_aq(U_i^a)P_i^a,
\]
define
\[
E_i=\frac12U_i^\top R^+U_i.
\]
Then, exactly,
\[
\dot E_i
=
-\frac2m\sum_{a=1}^m U_i^ae_aq(U_i^a)P_i^a.
\]
Because
\[
|u|q(u)=\frac{|u|}{1+u^2}\le\frac12,
\]
on any interval with bounded residuals,
\[
E_i(t)
\le
E_i(0)+C\int_0^t\sum_a|P_i^a(s)|\,ds.
\]
On \(\operatorname{Ran}R\), \(E_i\) is coercive:
\[
|U_i|^2\le 2\lambda_{\max}(R)E_i.
\]
Consequently, an empirical compact-time \(L^2\) bound for \(P\) implies an empirical \(L^1\) bound for \(E\), tightness of \(U\), and uniform integrability of \(|U|^2\). Indeed,
\[
\left(\int_0^T |P_i(s)|\,ds\right)^2
\le T\int_0^T|P_i(s)|^2\,ds.
\]
This is a useful orbit-specific improvement over a bare \(L^2\) estimate.

It does not control differences. Comparing two trajectories produces
\[
P\bigl(q(U)-q(\widetilde U)\bigr),
\]
and there is no dimension-free estimate
\[
\|P(q(U)-q(\widetilde U))\|_{2,n}
\le C\|P\|_{2,n}\|U-\widetilde U\|_{2,n}.
\]
To see this, choose a point \(u_0\) where \(q'(u_0)\neq0\), let one particle carry
\[
P_1=\sqrt n,\qquad
\widetilde U_1-U_1=a,
\]
and set the corresponding quantities to zero on all other particles. Then
\[
\|P\|_{2,n}=1,\qquad
\|\widetilde U-U\|_{2,n}=\frac{|a|}{\sqrt n},
\]
whereas, for fixed sufficiently small \(a\),
\[
\|P(q(\widetilde U)-q(U))\|_{2,n}
=
\bigl|q(u_0+a)-q(u_0)\bigr|
\asymp |a|.
\]
Thus the Lipschitz ratio is \(O(\sqrt n)\).

This spike is fully compatible with the coercive estimate: it occupies only \(1/n\) of the particles and contributes order one to the empirical \(P\)-second moment. It also remains compatible with deterministic operator-norm bounds for the reused Gaussian matrix. Hence the orbit energy itself cannot exclude precisely the configurations that destroy response stability.

For prescribed \(P_i(t)\), the scalar characteristic equation remains pathwise unique because its Lipschitz coefficient is proportional to \(|P_i(t)|\), which is time-integrable almost surely. But comparing two coupled reused-\(B_0,B_0^\top\) systems requires control of random Gronwall factors such as
\[
\exp\!\left(C\int_0^T|P_i(s)|\,ds\right).
\]
The energy estimate gives polynomial averaged bounds only. It gives neither uniform exponential integrability nor a sufficiently strong tail bound.

This is where Gaussian conditioning would need to enter. If \(D\) were independent of \(B_0\), \(B_0^\top D\) would have straightforward Gaussian tails. Here \(D\) is an adaptive function of the same \(B_0\) through both forward and backward passes. Conditioning on \(D\) is therefore invalid, and proving concentration by Gaussian Lipschitz methods requires the very response bound that is missing. The argument is circular.

The same issue blocks the discrete approximation. A finite-dimensional stability estimate involves
\[
\max_i|P_i|.
\]
The energy bound permits the deterministic estimate \(O(\sqrt n)\), producing a Gronwall factor \(e^{C\sqrt n}\), which is not compensated by \(\eta=n^{-2}\). A sub-Gaussian bound would improve this to \(e^{C\sqrt{\log n}}=n^{o(1)}\), but no such adaptive uniform tail estimate has been established.

Therefore the exact status is:

- coercivity, compact-time second-moment tightness, and uniform integrability of the first hidden state can be proved;
- the estimate does not yield response control or continuity of the reused two-sided Gaussian action;
- uniqueness of the limiting coupled system, identification of subsequential limits, and dimension-uniform Euler/GD error remain unproved.

The spike construction refutes the claimed deterministic bridge from orbit energy to stability. It does not prove that the actual random orbit develops such spikes, so it is a fatal gap in this affirmative proof route rather than a nonexistence theorem.

Payload:
I found no valid activation-independent negative resolution from finite distinct datasets.

The strongest candidate obstructions fail for precise reasons:

- Antipodal inputs obstruct only activations with a parity symmetry. An activation such as \(\sin+\cos\) has both even and odd parts and separates \(x\) from \(-x\).
- Symmetric datasets with character labels work only if the network omits that harmonic. Rich analytic activations can contain every degree and parity.
- Nonrepresentability is insufficient: the requirements demand only \(L(T)<L(0)\), not interpolation. Gradient flow can reduce the component of the initial prediction lying in the realizable space while leaving the label component orthogonal to that space unchanged.
- Arbitrarily close distinct inputs yield small coefficients, but the existence interval and constants may depend on the dataset.
- Low rank of the input Gram matrix does not force low rank of the nonlinear feature kernel.

There is a limited polynomial-activation obstruction. If \(\deg\phi=r<\infty\), a fixed-depth bias-free network produces input polynomials of bounded degree \(D\). On a sufficiently large normalized hypercube, a parity label of degree \(>D\) is orthogonal to the entire model class and every parameter tangent. However,
\[
\nabla_\theta L
=2\langle f-y,\partial_\theta f\rangle
=2\langle f,\partial_\theta f\rangle,
\]
so this does not force stationarity unless the limiting initial prediction is exactly zero. A scaling with nonzero initial prediction can still decrease loss by moving that prediction toward zero. In any case, this argument does not apply to analytic activations with infinitely many nonzero degrees.

Thus distinctness removes the clean information-theoretic obstruction supplied by duplicate contradictory samples. A universal negative theorem would need a different scaling incompatibility or dynamical invariant; symmetry, representability, antipodes, and finite-dimensional input geometry do not presently provide one.

The seven requirements do not force a scaling contradiction. They instead select the usual joint mean-field balance.

Under finite nondegenerate hidden states,
\[
\sigma_{1,n}\asymp1,\qquad \sigma_{2,n}\asymp n^{-1/2}.
\]
Writing \(s_{3,n}=\sigma_{3,n}\), the initialization orders are
\[
\|\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f\|^2\asymp n,\quad
\|\nabla_{W^{(2)}}f\|^2\asymp n^2s_{3,n}^2,\quad
\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f\|^2\asymp ns_{3,n}^2.
\]
Consequently, activity of all kernel blocks requires
\[
\lambda_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1},\qquad
\lambda_{2,n}\asymp (n^2s_{3,n}^2)^{-1},\qquad
\lambda_{1,n}\asymp (ns_{3,n}^2)^{-1}.
\]
The resulting feature-speed orders are
\[
V_{1,n}^2\asymp \frac1{n^2s_{3,n}^2},
\qquad
V_{2,n}^{\rm direct}\asymp\frac1{ns_{3,n}}.
\]
Thus simultaneous nonzero finite motion forces
\[
s_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1},
\qquad
(\lambda_{1,n},\lambda_{2,n},\lambda_{3,n})
\asymp(n,1,n^{-1}).
\]
This is consistent with exact GD because the hypothesis permits \(\eta_{0,n}=o(n^{-1})\). By contrast, the CLT output scale \(s_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1/2}\) freezes both hidden layers.

Nor does \(C_0\to0\) contradict integrated activity. At the standard scaling, raw output weights vanish as \(n^{-1}\), while normalized particles \(nW_j^{(3)}\) remain order one. With even smaller—or exactly zero—output initialization, the first two kernel blocks may vanish at \(t=0\), but output-layer motion creates
\[
W_j^{(3)}(t)=O(t/n).
\]
Then hidden velocities are \(O(t)\), hidden displacements are \(O(t^2)\), and the corresponding kernel integrals are positive for every \(T>0\), assuming their leading coefficients are nonzero. Properties 4 and 5 require positivity of time integrals, not positivity at \(t=0\).

Finally, reused-adjoint spike examples prove that operator-norm or action convergence alone does not supply uniform integrability. They do not prove that the exact Gaussian-initialized training orbit necessarily develops such spikes. Hence they expose a missing proof estimate, not nonexistence.

The rigorous conclusion is therefore: exponent balances and delayed motion yield no negative theorem. They isolate the only plausible regime,
\[
\sigma_{2,n}\asymp n^{-1/2},\quad
\sigma_{3,n}\asymp n^{-1},\quad
\lambda_n\asymp(n,1,n^{-1}),
\]
while the genuine unresolved issue remains compact-time control and uniqueness of the reused-adjoint nonlinear limit.

The paper is a genuine compact-time \(W_2\) path-limit result, but it cannot be applied or extended verbatim.

Its microscopic theorem is restricted to
\[
d\theta=\{-\beta X^\top(X\theta-y)+s(\theta_j,\alpha)\}\,dt+\sqrt2\,db,
\]
with one evolving population, coordinate-separable \(s\), and a fixed-dimensional parameter \(\alpha\) driven by the empirical law of that population. The proposed term
\[
\operatorname{diag}(q(U))B_0^\top D
\]
already violates this form: it multiplies a random-matrix action by an evolving coordinate mark and depends on a second evolving row population. The simultaneous forward action \(B_0H\) and rank-one-history overlaps introduce further cross-response kernels absent from Theorems 2.4–2.5. Deterministic gradient flow is also not a stated specialization because the theorem fixes nonzero Brownian forcing.

The first concrete failure in the AMP proof is equation (79): its linear row message reconstructs exactly \(X^\top(X\theta-y)\). The proposed dynamics requires additional vector channels and nonlinear cross-channel multiplication, so equations (61)–(64), the five-kernel DMFT closure, and the fixed-point contraction no longer follow.

Generic vector AMP likely can encode the dynamics at each fixed mesh. But extending Sections 4.2–4.4 requires new work: a matrix-valued two-population response system, existence and uniqueness, adaptive overlaps from both empirical laws, zero-noise dynamics, and uniform mesh removal. Thus this paper is a strong methodological blueprint, not the missing theorem.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 25912 | 2026-08-27T11:43:15.663Z | /root/functional_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
The \(q'/q\) identity gives a useful response estimate only when the dataset Gram matrix is invertible. It does not survive singular \(R\), and loss dissipation does not repair the missing directions.

Let
\[
A_i^a=e_aq(U_i^a)P_i^a,\qquad
\dot U_i=-\frac2mRA_i.
\]
For arctangent,
\[
q(u)=\frac1{1+u^2},
\qquad
\left|\frac{q'(u)}{q(u)}\right|
=\frac{2|u|}{1+u^2}\le1.
\]
The self-variation coefficient is therefore
\[
e_aP_i^aq'(U_i^a)
=
\frac{q'(U_i^a)}{q(U_i^a)}A_i^a.
\]

If \(R\) is invertible, then
\[
A_i=-\frac m2R^{-1}\dot U_i,
\]
so
\[
\int_0^T|e_aP_i^aq'(U_i^a)|\,dt
\le
\frac m2\|R^{-1}\|
\int_0^T|\dot U_i|\,dt.
\]
Thus, for full-rank datasets, the dangerous diagonal response coefficient can indeed be controlled by first-layer path length, with a dataset-dependent constant. This is a substantive positive observation.

For singular \(R\), however,
\[
\dot U_i=-\frac2mR\Pi_{\operatorname{Ran}R}A_i
\]
contains no information about
\[
A_i^{\ker}=\Pi_{\ker R}A_i.
\]
Nevertheless, linearizing the drift produces
\[
R\,\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac{q'(U_i)}{q(U_i)}A_i\right)\delta U_i.
\]
Although \(RA_i^{\ker}=0\), generally
\[
R\,\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac{q'(U_i)}{q(U_i)}A_i^{\ker}\right)\delta U_i
\neq0.
\]
Coordinatewise multiplication does not preserve \(\ker R\).

This can be seen explicitly with three normalized planar inputs at angles \(0,2\pi/3,4\pi/3\). Their Gram matrix has
\[
\ker R=\operatorname{span}\{(1,1,1)\},
\qquad
\operatorname{Ran}R=\{v:v_1+v_2+v_3=0\}.
\]
Take \(A^{\ker}=c(1,1,1)\). Its contribution to the base velocity is exactly zero. But if the three values
\[
\frac{q'(U^a)}{q(U^a)}
\]
are unequal, then for a generic \(\delta U\in\operatorname{Ran}R\),
\[
R\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac{q'(U)}{q(U)}A^{\ker}\right)\delta U\neq0.
\]
Thus a component completely invisible to both first-layer path length and the corresponding gradient dissipation reappears in the variational equation.

This is not merely an arbitrary off-orbit spike. At finite width the vector \(A_i\) is generated by the exact backpropagated sample fields. For generic weights it has no reason to lie in \(\operatorname{Ran}R\); the Gaussian initialization has full support, and neighborhoods of states with a prescribed nonzero \(\ker R\)-component occur with positive probability. Exact equality \(A_i\in\ker R\) is unnecessary: the point is that no path-length estimate controls its kernel projection.

Truncation also fails to close the proof. From an empirical \(L^2\) bound,
\[
\frac1n\#\{i:|P_i|>M\}\le \frac{C}{M^2},
\]
and the bad-set contribution to an \(L^1\) drift is \(O(M^{-1})\). On the good set, however, the response estimate costs
\[
e^{CMT}.
\]
There is no choice \(M\to\infty\) for which the polynomial truncation error remains controlled after multiplication by this exponential stability factor. Loss dissipation provides finite quadratic path length, not the exponential tail estimate needed here.

A leave-one-column/row stopping argument is plausible but currently circular. Removing a Gaussian column makes the leading cavity field conditionally Gaussian, suggesting
\[
\max_i|P_i|\lesssim\sqrt{\log n}.
\]
If established, this would give only \(n^{o(1)}\) response growth and could be compatible with \(\eta=n^{-2}\). But estimating the difference between the true and cavity trajectories requires controlling the influence of the removed column through both \(B_0\) and \(B_0^\top\). That influence satisfies the same response equation containing the uncontrolled singular-Gram component above. Conditional Gaussianity of the cavity source alone does not close the bootstrap.

Hence this strategy gives:

- a promising path-length response bound for invertible \(R\);
- no corresponding bound when \(m>d\) or \(R\) is otherwise singular;
- insufficient bad-particle truncation from loss dissipation;
- a plausible but unproved short-time leave-one-out bootstrap.

The precise fatal gap is the component of \(e\,q(U)\odot P\) in \(\ker R\): it is invisible to the actual first-layer motion and dissipation, but coordinatewise differentiation rotates it back into \(\operatorname{Ran}R\) in the response dynamics.

Han’s theorem does allow polylogarithmically many GFOM iterations, so choosing \(\eta_n^{-1}\) polylogarithmic is potentially useful. But it does not itself close the continuous-time limit.

The desired operations \(BH\) and \(q(U)\odot(B^\top D)\) can be encoded at fixed depth by vector-channel alternating GFOM updates. The obstruction is instead the conclusion: Han proves quantitative entrywise universality/comparison, not uniform Gaussian state evolution, \(W_2\) empirical path convergence, or a closed continuum response system for a growing number of iterations. Standard Gaussian AMP identification remains fixed-depth. Empirical-overlap feedback and polynomial-growth maps also require additional derivative and truncation arguments.

The later long-time paper, arXiv:2509.11426, is specific to generalized single-index gradient descent in a large-aspect-ratio regime and a special two-scalar recursion; it is not a general two-population asymmetric GFOM theorem.

CCM’s \(\eta_n\to0\) result is likewise restricted to its stated flow (12). Its mesh-removal and DMFT closure are not formulated for arbitrary vector-channel updates.

Thus Han supplies a promising quantitative route—polylogarithmic mesh depth—but the crucial uniform Gaussian state-evolution/closure theorem is still missing.

Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
Sequential localization does not, by itself, remove the bad-particle amplification in the coupled system.

Let
\[
A(\omega)=\int_0^T |P_t(\omega)|\,dt.
\]
From the available \(L^2\) estimate,
\[
\mathbb E A^2
\le
T\int_0^T\|P_t\|_2^2\,dt,
\]
so
\[
\mathbb P(A>M)\le C M^{-2}.
\]
On the good set \(G_M=\{A\le M\}\), pathwise response estimates cost at most \(e^{CM}\).

The coercive estimate does help with bad-state values. It gives uniform integrability of
\[
\sup_{t\le T}|U_t|^2,
\]
and boundedness of \(\arctan\) implies
\[
\|1_{G_M^c}\,\delta H\|_2\longrightarrow0.
\]
Since the Gaussian-background operator is bounded on \(L^2\),
\[
\|\Gamma(1_{G_M^c}\delta H)\|_2=o_M(1).
\]
The low-rank trained part has a similar estimate.

But this small source generated by bad particles acts on every good particle. A comparison inequality has the schematic form
\[
D_M(T)
\le
e^{CM}\left(
\varepsilon_n+o_M(1)+\int_0^T D_M(s)\,ds
\right).
\]
Taking \(n\to\infty\) first removes \(\varepsilon_n\), but leaves
\[
e^{CM}o_M(1).
\]
The fact that \(o_M(1)\to0\) polynomially or by uniform integrability does not imply that this product vanishes. Thus the proposed order of limits does not eliminate the amplification.

Discarding bad target particles is enough for convergence in probability when the forcing is externally prescribed. It is not enough here, because bad source particles influence good targets through \(\Gamma,\Gamma^*\), and the trained rank-one operator. One must first show that this global influence remains negligible after propagation through the good-particle response. That is exactly the missing estimate.

The singular-Gram issue is not removed either. On \(G_M\), the entire vector \(A=e\,q(U)\odot P\), including its \(\ker R\)-component, is bounded in time-integral and hence locally manageable. On \(G_M^c\), however, the uncontrolled null component enters the discarded global source. Although it is invisible to the base velocity \(RA\), coordinatewise differentiation can rotate it into \(\operatorname{Ran}R\). Consequently it contributes to the same amplified \(o_M(1)\) term.

There is also an independent obstruction to the requested \(W_2\) and kernel convergence. A uniform \(L^2\) bound for \(P\) does not imply uniform integrability of \(P^2\). Yet
\[
K_1
=
\mathbb E\!\left[q(U)^2P^2\right]
\]
contains precisely those quadratic tails. The coercive estimate controls \(U^2\), not \(P^2\). Bounded \(C,D,H\) does not solve this for the adaptive Gaussian part
\[
P=\Gamma^*D+Q^*D:
\]
the \(Q^*D\) term is bounded by the low-rank structure, but \(\Gamma^*D\) is not conditionally Gaussian because \(D\) depends on the same \(\Gamma\). Operator-norm control supplies only an \(L^2\) bound, not higher-moment uniform integrability.

Therefore sequential localization would close the proof only with an additional estimate of one of the following types:

\[
\sup_n\mathbb E\exp\!\left(c\int_0^T|P_{n,t}|\,dt\right)<\infty,
\]
together with uniform integrability of \(P_n^2\); or a one-sided/renormalized stability inequality whose constant grows at most polynomially in \(M\). Neither follows from loss dissipation, bounded phases, or the current coercive energy.

So the answer is:

- **(a) Uniqueness and identification:** not established; bad sources still influence and are exponentially amplified on good particles.
- **(b) \(W_2\) and kernel convergence:** not established; additionally, the required uniform integrability of \(P^2\) is missing.

Sequential localization is useful, but it does not complete the affirmative argument without a new adaptive Gaussian tail or monotone-stability theorem.

The perturbative route around
\[
\phi_\alpha(x)=x+\alpha\sin x
\]
is not ruled out by a zero-radius phenomenon, but it is not yet rigorous.

At \(\alpha=0\),
\[
\phi_0'(x)=1,
\]
so the problematic multiplier
\[
(\phi_\alpha'(U)-\phi_\alpha'(\widetilde U))P
\]
vanishes identically. The deep-linear vector field can therefore be controlled using \(L^2\) and operator norms. For small \(\alpha\), the defect is
\[
\alpha(\cos U-\cos\widetilde U)P,
\]
making a perturbative treatment plausible.

Formal coefficient growth is also compatible with positive radius. Derivatives of sine at real arguments are uniformly bounded. Products of \(k\) Gaussian-like variations have \(L^2\) growth roughly \(C^k k^{k/2}\), while analytic-composition and time-ordered Picard terms carry factorial denominators. These dominate the Gaussian moment growth. Simple models such as
\[
G\cos(\alpha G),\qquad e^{tG}
\]
have positive or infinite \(L^2\)-analytic radius. Zero radius would arise from expressions such as \(G\cos(\alpha G^2)\), but I found no argument forcing that same-source quadratic structure in the first \(\alpha\)-variation of the deep-linear orbit.

The unresolved step is an all-chaos estimate. Although the deep-linear middle matrix remains its initial Gaussian operator plus finite-rank updates, its continuous-time trajectory generally contains infinitely many Gaussian chaoses. Differentiation in \(\alpha\) introduces ordinary products, and multiplication is unbounded on \(L^2\). One needs a weighted Fock/chaos scale satisfying dimension-free estimates for:

- \(M_0\) and \(M_0^*\) between neighboring chaos radii;
- ordinary products;
- composition by sine and cosine;
- finite-rank contractions;
- time integration with summable radius loss.

A sufficient theorem would bound the Taylor coefficients by
\[
\|X_k(t)\|_{\rho'}\le A B^k,\qquad 0\le t\le T,
\]
uniformly in width, for some \(\rho'<\rho\) and \(T>0\). No estimate currently supplied proves this.

Therefore this construction remains promising and is not defeated by coefficient combinatorics or an evident lognormal obstruction. But a width-uniform weighted-all-chaos theorem for the deep-linear base orbit and its complete \(\alpha\)-variational hierarchy is still essential before any fixed nonzero \(\alpha\) affirmative result can be claimed.

Task name: /root

No rigorous spin-glass/quenched-ODE theorem located covers the required system.

The strongest nearby path theorem, Dembo–Lubetzky–Zeitouni’s Theorem 1.1, treats
\[
dX_i=dB_i-U_1'(X_i)\,dt+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_jJ_{ij}X_j\,dt
\]
with fully independent asymmetric, fixed disorder. It gives almost-sure weak empirical path-law convergence on fixed time intervals. The first fatal mismatch is reciprocal reuse: the block embedding
\[
\begin{pmatrix}0&B^\top\\B&0\end{pmatrix}
\]
has mirrored dependencies and a bipartite variance profile, violating row/entry independence used essentially in the large-deviation proof.

Symmetric spherical-spin theorems allow reciprocal disorder but require a fixed Gaussian Hamiltonian with isotropic overlap covariance and conclude primarily correlation/response convergence. They do not cover a bipartite vector-channel drift such as \(q(U)\odot B^\top D\), full coordinate path laws, or an endogenously trained finite-rank perturbation.

The learned finite-rank history may be analytically manageable after decomposing \(B(t)=B_0+\) history terms, but adding it changes the response closure and rate-function uniqueness; no cited theorem includes this extension.

Finally, Han’s polylogarithmic iteration result is unusable here: \(\lambda_1\asymp n\) and \(\eta_{0,n}\lambda_1\to0\) force \(\eta_{0,n}=o(n^{-1})\), hence \(T/\eta_{0,n}\gg n\), far beyond the polylogarithmic window.

A new reciprocal, multi-population continuous-time DMFT theorem with adaptive low-rank feedback and \(W_2\)/energy control is still required.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 26373 | 2026-08-27T11:54:01.012Z | /root/uniform_lift_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/uniform_lift_audit
Payload:
No activation-independent obstruction arises from the proposed dataset symmetries. In particular, \(\phi=\sin+\cos\) defeats all finite distinct-dataset loss-stall constructions.

If \((X,Y)\) is centered jointly Gaussian with equal variance \(v\) and covariance \(c\), then
\[
\mathbb E[(\sin X+\cos X)(\sin Y+\cos Y)]
=e^{-v}\bigl(\sinh c+\cosh c\bigr)
=e^{-v+c}.
\]
For normalized inputs, \(c=v\langle x,y\rangle\), so the first random-feature kernel is
\[
k(x,y)=e^{-v(1-\langle x,y\rangle)}
      =e^{-\frac v2\|x-y\|^2}.
\]
This Gaussian RBF kernel is strictly positive definite on every finite collection of pairwise-distinct inputs. Applying the same activation at the next Gaussian layer again maps equal-variance covariance \(c_2\) to \(e^{-v_2+c_2}\); since the preceding feature embedding separates distinct inputs, the resulting output-feature Gram matrix remains strictly positive definite.

Hence, at zero limiting initial output and for every nonzero label vector \(y\in\{\pm1\}^m\),
\[
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y>0,
\]
so the exact gradient-flow identity
\[
\frac{dL}{dt}=-4e^\top K e
\]
gives strict initial loss decrease from the output block alone.

Consequently:

- Antipodal inputs create no universal identity because \(\sin+\cos\) contains both parities.
- A linear relation among inputs constrains \(W x_a\), but not arbitrary nonlinear ridge evaluations \(\phi(Wx_a)\).
- A Gram null vector need not remain a null vector of the nonlinear feature or tangent kernel.
- Equal preactivations for every first-layer weight would force equal inputs.
- Bias-free architecture does not imply homogeneity when \(\phi(0)\ne0\).

Thus no universal negative theorem can be based solely on antipodes, label symmetry, linear dependence, Gram nullspaces, or absence of biases. The unresolved obstruction remains analytic—compact-time closure, uniform integrability, and uniqueness—not an exact finite-dataset invariant.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 26380 | 2026-08-27T11:54:13.488Z | /root/functional_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
I found no admissible nonlinear activation giving a finite exact closure for general correlated datasets. The standard closure mechanisms can be ruled out rigorously.

First, derivative closure is insufficient. For example,

- \(\sin,\cos\) are closed under differentiation, but products generated by forward/backward propagation produce all Fourier harmonics;
- \(\tanh' =1-\tanh^2\), but repeated products generate every power of \(\tanh\);
- quadratic activation produces polynomials of strictly increasing degree under successive differentiation and feedback;
- arctangent produces an infinite rational-function hierarchy.

The general obstruction is elementary. Let \(\mathcal A\subset C(\mathbb R)\) be a finite-dimensional space containing \(1\) and closed under pointwise multiplication. For any \(g\in\mathcal A\), the powers
\[
1,g,g^2,\ldots
\]
are linearly dependent, so \(p(g)=0\) for a nonzero polynomial \(p\). Because \(g(\mathbb R)\) is connected and contained in the finite root set of \(p\), \(g\) must be constant. Therefore every finite-dimensional pointwise function algebra on the connected state space is trivial. Any exact closure stable under the products generated by nonlinear forward and backward propagation cannot contain a nonconstant activation.

Second, the one-sample natural-coordinate construction cannot be extended to a general correlated dataset. Suppressing scalar residual factors, the first-layer sample vector satisfies
\[
\dot U=R\,\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(U))P.
\]
A coordinate map \(\Psi(U)\) that exactly removes the multiplier would need
\[
D\Psi(U)
=
\bigl(R\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(U))\bigr)^{-1}
=
\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac1{\phi'(U)}\right)R^{-1}.
\]
Write \(g=1/\phi'\). Then
\[
\partial_{U_a}\Psi_k=g(U_k)(R^{-1})_{ka}.
\]
Equality of mixed partial derivatives requires, for \(a\neq k\),
\[
g'(U_k)(R^{-1})_{ka}=0.
\]
If \(\phi'\) is nonconstant, then \(g'\not\equiv0\), so every off-diagonal entry of \(R^{-1}\) must vanish. Thus such a coordinate exists only for a diagonal sample Gram matrix or an affine activation. It cannot handle arbitrary finite datasets.

This also rules out a more general sample-mixing primitive of the obvious type: the matrix-valued one-form
\[
\operatorname{diag}(1/\phi'(U))R^{-1}\,dU
\]
has nonzero curl whenever \(R^{-1}\) has off-diagonal entries and \(\phi'\) varies.

Third, a finite-dimensional Gaussian invariant manifold is unavailable. With quadratic, trigonometric, tanh, or arctangent activation, the parameter velocity is nonlinear in the initial Gaussian marks. The time derivative at \(t=0\) already creates higher Wiener-chaos components; repeated feedback creates unbounded chaos order. A Gaussian family can remain invariant only when the velocity is affine in the Gaussian state. That would reduce the limiting evolution to an affine/deep-linear one, violating the surviving-nonlinearity requirements.

The activation-specific audit is therefore:

- **Quadratic:** exact polynomial calculations are possible at each fixed order, but polynomial degree grows without bound. No finite moment closure.
- **Sin/cos:** bounded phases and finite derivative algebra, but multiplication creates all harmonics and the reused Gaussian matrix creates an infinite response hierarchy.
- **Tanh:** bounded and nondegenerate, but powers of \(\tanh\) form an infinite algebra; no general correlated-sample natural coordinate.
- **Arctan:** the scalar transform works for one sample, but the noncommutation of \(R\) with \(\operatorname{diag}(q(U))\) gives the curl obstruction above.
- **Affine-plus-periodic activations:** globally well-conditioned derivatives may improve estimates, but they still generate an infinite product/response hierarchy and do not give exact finite closure.

All three kernel blocks can be positive for rich mixed-parity activations, and both nonlinear residuals can survive. Those requirements do not help closure; rather, they exclude the constant or affine cases in which finite closure would be possible.

Hence there is a rigorous obstruction to:

1. finite function/moment algebras;
2. a vector natural-coordinate cancellation for arbitrary \(R\);
3. finite-dimensional Gaussian invariant manifolds.

This does not rule out an infinite autonomous law/operator system with a full response hierarchy. It shows that any affirmative construction for arbitrary finite datasets must control such an infinite state; none of the proposed activations collapses it to a finite exact system while retaining genuine two-layer feature learning.

The clipped-localization argument does not currently close.

For bounded \(\phi\) and \(\phi'\), several estimates are valid:

- \(C(t)\) is its Gaussian initialization plus a pointwise bounded shift, so it retains uniform coordinate sub-Gaussian tails.
- \(D_a=C\phi'(S_a)\) is also sub-Gaussian.
- Writing
  \[
  B(t)=B_0+Q(t),\qquad
  \dot Q=\sum_a c_a(t)D_a(t)\otimes H_a(t),
  \]
  gives \(\|Q(t)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le C_T\).
- Moreover,
  \[
  Q(t)^*D_b(t)
  =\int_0^t\sum_a c_a(s)H_a(s)
       \langle D_a(s),D_b(t)\rangle\,ds
  \]
  has controlled pointwise tails.

However, \(Q(t)\) need not have finite rank uniformly in time: an integral of continuously varying rank-\(m\) operators can have arbitrarily large or full rank.

The unresolved term is
\[
B_0^*D_b(t).
\]
At \(t=0\), conditional on the forward variables, this is Gaussian in the independent output initialization and has controlled variance. At positive time, \(D_b(t)\) depends adaptively on \(B_0\) through the entire forward–backward trajectory, so this conditional independence is lost. Boundedness of \(D\) and an operator-norm bound on \(B_0\) do not control coordinate tails; adaptive alignment can create a \(\sqrt n\) spike.

Clipping \(P=B^*D\) at level \(R\) makes the finite-dimensional vector field Lipschitz, but its sensitivity constant generally behaves like
\[
e^{C R T}.
\]
Thus Gaussian concentration for the clipped system has constants depending on \(R\). Such estimates cannot remove clipping, because removal requires an \(R\)-independent square-uniform-integrability bound. Fixed coordinate cutoffs also fail to contain all coordinates since maxima of \(n\) sub-Gaussian variables grow like \(\sqrt{\log n}\).

Likewise, fixed-step Euler analysis is insufficient: the Gaussian-chaos degree and sensitivity constants grow with the number of steps \(T/\eta\).

The missing result is an orbit-specific estimate such as
\[
\sup_{n}\sup_{t\le T}
\|B_0^*D_a(t)\|_{\psi_2}<\infty,
\]
or an equivalent uniform moment hierarchy. A leave-one-column proof of this estimate itself requires stability for the reused-adjoint multiplier, making the present localization argument circular. No actual training-orbit counterexample is established, but bounded activation and the decomposition \(B=B_0+Q\) are insufficient for a complete proof.

The finite-sample theorem cannot be reorganized verbatim into a bound depending only on \(kh\).

For all first-order updates, Theorem 3.2 gives
\[
C_{\rm SE}(k!)^2
\sqrt{\frac{\log(10k^2/\delta)}{n}},
\]
where \(C_{\rm SE}\) depends on the full \(k\)-step state evolution. The paper explicitly identifies the factorial dependence as an open problem.

The proof’s obstruction is structural:

- It conditions on and inverts the full \(k\)-step history Gram matrices.
- Near-identity Euler iterates become increasingly collinear as \(h\to0\), so the smallest Gram eigenvalues are not uniformly bounded; constants contain inverse powers of these eigenvalues.
- Once \(k>\min(n,d)\), the empirical history Gram matrices are singular identically.
- Even pretending all hidden constants were uniform, Stirling’s formula limits the displayed estimate to approximately \(k=O(\log n/\log\log n)\).

Here the required scaling gives \(k_n=T/\eta_{0,n}\gg n\), so the theorem is far outside its valid proof regime.

Fixed-\(k\) encoding of reused \(B,B^\top\) and vector-channel postprocessing is plausible, subject to global Lipschitz assumptions. But a compact-time theorem would require a fundamentally new conditioning scheme—such as compressed increment/operator-subspace control—that avoids inversion of the complete time-history Gram.

I cannot give a complete affirmative or negative resolution. The remaining obstruction is substantive, not merely technical.

For route A, the strongest bounded candidates are trigonometric activations. A complex phase would satisfy
\[
\frac{d}{dz}e^{iz}=ie^{iz},
\]
but the network is real, and real \(\sin/\cos\) requires both phase components. More importantly, multiplication in the exact gradient produces
\[
e^{ikz},\qquad |k|\to\infty.
\]
Thus derivative closure does not imply gradient-flow closure. Before training, Gaussian characteristic-function identities evaluate these modes. After training, the phases depend adaptively on the same middle matrix through both \(B\) and \(B^\top\), so ordinary Gaussian conditioning is unavailable. One obtains an infinite covariance-response hierarchy rather than a finite system.

Other natural choices fail similarly:

- quadratic: unbounded polynomial degree and moment hierarchy;
- tanh: all powers of tanh;
- arctan: scalar natural coordinate, but no integrable vector coordinate for correlated data;
- bounded saturating \(C^2\) functions: the evolving law of particles crossing the transition region is required;
- affine-plus-periodic functions: infinite Fourier hierarchy remains.

The correlated-data coordinate obstruction is exact. For
\[
\dot U=R\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(U))P,
\]
a transform eliminating the multiplier would require
\[
D\Psi=\operatorname{diag}(1/\phi'(U))R^{-1}.
\]
Its mixed partial derivatives agree for arbitrary \(U\) only if \(R^{-1}\) is diagonal or \(\phi'\) is constant. Thus no fixed nonlinear activation admits this exact cancellation for every dataset.

The seven properties themselves do not yield a negative theorem:

1. **Full convergence** is the unresolved adaptive-Gaussian problem.
2. **Autonomous closure** could in principle use an infinite response/operator state; failure of finite closure does not exclude it.
3. **Finite hidden states** are compatible with bounded activations and the known \(W^{(2)}\sim n^{-1/2}\) balance.
4. **Both feature velocities** are compatible with \(\lambda_1\asymp n,\lambda_2\asymp1\).
5. **All blocks active** are compatible with
   \[
   \lambda_1\asymp n,\quad\lambda_2\asymp1,\quad\lambda_3\asymp n^{-1},
   \]
   and trained \(W^{(3)}\sim n^{-1}\).
6. **Surviving nonlinearity** is automatic for a non-affine continuous activation under any full-support nondegenerate Gaussian marginal: zero nonlinear residual would force equality with an affine function everywhere.
7. **Moving kernel and loss** can hold for each fixed dataset even when the relevant eigenvalue is arbitrarily small. No dataset-uniform rate is required.

Near duplicates only produce dataset-dependent constants tending to zero. Arbitrarily large \(m\) introduces finite \(m\)-dependent constants but no new power of \(n\). Rank-deficient input Grams do not force nonlinear feature-kernel rank deficiency. Mixed-parity analytic activations evade antipodal and label-nullspace arguments.

Therefore:

- a finite exact closure is ruled out for the standard algebraic, Fourier, Gaussian-manifold, and coordinate-transform mechanisms;
- an infinite autonomous adaptive-Gaussian closure is not ruled out, but no rigorous uniqueness or compact-time convergence theorem has been established;
- no activation-independent distinct-dataset invariant presently contradicts all seven requirements.

The problem remains unresolved. A complete affirmative result needs a new theorem controlling the infinite reused-\(B,B^\top\) response hierarchy. A complete negative result needs a dynamical invariant stronger than initialization rank, symmetry, or near-collision degeneracy.

The continuous-time Picard route fails at the same reused-multiplier estimate as the Euler route.

If \(\Delta_rU=U^{(r)}-U^{(r-1)}\), then the next Picard increment contains
\[
\bigl(q(U^{(r)})-q(U^{(r-1)})\bigr)P^{(r)}.
\]
Since \(q\) is Lipschitz, an \(L^2\) recurrence requires
\[
\mathbb E_n\!\left[
 |\Delta_rU|^2|P^{(r)}|^2
\right]
\le C_r\,\mathbb E_n|\Delta_rU|^2
\]
with dimension-independent \(C_r\) whose growth is compatible with the time-simplex factorial.

Neither an operator-norm bound nor exchangeability implies this. With a uniformly random coordinate \(I\), set
\[
P_i=\sqrt n\,\mathbf1_{\{i=I\}},
\qquad
\Delta U_i=\mathbf1_{\{i=I\}}.
\]
Then
\[
\|P\|_2=1,\qquad
\|\Delta U\|_2^2=n^{-1},\qquad
\|P\Delta U\|_2^2=1.
\]
Thus the required multiplier constant can grow like \(n\).

Gaussian Wick contractions do not directly fix this. After one Picard substitution, the fields are adaptive nonlinear functions of the reused \(B_0\) and \(B_0^*\), not jointly Gaussian. Expanding sine or cosine gives infinite Gaussian chaos, and repeated matrix reuse introduces Malliavin contraction terms. A finite-chaos hypercontractive estimate therefore does not apply.

For each fixed Picard depth \(r\), the finitely many matrix contractions and coordinate nonlinearities can plausibly be encoded as a finite tensor program; deterministic time integrals can be bundled into scalar nonlinearities or approximated by fixed quadrature. This yields only
\[
n\to\infty\quad\text{with }r\text{ fixed}.
\]
Compact-time convergence additionally needs
\[
\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_n
\|X_n-X_n^{(r)}\|=0,
\]
which is exactly what the failed multiplier estimate must establish.

Hence expected normalized \(L^2\) bounds and \(\|B_0\|_{\mathrm{op}}\) do not give a uniform Picard series. The missing ingredient is a width-uniform all-moment or Malliavin/source-norm estimate for \(P^{(r)}\) and its correlation with the Picard increments. Without it, fixed-depth tensor-program convergence cannot be lifted to continuous time.

No applicable theorem was found.

The rigorous literature separates into two nonoverlapping classes:

- Nonlinear gated random-neural-network LDPs, such as Cabana–Touboul, allow bounded Lipschitz state-dependent interactions but require directed independent Gaussian weights.
- Reciprocal/symmetric spin-glass dynamics allow reused symmetric disorder but only for special bilinear or Gaussian-Hamiltonian structures.

Faugeras–MacLaurin permits correlated Gaussian weights, but only in a fixed finite discrete-time model with stationary, absolutely summable spatial covariance. Exact transpose equality is not verified within that covariance class, and the theorem supplies neither a continuous-time limit nor path-energy control.

The trained edge drift can be eliminated algebraically:
\[
B(t)H(t)=B_0H(t)+\int_0^t a_s\langle b_s,H(t)\rangle\,ds,
\]
with an analogous formula for \(B(t)^\top D(t)\). Thus the learned part becomes finite-rank path-dependent overlap feedback and would likely be manageable after establishing the random reciprocal dynamics. But no existing theorem covers that reciprocal nonlinear core.

Accordingly, the needed result lies in an unproved intersection: multi-population reciprocal Gaussian disorder, nonlinear vector channels, and law-dependent path feedback. A new theorem is required.

For \(\alpha=0\), the model is exactly a depth-three linear factorization, but even this base point does not generally reduce to finitely many overlaps.

Let
\[
A=W^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times d},\qquad
B=W^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},\qquad
c=W^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n,
\]
and
\[
\theta=A^\top B^\top c,\qquad f=X\theta,\qquad
g=\frac2mX^\top(X\theta-y).
\]
The exact gradient-flow equations are
\[
\dot A=-\lambda_1(B^\top c)g^\top,
\]
\[
\dot B=-\lambda_2c(Ag)^\top,
\]
\[
\dot c=-\lambda_3BAg.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\dot\theta
=
-\left[
\lambda_1\|B^\top c\|^2I_d
+\lambda_2\|c\|^2A^\top A
+\lambda_3A^\top B^\top BA
\right]g.
\]
This is the exact deep-linear output equation.

There are exact balancedness invariants:
\[
\frac1{\lambda_2}B^\top B-\frac1{\lambda_1}AA^\top
=
\text{constant},
\]
and
\[
\frac1{\lambda_2}BB^\top-\frac1{\lambda_3}cc^\top
=
\text{constant}.
\]
However, their constants retain the full iid Gaussian random-matrix background. They do not collapse it to scalar overlaps.

For example, differentiating
\[
b_1=c^\top BB^\top c=\|B^\top c\|^2
\]
introduces terms involving
\[
b_2=c^\top(BB^\top)^2c.
\]
Differentiating \(b_2\) introduces \(b_3\), and so on. Equivalently, the dynamics explores the Krylov family
\[
c,\ BB^\top c,\ (BB^\top)^2c,\ldots.
\]
Thus the exact \(\alpha=0\) closure is a spectral-measure or Volterra closure, not a finite-dimensional overlap system, unless one imposes special balanced initialization not present in the problem.

For
\[
\phi_\alpha(x)=x+\alpha\sin x,
\]
each fixed-\(n\) ODE is locally analytic in \(\alpha\). Formally, the Gaussian-chaos expansion is favorable:

- multiplication by one initial Gaussian raises or lowers chaos order with coefficients of size \(O(\sqrt{k})\);
- the sine expansion supplies factorially decaying coefficients;
- time-ordered ODE expansions supply an additional \(1/r!\).

This suggests a shrinking-radius chaos norm of the form
\[
\|F\|_\rho
=
\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\rho^k}{\sqrt{k!}}\|\Pi_kF\|_2,
\]
or a nearby factorially weighted norm. Multiplication by the Gaussian background should map radius \(\rho\) to a slightly smaller radius \(\rho'<\rho\), as in a Cauchy–Kowalevski argument.

The unresolved estimate is uniformity in both chaos order and width. Reusing \(B_0\) and \(B_0^\top\) creates contractions between all prior forward and backward occurrences of the same Gaussian entries. At total order \(k\), the number of possible pairings grows factorially. Factorial decay from sine and time ordering may compensate this for small \(|\alpha|\) and short \(T\), but that requires an explicit diagram bound of the form
\[
\sup_n\|\Pi_k\mathcal T_{n,r}\|_2
\le
C^{k+r}k!^{\,\beta}r!^{\,\gamma}
\]
with exponents strictly dominated by the chosen analytic weights. No such bound has yet been established. Fixed-order Gaussian-chaos or tensor-program convergence is insufficient because its constants may grow too rapidly to sum over all orders.

There is also a zeroth-order degeneracy relevant to the seven properties. For some datasets,
\[
X^\top y=0.
\]
Then the \(\alpha=0\) deep-linear flow starts exactly stationary at zero output. Feature learning for \(\alpha\neq0\) must arise entirely from higher perturbative orders. A fixed distinct dataset can annihilate several low orders, although a sufficiently rich analytic activation should eventually produce a nonzero direction. Since the required lower bounds are datasetwise rather than uniform, this is not a contradiction, but it prevents proving activity merely by continuity from \(\alpha=0\).

Hence the perturbative route remains plausible for \(\phi=x+\alpha\sin x\), especially for small fixed \(\alpha\) and short time, but it is not yet a theorem. It requires:

1. a rigorous continuous-time spectral/Volterra limit already at \(\alpha=0\);
2. width-uniform factorial diagram estimates for adaptive two-sided reuse;
3. resummation in a shrinking analytic radius;
4. dataset-specific proof that some nonlinear order activates both hidden layers and moves the kernel.

The hard obstruction is not finite-\(n\) analyticity in \(\alpha\); it is uniform all-orders control of the reused Gaussian contractions.

Traffic probability handles every fixed finite computation diagram, including matrix multiplication, diagonal extraction, and coordinatewise products. Countability can place all fixed rational diagrams on one full-sequence event. But this is moment/test-graph convergence, not operator-norm convergence of the resulting adaptive actions.

Ordinary strong random-matrix convergence gives operator-norm convergence for every fixed noncommutative polynomial, including rectangular Gaussian block embeddings. It does not include diagonal matrices or Hadamard operations adaptively generated from the same Gaussian matrix.

The recent vector-traffic GFOM manuscript makes the limitation explicit: its diagram size and algorithmic time are fixed independently of \(n\). Action convergence similarly gives compactness from finitely many bounded probes, not a canonical full-sequence operator calculus for adaptive nonlinear ODEs.

Therefore no cited theorem covers growing polynomial degree, positive-time polynomial ODE functional calculus, or path energies. A new strong-traffic/action stability theorem—controlling dependent diagonal operations in a common normed algebra—would be necessary.
