Payload:
No universal obstruction arises from antipodes, rank, labels, or time-zero kernel degeneracy. The activation
\[
\phi(x)=\sin x+\cos x
\]
defeats all these constructions.

For equal-variance centered Gaussian \(X,Y\) with covariance \(c\),
\[
\mathbb E[\phi(X)\phi(Y)]
=e^{-v+c}.
\]
Its derivative \(q=\cos-\sin\) satisfies the identical formula
\[
\mathbb E[q(X)q(Y)]=e^{-v+c}.
\]
Hence both activation and derivative covariance kernels are Gaussian RBF kernels on the corresponding feature embeddings. They are strictly positive definite on every finite set of distinct inputs. Repeated Gaussian layers preserve distinctness of those embeddings and therefore preserve strict positive definiteness.

The initialization kernels of the parameter blocks are Schur products of activation and derivative covariance matrices. The first-layer kernel additionally contains the input Gram matrix. If \(A\) is strictly positive definite and \(B\) is positive semidefinite with positive diagonal, then \(A\circ B\) is strictly positive definite: writing
\[
B=\sum_r v_rv_r^*
\]
gives
\[
x^*(A\circ B)x
=\sum_r
(\operatorname{diag}(v_r)x)^*
A(\operatorname{diag}(v_r)x),
\]
and positive diagonal ensures that some summand is nonzero for every \(x\ne0\). Thus even when \(m>d\), an input-Gram nullspace does not produce a first-layer tangent-kernel nullspace.

Consequently, under any scaling making the blocks finite and active, every nonzero residual has positive quadratic form in every block at initialization. This covers antipodal inputs, arbitrary linear dependence, and every \(\{\pm1\}\)-label vector. Near duplicates can make eigenvalues arbitrarily small but not zero; dataset-dependent times and constants are permitted.

A symmetry may make a particular derivative of the kernel vanish at one time, but that does not force the kernel to remain constant on an interval. I found no identity valid for mixed-parity ridge features that would impose such constancy while both hidden layers move.

Therefore no simple universal negative theorem follows from the finite dataset or scaling. Any genuine impossibility result must come from failure of the reciprocal compact-time mean-field limit itself.

No existing primary-source theorem located through August 2026 directly covers the required deterministic reciprocal rectangular Gaussian ODE.

There is, however, a legitimate way to avoid any random-matrix theorem at growing iteration depth. Use the comparison
\[
\text{fine GD}_{\eta_n}
\longrightarrow
\text{finite-}n\text{ ODE}
\longrightarrow
\text{finite-}n\text{ coarse Euler}_{h}
\longrightarrow
\text{fixed-}h\text{ mean-field Euler}
\longrightarrow
\text{limiting ODE}.
\]
If the first two errors are bounded uniformly in width by \(C_T\eta_n\) and \(C_Th\), then the random-matrix theorem is invoked only at fixed \(h\), hence at the fixed depth \(T/h\). This remains valid even though admissibility forces \(T/\eta_n\gg n\).

Reeves does not itself provide the bridge: its theorem is for GOE matrices, while the dilation
\[
\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\B^\top&0\end{pmatrix}
\]
has a bipartite variance profile and is not GOE. A transpose-aware rectangular fixed-depth Tensor Program theorem is the more natural identification tool.

The essential unresolved lemma is width-uniform mesh removal. Ordinary \(L^2\) Lipschitz theory is insufficient because coordinate products do not map \(L^2\times L^2\) to \(L^2\), Gaussian maxima grow with \(n\), and trained rank-one matrix histories must be controlled. For bounded activations and derivatives, a finite empirical \(L^{2p}\) energy hierarchy may close; for quadratic activation, degree escalation makes this substantially harder.

Thus fixed-mesh then continuous time is a valid architecture, but only after proving new uniform moment, stability, Euler-error, and velocity-integrability estimates. No audited external theorem supplies those estimates automatically.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 29567 | 2026-08-27T13:27:17.312Z | /root/functional_audit ===

Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
I cannot close the full affirmative theorem by fixed-mesh tensor programs plus ordinary path-law compactness. The obstruction is identification of the reused Gaussian action as the mesh tends to zero.

For a bounded mixed-parity activation, it is useful to work with a bounded first-hidden coordinate \(H=\phi(U)\), or \(T=\tanh U\) for
\[
\phi(u)=c+\tanh u+\varepsilon\tanh^2u.
\]
After eliminating the trained middle matrix,
\[
B_t
=
B_0-\frac2m\int_0^t\sum_a e_a(s)D_s^a\otimes_nH_s^a\,ds,
\]
so
\[
S_t^a
=
B_0H_t^a
-\frac2m\int_0^t\sum_b e_b(s)D_s^b
\langle H_s^b,H_t^a\rangle_n\,ds,
\]
\[
P_t^a
=
B_0^\top D_t^a
-\frac2m\int_0^t\sum_b e_b(s)H_s^b
\langle D_s^b,D_t^a\rangle_n\,ds.
\]
All terms except \(B_0H\) and \(B_0^\top D\) pass to a limit under path-\(W_2\) convergence and sufficient uniform integrability.

For every fixed mesh with a fixed number \(K\) of alternating queries, Gaussian conditioning gives an exact finite state evolution:

- condition on the spans of previous \(H\)- and \(D\)-queries;
- decompose each new \(B_0H_k\) or \(B_0^\top D_k\) into its conditional projection plus an orthogonal Gaussian innovation;
- identify empirical covariances and projection coefficients by finite-dimensional laws of large numbers.

Because \(K\) is fixed, every conditioning Gram matrix has fixed size and all response variables form a finite recursion. Thus fixed-mesh convergence is genuinely tractable.

The candidate continuous law is a two-sort Volterra/cavity system. Its forward and backward sources consist of Gaussian processes with covariances
\[
\mathbb E[Z_H^a(t)Z_H^b(s)]
=
\mathbb E[H_t^aH_s^b],
\]
\[
\mathbb E[Z_D^a(t)Z_D^b(s)]
=
\mathbb E[D_t^aD_s^b],
\]
plus causal memory terms determined by the forward and backward response kernels.

Once those covariance and response kernels are prescribed and bounded, the effective path equations are locally well posed. The only nonuniform scalar term has the form
\[
\dot H=a(H)P
\]
with bounded Lipschitz \(a\). Comparing two solutions gives
\[
\sup_{s\le t}|\Delta H_s|
\le
C\exp\left(C\int_0^t|P_s|\,ds\right)
\int_0^t|\Delta P_s|\,ds.
\]
A continuous Gaussian \(P\) with bounded covariance, plus bounded memory, has finite moments of this random Gronwall factor on sufficiently short intervals. Hence prescribed-kernel pathwise uniqueness and short-time stability are provable.

This does not identify the finite-width limit. When \(K\to\infty\), the conditioning vectors are increments of a smooth path and become nearly linearly dependent. Their raw covariance Gram matrices have vanishing smallest eigenvalues. Rewriting the unstable projections as Onsager memory terms requires convergence of empirical response traces uniformly in \(K\). Fixed-\(K\) Gaussian conditioning gives no such estimate.

Ordinary empirical compactness cannot bypass this. The marginal law of \(H_n\) does not determine \(B_0H_n\) when \(H_n\) depends on \(B_0\). For example, an independent Gaussian vector and an adaptively chosen vector of the form \(B_0^\top v\) can have the same limiting coordinate marginal, while their images under \(B_0\) have different joint laws and responses. Thus a subsequential path-law limit loses exactly the alignment information required by the reused matrix.

Some of the seven properties can be verified conditionally on the missing convergence theorem. With \(C(0)=0\),
\[
C(t)=tV+O(t^2),
\qquad
V=\frac2m\sum_a y_a\,\phi(S_0^a).
\]
For the mixed-parity activation above, the output feature Gram is strictly positive definite for every finite pairwise-distinct sphere dataset. Hence \(V\neq0\) for every nonzero label vector. It follows that

- the initial loss derivative is strictly negative through the output block;
- \(D(t)=tV\phi'(S_0)+O(t^2)\);
- the middle-layer kernel block has a strictly positive \(t^2\) coefficient;
- both hidden velocities become nonzero at positive infinitesimal time under the nondegeneracy of the derivative gates.

These calculations do not establish convergence or exclude an exact cancellation in the total kernel trajectory for every dataset.

Therefore the actually closable result is:

> Fixed-query Gaussian state evolution and prescribed-kernel short-time Volterra well-posedness hold for bounded smooth mixed-parity gates.

The missing theorem is:

> Uniform convergence of empirical covariance and response/Onsager coefficients as the number of alternating \(B_0,B_0^\top\) queries tends to infinity with the mesh.

Without that theorem, fixed-mesh tensor programs plus compactness do not prove the continuous exact flow, because the compact topology does not retain the adaptive Gaussian action.

ArXiv:2603.09310 does not provide the missing theorem.

Its main result is a finite-dimensional distributional identity between an auxiliary Gaussian system and a perturbed original system, for \(\sigma>0\), real \(z\), and a fixed number of stages. Its finite-dimensional removal of the perturbation uses an explicitly unproved analytic-continuation claim.

The asymptotic DMFT theorem also fixes the number of stages and assumes “existence and concentration of DMFT,” including concentration of the relevant Grams, overlaps, and Gaussian contractions. Those are central proof obligations here, so invoking the theorem would be circular. Its proof supplies only a sketch of the recursive estimates and concludes convergence of expectations for bounded Lipschitz functions of a finite block state—not empirical path-\(W_2\), moments, velocities, kernels, or a joint continuous-time limit.

The search also found no rigorous zero-Brownian recurrent-ODE theorem covering the target. Classical neural-network and spin-glass path LDPs rely on nondegenerate Gaussian innovations or Langevin diffusion and corresponding Gaussian-reference change-of-measure arguments. Formal deterministic RNN DMFT papers do not prove finite-width empirical path convergence.

Even a theorem for
\[
\dot x=-x+J\phi(x)
\]
with independent directed \(J\) would not directly apply: the target has reciprocal \(B/B^\top\) correlations, a bipartite variance profile, two adaptively gated populations, endogenous output aggregates, and learned rank-one feedback.

Thus Panahi is a fixed-stage, assumption-driven comparison result, not a continuous-time bridge. The most plausible architecture remains fixed-mesh transpose-aware Tensor Programs followed by a new width-uniform deterministic Euler/energy argument.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 29703 | 2026-08-27T13:31:13.729Z | /root/uniform_lift_audit ===

The rigorous random-RNN/spin-glass large-deviation theorem does not apply to this network.

I checked the primary symmetric result of [Ben Arous–Guionnet](https://cims.nyu.edu/~benarous/Publications/benarous_31.pdf). It studies
\[
dx_j=dB_j-U'(x_j)\,dt
+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt N}\sum_iJ_{ji}x_i\,dt,
\]
with:

- fixed symmetric Gaussian couplings \(J\);
- nondegenerate Brownian noise;
- bounded spins;
- product initial law;
- a short-time/high-temperature condition \(2\beta^2A^2T<1\).

Its path-space density and rate function rely explicitly on Girsanov absolute continuity relative to uncoupled Wiener dynamics.

The reciprocal pair \((B_0,B_0^*)\) can be represented by the symmetric bipartite block
\[
\begin{pmatrix}
0&B_0^*\\
B_0&0
\end{pmatrix},
\]
so reciprocity itself is compatible with symmetric spin-glass structure. The exact mismatches are:

1. The neural dynamics is deterministic. Coupled deterministic path laws are generally singular relative to uncoupled path laws, so the Girsanov/entropy argument disappears.

2. The underlying trainable coordinates are unbounded, even if \(\phi\) is bounded.

3. The coupling matrix is trained. Eliminating it produces correlation-dependent Volterra memory rather than a fixed pair interaction.

Adding Brownian noise of size \(\varepsilon\) does not immediately repair this: the Girsanov exponent scales as \(\varepsilon^{-2}\), and the short-time estimates are not uniform as \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\).

Direct Gaussian integration over \(B_0\) has a related obstruction. For externally fixed \(H,D\), the fields \((B_0H,B_0^*D)\) are Gaussian with covariances determined by \(Q_H,Q_D\). But \(H,D\) depend on \(B_0\). Imposing the trajectory constraints introduces an Onsager/Jacobian determinant. In deterministic dynamics the map \(B_0\mapsto\) trajectories is singular and noninjective, so there is no immediate Sanov tilt or path-space density.

A valid theorem would need a new two-species deterministic path LDP with symmetric Gaussian disorder, random initial data, endogenous Volterra drift, response-determinant control, quenched concentration, and uniqueness of the minimizer. None of these follows from the inspected Langevin theorem.

Thus the spin-glass analogy is structurally useful, but no existing theorem checked supplies the required zero-noise trained-\(B\) limit.

Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER

Payload:
The identity
\[
\phi^2+(\phi')^2=2
\]
does not cancel the bad \(P\)-multiplier. It cancels only the common rotational part of the phase dynamics.

Set
\[
h(U)=\sin U+\cos U,\qquad
q(U)=\cos U-\sin U,
\]
and
\[
v(U)=\binom{h(U)}{q(U)},\qquad
A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Then
\[
|v|^2=2,\qquad
\frac d{dU}v=Av.
\]
For the normalized multi-sample flow,
\[
\dot U^a
=
-\frac2m\sum_bR_{ab}e_bq(U^b)P^b.
\]
Therefore
\[
\dot v^a=\dot U^aAv^a.
\]

Linearizing gives
\[
\delta\dot v^a
=
\dot U^aA\delta v^a
+
\delta\dot U^aAv^a.
\]
Because \(A\) is skew,
\[
\langle\delta v^a,\dot U^aA\delta v^a\rangle=0.
\]
This is the useful circle cancellation.

However,
\[
\delta\dot U^a
=
\frac2m\sum_bR_{ab}e_bh(U^b)P^b\,\delta U^b
-\frac2m\sum_bR_{ab}
\left[
e_bq(U^b)\delta P^b
+\delta e_bq(U^b)P^b
\right].
\]
The first term contains
\[
R\,\operatorname{diag}(e\,h(U)\,P)\,\delta U.
\]
It has no sign and remains in the phase-energy derivative through
\[
2\langle\delta v,\delta\dot U\,Av\rangle.
\]
Thus the circle identity removes \(\dot U A\delta v\), but not the variation of the angular velocity.

There is an explicit full-network counterterm. Consider one sample and a state with
\[
q(U)=0,\qquad h(U)=\pm\sqrt2.
\]
Then the base first-layer velocity is zero:
\[
\dot U=-2e\,q(U)P=0.
\]
Perturb only \(U\). Since
\[
\delta h=q(U)\delta U=0,
\]
the first hidden feature does not vary to first order. Consequently,
\[
\delta S=\delta D=\delta P=\delta e=\delta B=\delta C=0
\]
at the linearized level. Hence \(\delta P=0\) is consistent with the full coupled network, not an externally frozen approximation.

Nevertheless,
\[
\delta\dot U
=
2e\,h(U)P\,\delta U.
\]
Whenever \(e\,h(U)P>0\), this direction expands exponentially:
\[
\delta U(t)=
\delta U(0)e^{2e hP t}
\]
while the base state remains at the activation extremum.

Equivalently,
\[
|\delta v|^2=2|\delta U|^2
\]
at that phase, and
\[
\frac d{dt}|\delta v|^2
=
8e\,h(U)P\,|\delta U|^2.
\]
Thus even the circle-state response has a genuine indefinite expanding direction.

At finite width, the corresponding empirical derivative contains
\[
\operatorname{diag}(h_iP_i).
\]
Since \(|h_i|\le\sqrt2\),
\[
\|\operatorname{diag}(hP)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
=
\max_i|h_iP_i|.
\]
For Gaussian-scale \(P_i\), this is generically \(O(\sqrt{\log n})\), so the identity does not give dimension-uniform deterministic ODE or Euler stability.

The same term prevents fixed-moment closure. A \(p\)-response energy contains
\[
\mathbb E\langle |P||\delta v|^p\rangle_n,
\]
which again requires higher moments or an exponential random Gronwall estimate. The skew cancellation does not alter that term.

The identities are still useful for:

- uniform boundedness of the forward gates;
- exact preservation of the phase-circle constraint;
- bounding local Taylor/Euler defects in terms of moments of \(P\).

They do not provide the missing stability theorem. The explicit surviving counterterm is
\[
R\,\operatorname{diag}(e\,\phi(U)P),
\]
arising from \(\phi''=-\phi\).

The proposed rates are viable only under a projection-level proof that completely avoids recursive basis perturbation.

Writing \(L=\log n\),
\[
K=e^{D\sqrt L},\qquad \tau^{-c}=e^{cE\sqrt L},\qquad e^{CRT}=e^{CAT\sqrt L}
\]
are all \(n^{o(1)}\). Hence \(n^{-1/2}\) concentration can dominate any fixed product of these factors. Discarded residuals accumulate as
\[
K\tau e^{CRT}
 =e^{(D-E+CAT)\sqrt L},
\]
so one needs \(E>D+CAT\), with the appropriate stability constant.

But
\[
\tau^{-K}
 =\exp\!\big(E\sqrt L\,e^{D\sqrt L}\big)
\]
is superpolynomial and fatal. Ordinary induction comparing successive Gram–Schmidt or SVD bases produces precisely this danger.

The favorable exact identity is that, for orthonormal retained bases \(Q,P\), conditioning on
\[
Y=GQ,\qquad Z=G^\top P
\]
gives
\[
\mathbb E[G\mid Y,Z]
 =YQ^\top+PZ^\top-P(P^\top Y)Q^\top,
\]
with independent Gaussian residual \(P^\perp G'Q^\perp\). This is inverse-free and handles exact singularity.

The unresolved proof obligations are:

- avoid comparing bases, using only invariant projections and charging residuals below \(\tau\) through \(\|G\|_{\rm op}\);
- handle discontinuity of hard spectral thresholding near \(\tau\);
- verify the alternating two-sided filtration and compatibility \(P^\top Y=Z^\top Q\);
- prove coordinatewise subgaussian or sufficiently high-moment bounds for adaptive conditional means—operator-norm control alone is insufficient;
- show truncation at \(A\sqrt{\log n}\) changes states and velocities by \(o(1)\);
- obtain incremental stability \(e^{CRK\Delta}=e^{CRT}\), not \(e^{CK}\);
- ensure every inverse-threshold factor occurs only to fixed order.

If these are proved, an error of the form
\[
n^{-1/2}e^{c_1\sqrt{\log n}}
+e^{-(E-D-c_2AT)\sqrt{\log n}}
+\text{truncation error}
\]
would suffice. At present, the projection-only induction and coordinate-tail lemma are theorem-scale gaps, with hidden \(\tau^{-K}\) amplification the principal adversarial failure mode.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 30368 | 2026-08-27T13:44:16.038Z | /root/uniform_lift_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/uniform_lift_audit
Payload:
The growing-depth conditioning scheme is not yet rigorous. The central missing result is a logarithmic-depth reciprocal Gaussian-conditioning theorem with tail and tangent control.

On a coordinate cutoff \(|P_i|,|C_i|\le R\), the vector-field Lipschitz constant is at least \(CR\), because
\[
\partial_U(q(U)P)=q'(U)P.
\]
Ordinary Euler–Gronwall estimates therefore give
\[
\text{error}\lesssim \delta e^{CRT}.
\]
Taking \(R\asymp\sqrt{\log n}\), as required for Gaussian maxima, and \(\delta\asymp1/\log n\), this bound diverges. Thus deterministic cutoff stability is insufficient. One needs the averaged product estimate
\[
\prod_k(1+C\delta |P_i^k|),
\]
with uniform empirical moments.

State evolution predicts \(\max_i|P_i|=O(\sqrt{\log n})\), but normalized \(L^2\) convergence cannot establish this because it permits isolated \(\sqrt n\)-spikes. A maximum bootstrap requires conditional sub-Gaussian tails and an \(\ell^\infty\)-small conditioning remainder at every step.

For Gaussian \(B_0\), causal conditioning can in principle proceed by alternating forward observations \(B_0H^k\) and backward observations \(B_0^*D^k\). The unexplored component remains Gaussian on orthogonal complements, while conditional means generate Onsager terms. After \(K\) steps, the observed subspaces have dimension \(O(mK)\). At \(K=O(\log n)\), however, one needs quantitative control of all projection errors. Near-singular Gram matrices require a threshold \(\rho_n\): discarded directions cost \(O(\sqrt{\rho_n})\), while retained inverse coefficients cost \(\rho_n^{-1/2}\). No proven rate currently balances these errors through logarithmic depth without covariance lower bounds.

If uniform sub-Gaussian message and response bounds were available, the limiting Volterra system could plausibly be unique by the Osgood estimate
\[
E'(t)\le CE(t)\log(C/E(t)).
\]
Finite-\(p\) estimates are insufficient, and convergence of response kernels additionally requires tangent state evolution.

A sufficient theorem must establish jointly, for \(K\le c\log n\):

- reciprocal Gaussian innovation and Onsager formulas;
- \(n^{-1/2+o(1)}\) normalized and \(\ell^\infty\) remainder bounds;
- uniform sub-Gaussian message tails;
- uniform tangent-product moments;
- concentration of all correlation and response observables;
- projection estimates valid for singular covariance.

The exact unclosed step is the \(\ell^\infty\)/sub-Gaussian conditioning remainder for adaptive reciprocal queries and their tangent recursions. Without it, both the field-max bootstrap and averaged Euler stability are circular.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 30370 | 2026-08-27T13:44:16.038Z | /root/functional_audit ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/functional_audit
Payload:
The proposed theorem is false without an additional uniform \(O(1)\) state-growth assumption. An \(n^{o(1)}\) response bound is not sufficient to remove a \(\sqrt{\log n}\) field cutoff.

The exact Gaussian conditioning step itself is benign. Let \(V\) and \(U\) have orthonormal columns spanning the previous right and left query spaces, and let
\[
Y=AV,\qquad X=A^\top U.
\]
Conditionally on these observations,
\[
A
=
YV^\top+UX^\top-UU^\top YV^\top
+
P_U^\perp\widetilde A P_V^\perp,
\]
where \(\widetilde A\) is an independent Gaussian matrix with the original variance. The compatibility identity
\[
U^\top Y=X^\top V
\]
verifies both constraints. Thus the fresh orthogonal innovation remains exactly Gaussian without any covariance lower bound.

SVD thresholding is needed only to construct stable bases from nearly dependent raw queries. If a discarded query direction has covariance at most \(\tau\), its innovation size is \(O(\sqrt\tau)\). Over
\[
K\asymp e^{D\sqrt{\log n}}
\]
steps, the unamplified accumulated error is
\[
K\sqrt\tau
=
\exp\left((D-E/2)\sqrt{\log n}\right).
\]
Therefore \(E>2D\) would suffice only if subsequent response amplification were uniformly \(O(1)\). If the allowed response is
\[
e^{CAT\sqrt{\log n}},
\]
the actual condition becomes
\[
E>2(D+CAT).
\]

The decisive counterexample does not require a random matrix. Let
\[
\delta=e^{-D\sqrt{\log n}},\qquad
R=A\sqrt{\log n},
\]
and consider the causal scalar update
\[
x_{k+1}=(1+\delta R)x_k,\qquad x_0\sim N(0,1).
\]
Its per-step Jacobian is exactly \(1+\delta R\), within the proposed cutoff. For \(K=\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor\),
\[
x_K
=
(1+\delta R)^Kx_0
=
\exp\left(AT\sqrt{\log n}+o(1)\right)x_0.
\]
The total response is
\[
e^{AT\sqrt{\log n}}=n^{o(1)},
\]
which satisfies the proposed response allowance. Nevertheless,
\[
\mathbb P\left(|x_K|\le A\sqrt{\log n}\right)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Thus clipping occurs with probability tending to one. It cannot be removed as a rare event.

Feeding this state into a later \(A\)- or \(A^\top\)-query transfers the same scale to the conditional projection mean. The orthogonal innovation remains Gaussian, but the full conditional field is not \(O(1)\)-sub-Gaussian because its mean is already \(n^{o(1)}\). SVD thresholding controls ill-conditioning of the query span; it does not control the coordinate size or leverage of the conditional mean.

There is another necessary restriction. If a program is allowed to divide by a small query singular value, then an \(\varepsilon\)-sized query difference can be rescaled to an order-one message. Dropping that direction produces order-one error. A uniform Jacobian bound by \(R\) excludes this when
\[
\varepsilon^{-1}>R.
\]
Since discarded directions satisfy \(\varepsilon\lesssim\sqrt\tau\), the restriction must be stated explicitly. Without it, covariance thresholding is invalid.

The concentration arithmetic is otherwise plausible:

- \(K=n^{o(1)}\) permits a union bound over all steps;
- Lipschitz constants \(n^{o(1)}\) preserve Gaussian concentration rates \(n^{-c+o(1)}\);
- orthogonal innovations have the correct conditional covariance;
- sufficiently aggressive \(E\) can dominate accumulated threshold errors.

What fails is the inference
\[
\text{response }n^{o(1)}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\text{fields }O(\sqrt{\log n}).
\]
It is false even for a scalar causal recursion.

A valid theorem needs additional hypotheses:

1. uniform \(O(1)\) moment or Lyapunov bounds for every state field;
2. uniformly bounded Onsager projection means;
3. a no-small-singular-value-amplification condition;
4. quantitative row-incoherence or coordinate-tail control for conditional projections;
5. \(E\) large enough to dominate both the number of steps and response amplification.

Without these, causal Gaussian projection plus \(n^{o(1)}\) response does not yield uniform state evolution or removable clipping.

No universal impossibility follows from symmetry, rank, antipodes, close pairs, or label cancellation.

A concrete activation defeats all such obstructions:

\[
\psi_\varepsilon(x)
=
\frac{x+\varepsilon(\sin x+\cos x)}
{\sqrt{1+2\varepsilon e^{-1/2}+\varepsilon^2}},
\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\frac1{2\sqrt2}.
\]

It is analytic, non-affine, at most linearly growing, and \(\psi_\varepsilon'\) is bounded and bounded away from zero. Both \(\psi_\varepsilon\) and \(\psi_\varepsilon'\) have nonzero Gaussian-Hermite coefficients in every degree.

For any finite set of distinct normalized inputs, their covariance kernels are strictly positive definite. Indeed, if \(a_k\neq0\) are the Hermite coefficients,

\[
K(x,y)=\sum_{k\ge0}a_k^2\langle x,y\rangle^k.
\]

If \(c^\top Kc=0\), then

\[
\sum_i c_i x_i^{\otimes k}=0
\]

for every \(k\). Choose \(v\) such that the finitely many \(v^\top x_i\) are distinct and contract for \(k=0,\ldots,m-1\); the resulting Vandermonde system gives \(c=0\). The same argument applies to \(\psi_\varepsilon'\). Applying it again to the strictly-positive-definite first-layer feature Gram proves that the second-layer output-feature Gram is strictly positive definite.

Consequently, for every nonzero label vector \(y\),

\[
c_1(S)=\frac2m\sum_a y_a\psi_\varepsilon(S_a)
\]

is nonzero in \(L^2\). Moreover,

\[
U^{D}_{ab}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
c_1(S)^2\psi_\varepsilon'(S_a)\psi_\varepsilon'(S_b)
\right]
\]

is strictly positive definite. If \(v^\top U^Dv=0\), analyticity and the full support of the nondegenerate Gaussian \(S\) imply

\[
c_1(s)\sum_a v_a\psi_\varepsilon'(s_a)\equiv0.
\]

Since \(c_1\not\equiv0\), coordinate variation forces \(v=0\). This eliminates label cancellation in the leading middle-layer field. The reused-adjoint cavity innovation has covariance \(U^D\); after first-layer gating its covariance is the Schur product of two strictly-positive-definite matrices. Hence even when the input Gram \(R\) is singular,

\[
\operatorname{tr}(R^2\Sigma)>0
\]

for that innovation covariance \(\Sigma\), because \(R\neq0\). Thus singular input geometry cannot kill first-layer motion.

For the especially transparent choice \(\phi=\sin+\cos\),

\[
K_{ab}=e^{-1+R_{ab}}
=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\|x_a-x_b\|^2}{2d}\right).
\]

For an opposite-label pair with correlation \(r<1\), the forcing energy is strictly positive and tends to zero only as \(r\uparrow1\). Thus arbitrarily close but fixed distinct inputs cause poor conditioning, not an exact obstruction. Antipodal inputs are also separated because the activation has both even and odd components.

There is an activation-specific obstruction for every polynomial activation of degree \(p\): take the full hypercube in dimension \(d>p^2\) and parity labels \(y(x)=\prod_r x_r\). Every depth-two network feature and parameter derivative has input degree at most \(p^2\), hence zero correlation with the parity labels. In the width-first scaling with zero limiting output, the limiting trajectory is stationary. This rules out polynomial workbenches, but cannot establish the requested universal negative theorem.

Therefore a valid negative resolution would need a genuine theorem showing that the reciprocal reused-matrix dynamics cannot possess a local autonomous width limit for any such universal activation. Geometry, equivariance, finite dimension, arbitrary labels, antipodes, and arbitrarily close fixed pairs do not provide that theorem. The evidence instead supports the affirmative route; its remaining obligation is the quantitative continuous-time reused-matrix limit, not dataset separability.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 30903 | 2026-08-27T14:02:48.920Z | /root/special_activation_route ===
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER

Payload:
Route assessment:

- \(\phi(x)=\sin x+\cos x\) with
  \[
  \sigma_1^2=d^{-1},\quad \sigma_2^2=n^{-1},\quad \sigma_3^2=n^{-4},\qquad
  (\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)=(n/d,1,1/n)
  \]
  passes every dataset-geometry and nondegeneracy test for every finite set of distinct RMS-normalized inputs.

- Its initialization kernels satisfy
  \[
  Q^{(1)}_{ab}=e^{-1+R_{ab}}
  =e^{-\|x_a-x_b\|^2/(2d)},\qquad
  Q^{(2)}_{ab}=e^{-1+Q^{(1)}_{ab}},
  \]
  and are strictly positive definite. The derivative kernel equals \(Q^{(1)}\). Consequently arbitrary labels cannot cancel output learning, either hidden block, or feature motion.

- With \(C=nc\), \(C(0)\to0\), and \(\gamma=2/m\),
  \[
  C(t)=\gamma t\,g_y+O(t^2),\qquad
  g_y(S)=\sum_a y_a\phi(S^a).
  \]
  The matrix
  \[
  M_{ab}
  =\mathbb E\!\left[g_y(S)^2\phi'(S^a)\phi'(S^b)\right]
  \]
  is strictly positive definite: a zero quadratic form would make an analytic function vanish on the full-support Gaussian law, and coordinate variation then forces every coefficient to vanish. Exact one-round Gaussian regression shows that the reused-adjoint field \(B_0^\top V\) has an independent innovation of covariance \(M\), plus its response term. This proves strict leading first-layer motion even when the input Gram matrix is singular. The middle-layer coefficient is strictly positive directly from \(Q^{(1)}>0\).

- Cancellation in total second-layer motion is impossible. For all second-preactivations collected into \(S\), hidden parameters \(h=(W^{(1)},B)\), their mobility \(M_h\), and
  \(v_{ja}=e_aC_j\phi'(S_j^a)\),
  \[
  \dot S=-\frac2mJ_SM_hJ_S^*v,\qquad
  \langle v,\dot S\rangle
  =-\frac2m\|M_h^{1/2}J_S^*v\|^2.
  \]
  The nonzero \(B\)-gradient therefore forces \(\dot S\ne0\).

- Kernel motion also has a clean positive certificate. Put
  \[
  \Psi(h)=\frac12\left\|\sum_a y_a\phi(S^a(h))\right\|_n^2.
  \]
  Then
  \[
  h(t)=h_0+\frac{\gamma^2t^2}{2}M_h\nabla\Psi(h_0)+O(t^3),
  \]
  and hence
  \[
  y^\top\!\left(\Theta(t)-\Theta(0)\right)y
  =2\gamma^2t^2\|M_h^{1/2}\nabla\Psi(h_0)\|^2+O(t^3)>0.
  \]
  Also
  \[
  \dot{\mathcal L}(0)=-\frac4{m^2}y^\top Q^{(2)}y<0.
  \]
  Both Gaussian hidden marginals initially have variance one, and their nonlinear regression residuals are strictly positive and persist locally.

The unresolved bridge is the compact-time width limit for the reciprocal reused \(B_0,B_0^\top\) dynamics. Fixed-step Gaussian conditioning does not imply it. The promising self-contained route is adaptive two-sided conditioning with orthonormal source compression; this removes all Gram inverses and \(\tau^{-K}\) losses. However, it still requires a proved uniform tail/stability estimate for adaptive conditional means. An \(L^2\) bound does not exclude column alignment, while a max-norm estimate can produce lognormal response tails and destroys uniform Euler stability. Neither the gradient identities nor
\(\phi^2+(\phi')^2=2\) presently closes that estimate.

The single-sample arctangent coordinate argument cannot repair this: for two independent, nonorthogonal inputs, the fields
\[
V_x(w)=\phi'(x^\top w)x,\qquad V_y(w)=\phi'(y^\top w)y
\]
have
\[
[V_x,V_y]
=(x^\top y)\!\left[
\phi'(x^\top w)\phi''(y^\top w)y
-\phi'(y^\top w)\phi''(x^\top w)x
\right].
\]
Flattening all sample gates would force this bracket to vanish, which forces \(\phi''\equiv0\).

Therefore the affirmative construction is algebraically and geometrically sound, and no universal negative obstruction survives, but a fully rigorous proof of properties 1–2 is not currently closed. Claiming complete resolution would overstate the result.

Obstruction audit complete: no universal impossibility was found.

Distinct-input geometry is not an obstruction for mixed-parity analytic activations with every Hermite degree, such as normalized \(x+\varepsilon(\sin x+\cos x)\). Their activation and derivative kernels are strictly positive definite on every finite distinct normalized dataset, including antipodal and arbitrarily close inputs. Consequently, arbitrary \(\pm1\) labels cannot cancel the output forcing, and singular input Gram matrices do not eliminate leading first-layer motion.

Polynomial activations do fail—for example via hypercube parity labels—but that does not extend to universal nonpolynomial activations. Thus any negative resolution would require a genuine impossibility theorem for the reused random-matrix dynamics itself. I found no such theorem or mechanism; the substantive unresolved bridge is the affirmative continuous-time random-operator limit.

Here is the rigorous lemma chain I would use for the reused-matrix part.

### 1. Exact normalized dynamics

Fix a dataset \(\{(x_a,y_a)\}_{a=1}^p\), with \(p,d\) fixed, \(\|x_a\|^2=d\), \(|y_a|\le1\), and

\[
R_{ab}=d^{-1}x_a^\top x_b.
\]

Take

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(1)}_{ir})=d^{-1},\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(B_{ji})=n^{-1},\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(3)}_j)=n^{-4},
\]

and define \(C=nW^{(3)}\). With

\[
\lambda_1=\frac nd,\qquad \lambda_2=1,\qquad \lambda_3=\frac1n,
\]

the normalized variables

\[
U_i^a=W_i^{(1)}\!\cdot x_a,\quad
H^a=\phi(U^a),\quad
S^a=BH^a,\quad
G^a=\phi(S^a),
\]

\[
D^a=C\odot\phi'(S^a),\quad
P^a=B^\top D^a,\quad
f_a=\langle C,G^a\rangle_n,\quad e_a=f_a-y_a
\]

satisfy the exact Euler update, for the averaged loss \(p^{-1}\sum_a e_a^2\),

\[
U_+^a
=
U^a-\frac{2\eta}{p}
\sum_b R_{ab}e_b\,\phi'(U^b)\odot P^b,
\]

\[
B_+
=
B-\frac{2\eta}{p}
\sum_b e_b\,D^b\otimes_n H^b,
\]

\[
C_+
=
C-\frac{2\eta}{p}
\sum_b e_bG^b.
\]

Thus exact GD is Euler discretization of the corresponding finite-width ODE.

For \(\phi\in C_b^4\), put

\[
H=\|\phi\|_\infty,\quad
J=\|\phi'\|_\infty,\quad
Q=\|\phi''\|_\infty.
\]

On

\[
T\le T_b:=\min\{1,(4H^2)^{-1}\},
\]

and on \(\|C(0)\|_\infty\le1\),

\[
|e_a(t)|\le E:=2(1+H),\qquad
\|C(t)\|_\infty\le \bar C:=1+2T_bEH,
\]

hence

\[
\|D^a(t)\|_\infty\le D:=\bar C J.
\]

These follow directly from

\[
|e_a|\le1+H\|C\|_2,\qquad
\frac d{dt}\|C\|_2\le2H\max_a|e_a|.
\]

A fixed-net Gaussian estimate gives \(\|B(0)\|_{\rm op}\le20\) with probability \(1-e^{-cn}\); rank-one integration then bounds \(\sup_{t\le T}\|B(t)\|_{\rm op}\) explicitly.

### 2. Exact Han serialization

Introduce a smooth odd cutoff \(\tau_R\), \(R\ge1\), satisfying

\[
\tau_R(x)=x\quad(|x|\le R),\qquad
|\tau_R(x)|\le |x|,\qquad
\|\tau_R^{(r)}\|_\infty\le c_r,\quad 1\le r\le4.
\]

Only replace \(P\) by \(\tau_R(P)\) in the \(U\)-update.

After \(k\) physical steps,

\[
B_k
=
B_0-\frac{2h}{p}
\sum_{\ell<k,b}e_{\ell b}\,
D_{\ell b}\otimes_n H_{\ell b}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
S_{ka}
=
B_0H_{ka}
-\frac{2h}{p}
\sum_{\ell<k,b}e_{\ell b}
D_{\ell b}\langle H_{\ell b},H_{ka}\rangle_n,
\]

\[
P_{ka}
=
B_0^\top D_{ka}
-\frac{2h}{p}
\sum_{\ell<k,b}e_{\ell b}
H_{\ell b}\langle D_{\ell b},D_{ka}\rangle_n.
\]

Serialize one physical step into \(p\) asymmetric GFOM stages \(r=(k,a)\):

\[
F_r^1=H_{ka},\qquad
G_r^1=L_{ka},
\]

\[
G_r^2=D_{ka},\qquad
F_r^2=M_{ka},
\]

where \(L,M\) are the two displayed historical terms. Han’s order is exact: the \(B_0^\top\) query may depend on the current \(B_0\) query, so \(D_{ka}=C_k\phi'(S_{ka})\) is permitted.

Condition on the independent initial marks
\(\{U_{0,i}^{1:p},C_{0,j}\}\). They then become coordinate-dependent deterministic parameters in the row maps, while \(B_0\) remains independent Gaussian. Set Han’s formal \(u^{(0)}=v^{(0)}=0\).

A discrete derivative induction through order three gives

\[
\Lambda_{R,T}
\le
\exp\!\bigl(c_\phi(1+R)^4T\bigr)
\]

for every value-at-zero, Lipschitz constant, and mixed partial required by Han’s \((D^\ast2)\). The same bound implies \((U^\ast2)'\). The historical sums do not introduce a \(1/h\): every historical coordinate carries \(h\), and \(hK\le T\).

The exact external result used here is Han, arXiv:2406.19061v2, asymmetric GFOM state-evolution theorems `GFOM_se_asym` and `GFOM_se_asym_avg`. Its assumptions and conclusion are:

- \(A=A_0/\sqrt n\), with independent centered subgaussian entries;
- proportional rectangular dimensions;
- the \(C^3\), value, and derivative bounds above;
- no covariance nondegeneracy assumption;
- averaged error bounded by

\[
\left(
K\Lambda\Lambda_\psi\log n
(1+\|u^{(0)}\|_\infty+\|v^{(0)}\|_\infty)
\right)^{C_0s^5}
n^{-1/C_0^s}.
\]

For

\[
h_n=(\log\log n)^{-\gamma},\qquad 0<\gamma<1,
\]

the serialized horizon is

\[
s_n\le pT/h_n+p=O((\log\log n)^\gamma).
\]

Even with \(R_n=O(\log\log\log n)\), the logarithm of Han’s positive factor is \(O((\log\log n)^{5\gamma+1})\), while the negative logarithm is

\[
-\frac{\log n}{C_0^{s_n}}
=
-\exp\!\left(
\log\log n-O((\log\log n)^\gamma)
\right).
\]

Hence the theorem’s error tends to zero along the full sequence.

### 3. Empirical-scalar oracle replacement

Replace every adaptive output, error, \(H\)-Gram, and \(D\)-Gram by its deterministic state-evolution expectation. Conditional on the initial marks, this gives an admissible deterministic-row-map GFOM.

Let \(\zeta_n\) be the maximum oracle empirical error over all required scalar quantities. Each is an empirical average of a pseudo-Lipschitz row-history function. Han’s averaged theorem, with a union bound over \(O(p^2K_n^2)\) quantities, gives

\[
\zeta_n\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]

Couple the adaptive and oracle algorithms using the same \(B_0\). On \(\|B_0\|_{\rm op}\le20\), let \(d_k\) be the sum of normalized \(L^2\) errors in \(U,C\) and the operator-norm error in \(B\). Then

\[
|\widehat\gamma_k-\bar\gamma_k|
\le c_Rd_k+\zeta_n
\]

for every adaptive scalar, and the exact updates give

\[
d_{k+1}
\le
(1+c_Rh)d_k+c_Rh\zeta_n.
\]

All scalar occurrences are either in an \(h\)-weighted parameter update or an \(h\)-weighted historical rank-one sum. Thus no \(\zeta_n/h\) term appears. Discrete Grönwall yields

\[
\max_{kh\le T}d_k
\le
c_RT e^{c_RT}\zeta_n
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]

### 4. Uniform response and tail lemma

The canonical state evolution of the serialized oracle GFOM is exactly

\[
S_{ka}
=
Z_{ka}+L_{ka}
+\sum_{(\ell,b)<(k,a)}
\alpha_{ka,\ell b}D_{\ell b},
\]

\[
P_{ka}
=
Y_{ka}+M_{ka}
+\sum_{(\ell,b)\le(k,a)}
\beta_{ka,\ell b}H_{\ell b},
\]

where

\[
\alpha_{ka,\ell b}
=
\mathbb E\frac{\partial H_{ka}}{\partial Y_{\ell b}},
\qquad
\beta_{ka,\ell b}
=
\mathbb E\frac{\partial D_{ka}}{\partial Z_{\ell b}},
\]

and

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_{ka},Z_{\ell b})
=\mathbb E[H_{ka}H_{\ell b}],
\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(Y_{ka},Y_{\ell b})
=\mathbb E[D_{ka}D_{\ell b}].
\]

Define

\[
A_r=\sum_{q<r}|\alpha_{rq}|,\qquad
B_r=\sum_{q\le r}|\beta_{rq}|.
\]

A lexicographic source-derivative induction is essential:

- \(H_{ka}\) is computed before the current cycle’s \(P\)-queries, so \(\alpha\) has no current-cycle diagonal.
- \(\beta_{r,r}=\mathbb E[C_r\phi''(S_r)]\) is the sole \(O(1)\) diagonal response.
- Every off-diagonal response contains the \(h\) from the end-of-cycle \(U,C\) update.

Under the bootstrap \(A_q\vee B_q\le R_0\), each off-diagonal source derivative satisfies

\[
|\partial H_r/\partial Y_q|
+
|\partial D_r/\partial Z_q|
\le
c_*h
\exp\!\left(
c_*h\sum_{j=\operatorname{cyc}(q)}^{\operatorname{cyc}(r)-1}
\left(1+R_0+\sum_a|P_{ja}|\right)
\right).
\]

For example, the \(Y_q\)-source obeys

\[
|\delta P_{ka}|
\le
\mathbf 1_{\{(k,a)=q\}}
+
(R_0+2TED^2)J
\max_{\ell\le k}|\delta U_\ell|,
\]

followed by

\[
u_{k+1}
\le
\left[
1+c_*h
\left(1+R_0+\sum_a|P_{ka}|\right)
\right]u_k
+c_*h\mathbf 1_{\{q\text{ is in cycle }k\}}.
\]

For a \(Z_q\)-source, one first uses the already-proved individual bound
\(|\alpha_{r,q}|=O(h)\); bounding only \(\sum|\alpha|\) would incorrectly lose the \(h\).

One explicit dominating constant is

\[
c_*
=
2^{12}p
\left(
1+E+\bar C+H+J+Q+D
+2E(H^2+D^2)
\right)^4.
\]

If \(B_r\le R_0\), then

\[
|P_{ka}-Y_{ka}|
\le
M_0+HR_0,\qquad
M_0=2TED^2H,
\]

and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_{ka})\le D^2\). Correlation-free Jensen gives

\[
\mathbb E
\exp\!\left(
c_*h\sum_{j,a}|P_{ja}|
\right)
\le
2\exp\!\left[
c_*pT(M_0+HR_0)
+\frac12(c_*pTD)^2
\right].
\]

Therefore

\[
A_r\vee B_r
\le
\Theta_T(R_0),
\]

where

\[
\Theta_T(R)
=
c_*
\left[
1+2pT
\exp\!\left(
c_*T(1+R)
+c_*pT(M_0+HR)
+\frac12(c_*pTD)^2
\right)
\right].
\]

Let

\[
R_0=2c_*,
\qquad
M_1=2ED^2H,
\]

\[
Q_0
=
c_*(1+R_0)
+c_*p(M_1+HR_0)
+\frac12(c_*pD)^2,
\]

and

\[
T_*=
\min\left\{
T_b,\,
Q_0^{-1},\,
(2ep)^{-1}
\right\}.
\]

Then \(\Theta_T(R_0)\le R_0\) for \(T\le T_*\), closing the induction uniformly in \(h\) and \(R\).

In particular,

\[
P_{ka}=Y_{ka}+\mu_{ka},\qquad
|\mu_{ka}|\le M_1+HR_0,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(Y_{ka})\le D^2,
\]

so

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
P_{ka}^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_{ka}|>x\}}
\right]
\le C_\phi e^{-c_\phi x^2}
\]

uniformly over \(h,R,k,a\).

### 5. Operator action law and continuous time

The continuous limit need not be defined through a Volterra response equation.

Enumerate all finite typed probe programs constructed from:

- current right marks \(U^{1:p}\);
- current left mark \(C\);
- \(B,B^\ast\);
- bounded rational smooth coordinate maps;
- normalized inner products.

The limiting empirical inner products of right and left probes define two positive semidefinite forms. Quotient their null spaces and complete. The finite-width identities

\[
\langle Bv,u\rangle_n=\langle v,B^\ast u\rangle_n,
\qquad
\|Bv\|_n\le M\|v\|_n
\]

pass to the limit. Hence the limiting state contains a genuine bounded operator

\[
B:\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L
\]

with its genuine adjoint. The commutative coordinate probe algebras have \(L^2\) representations. This is an \(n\)-independent current action law, not a retained array or history.

For fixed \(R\), the cutoff vector field is locally Lipschitz on

\[
(\mathcal H_R)^p
\times L^2_L
\times\mathcal B(\mathcal H_R,\mathcal H_L),
\]

because

\[
\|u\otimes v\|_{\rm op}=\|u\|_2\|v\|_2
\]

and \(\tau_R\) is bounded Lipschitz. Picard–Lindelöf therefore gives a unique cutoff operator flow.

Run two cutoff Euler meshes on the same finite-width initialization. Dimension-independent stability gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}d_n(X_n^h(t),X_n^{h'}(t))
\le C_R(h+h').
\]

Every finite probe differs by at most \(C_{R,\mathrm{depth}}(h+h')\). Passing \(n\to\infty\) makes the fixed-mesh action laws Cauchy. Their limit is exactly the Banach-space cutoff ODE. This proves continuous-time identification and restartability without a separate continuous response theorem.

### 6. Removing the cutoff

For uncut and cutoff states starting together, let

\[
d(t)
=
\sum_a\|U^a-U_R^a\|_2
+\|C-C_R\|_2
+\|B-B_R\|_{\rm op}.
\]

Then

\[
d'(t)
\le
c(1+R)d(t)
+c\|P_R(t)\mathbf1_{\{|P_R(t)|>R/2\}}\|_2,
\]

using

\[
|x|\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R\}}
\le
2|y|\mathbf1_{\{|y|>R/2\}}+2|x-y|.
\]

Moreover, uniformly in \(R\),

\[
\|\dot P_R(t)\|_2\le C,
\]

because

\[
\dot P_R
=
\dot B_R^\ast D_R
+B_R^\ast
\left[
\dot C_R\phi'(S_R)
+C_R\phi''(S_R)
\bigl(\dot B_RH_R+B_R(\phi'(U_R)\dot U_R)\bigr)
\right],
\]

and \(|\tau_R(P)|\le|P|\).

A smooth pseudo-Lipschitz majorant of
\(x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R/2\}}\), Han’s averaged theorem, the oracle coupling, and the preceding state-evolution tail yield

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\|P_R(t)\mathbf1_{\{|P_R(t)|>R/2\}}\|_2
\le
Ce^{-cR^2}+Ch+o_{\mathbb P}(1).
\]

Thus

\[
d(T)
\le
Ce^{cRT}
\left(e^{-cR^2}+h+o_{\mathbb P}(1)\right).
\]

Choose

\[
h_n=(\log\log n)^{-\gamma},\qquad
R_n=\log\log\log n,
\]

and shrink \(T_*\) so \(cT_*<\gamma/2\). The right-hand side vanishes.

The limiting uncut flow is unique in the natural subgaussian action-state class. If two solutions have distance \(d\), all terms except the first-layer gate are \(O(d)\). Uniform Gaussian tails give, with
\(A=4K\sqrt{\log(eK/d)}\),

\[
\|[\phi'(U)-\phi'(\widetilde U)]P\|_2
\le
C\,d\sqrt{\log(eK/d)}.
\]

Hence

\[
d(t)
\le
C\int_0^t
d(s)\sqrt{\log(eK/d(s))}\,ds.
\]

Since

\[
\int_{0^+}\frac{dr}{r\sqrt{\log(eK/r)}}=\infty,
\]

Bihari–Osgood gives \(d\equiv0\).

### 7. Exact-GD diagonal

Take

\[
\eta_{0,n}=e^{-n}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_1=\frac nd e^{-n}\to0,\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_2=e^{-n}\to0,\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_3=\frac{e^{-n}}n\to0.
\]

On the bounded region above,

\[
\|P\|_\infty\le\sqrt n\,\|P\|_2,
\]

so a finite-width local Lipschitz constant is at worst \(C\sqrt n\); a coarser polynomial bound would also suffice. The deterministic Euler estimate is therefore

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d(\mathrm{GD}_n(t),\mathrm{ODE}_n(t))
\le
\operatorname{poly}(n)e^{-n}e^{C\sqrt nT}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Han is used only on the auxiliary coarse mesh \(h_n\), never on the \(e^n\) exact-GD steps.

### Application to \(\phi=\sin+\cos\)

Here

\[
H=J=Q=\sqrt2,
\qquad
\mathbb E[\phi(G)^2]=1.
\]

For jointly standard Gaussian \(U_a,U_b\) with covariance \(R_{ab}\),

\[
\mathbb E[\phi(U_a)\phi(U_b)]
=
e^{-1+R_{ab}},
\]

and likewise

\[
\mathbb E[\phi'(U_a)\phi'(U_b)]
=
e^{-1+R_{ab}}.
\]

Thus every constant above depends only on the fixed sample count and numerical activation bounds. Han permits singular \(R\). For distinct normalized inputs,

\[
e^{-1+R_{ab}}
=
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\|x_a-x_b\|^2}{2d}\right)
\]

is strictly positive definite.

### Adversarial audit

The following potential failures are genuinely addressed:

- Han is not applied to the exponentially many exact-GD steps.
- Random empirical feedback is removed before invoking Han.
- Every adaptive scalar is \(h\)-weighted, so no \(1/h\) oracle error occurs.
- The same-stage reused-adjoint response is retained as the diagonal \(\beta_{r,r}\).
- Singular Gaussian covariances are allowed by Han.
- Continuous time is obtained by same-width cross-mesh stability, not by extrapolating a finite-step theorem.
- Cutoff removal uses a proved uniform Gaussian tail, not fixed-moment extrapolation.
- Autonomous uniqueness is proved in the current operator state, avoiding an unclosed infinite response-derivative hierarchy.

The only presentation-heavy obligation remaining is to spell out the routine lexicographic differentiation indices for the response lemma. The inequalities above contain the full induction, including the sole diagonal term and the individual \(O(h)\) bound needed to prevent a hidden \(1/h\). No additional external theorem is required for that bookkeeping.

Payload:
The remaining stability gap can be closed without constructing a continuous infinite response hierarchy. The correct route is:

1. use the discrete Han response coefficients only to prove a mesh- and cutoff-uniform subgaussian bound for the reused-adjoint field \(P\);
2. take \(h\downarrow0\) at fixed cutoff by finite-width Euler stability before taking the state-evolution limit;
3. remove the cutoff and prove uniqueness directly on the current GNS action state by an Osgood estimate.

This avoids both an infinite Hölder cascade and a false Picard argument on an open \(L^2\) set.

### Lemma: response control and uncut action-flow uniqueness

Let \(p\) be the number of samples, \(|R_{ab}|\le1\), \(|y_a|\le1\), and put \(\gamma=2/p\). Suppose

\[
b:=\max_{0\le r\le2}\|\phi^{(r)}\|_\infty\ge1.
\]

For \(\phi=\sin+\cos\), one may take \(b=2\). Let

\[
T_*=\frac1{256b^2}.
\]

Use a smooth cutoff \(\tau_R\) satisfying

\[
\tau_R(x)=x\quad (|x|\le R),\qquad
|\tau_R(x)|\le |x|,\qquad
|\tau_R'(x)|\le2.
\]

For the exact serialized square-matrix canonical state evolution, write \(q=(k,a)\) in lexicographic order:

\[
S_q
=
Z_q+L_q+\sum_{r<q}\alpha_{qr}D_r,
\]

\[
P_q
=
Y_q+M_q+\sum_{r\le q}\beta_{qr}H_r,
\]

where

\[
L_{ka}
=
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,c}
e_{\ell c}Q^H_{\ell c,ka}D_{\ell c},
\]

\[
M_{ka}
=
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,c}
e_{\ell c}Q^D_{\ell c,ka}H_{\ell c},
\]

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_q,Z_r)=Q^H_{qr}
=\mathbb E[H_qH_r],
\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(Y_q,Y_r)=Q^D_{qr}
=\mathbb E[D_qD_r],
\]

and

\[
\alpha_{qr}
=
\mathbb E\frac{\partial H_q}{\partial Y_r},
\qquad
\beta_{qr}
=
\mathbb E\frac{\partial D_q}{\partial Z_r}.
\]

The physical updates are

\[
U_{k+1}^a
=
U_k^a-\gamma h\sum_c
R_{ac}e_{kc}\phi'(U_k^c)\tau_R(P_{kc}),
\]

\[
C_{k+1}
=
C_k-\gamma h\sum_c e_{kc}\phi(S_{kc}),
\]

with \(H_{ka}=\phi(U_k^a)\), \(D_{ka}=C_k\phi'(S_{ka})\), and \(C_0=0\).

Then, for every \(Kh\le T_*\), every \(R\ge1\), and every Gaussian covariance, including singular ones,

\[
\sup_q\sum_{r<q}|\alpha_{qr}|<\frac18,
\qquad
\sup_q\sum_{r\le q}|\beta_{qr}|<\frac18,
\]

and

\[
\sup_q\|P_q\|_{\psi_2}\le K_\phi:=4b.
\]

All constants are independent of \(h,R,K\), and of covariance rank.

#### Proof

The scalar envelopes are deterministic. If \(c_k=\|C_k\|_\infty\), then

\[
c_{k+1}
\le c_k+2hb(1+bc_k).
\]

Hence, for \(kh\le T\),

\[
c_k\le c_T:=\frac{e^{2b^2T}-1}{b},
\qquad
|e_{ka}|\le E_T:=e^{2b^2T},
\qquad
\|D_{ka}\|_\infty\le d_T:=bc_T.
\]

For \(T\le T_*\),

\[
E_T<2,\qquad c_T\le4bT,\qquad d_T\le4b^2T\le\frac1{64}.
\]

The absolute coefficient masses of the learned terms satisfy

\[
\ell_H
:=
\sup_q\sum_r |\text{coefficient of }D_r\text{ in }L_q|
\le2TE_Tb^2,
\]

\[
\ell_D
:=
\sup_q\sum_r |\text{coefficient of }H_r\text{ in }M_q|
\le2TE_Td_T^2.
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal A
=
\sup_q\sum_{r<q}|\alpha_{qr}|,
\qquad
\mathcal B
=
\sup_q\sum_{r\le q}|\beta_{qr}|.
\]

For the left source derivatives define

\[
s_*=\sup_q\sum_r\left|\frac{\partial S_q}{\partial Z_r}\right|,
\quad
c_*=\sup_k\sum_r\left|\frac{\partial C_k}{\partial Z_r}\right|,
\quad
d_*=\sup_q\sum_r\left|\frac{\partial D_q}{\partial Z_r}\right|.
\]

Because state-evolution order parameters are frozen when differentiating a single-site source map,

\[
s_*\le1+(\mathcal A+\ell_H)d_*,
\]

\[
c_*\le2TE_Tb\,s_*,
\]

and

\[
d_*
\le b c_*+c_Tb\,s_*
=
\theta_Ts_*,
\qquad
\theta_T:=b(2TE_Tb+c_T).
\]

Under the bootstrap \(\mathcal A\le1\),

\[
\theta_T\le8b^2T\le\frac1{32},
\qquad
\ell_H\le4b^2T\le\frac1{64}.
\]

Thus

\[
d_*
\le
\frac{\theta_T}
{1-\theta_T(\mathcal A+\ell_H)}
<\frac1{16}.
\]

Since absolute expectation is bounded by expectation of the absolute derivative,

\[
\mathcal B
\le d_*<\frac1{16}.
\]

This estimate includes the same-query diagonal term

\[
\frac{\partial D_q}{\partial Z_q}
=
C_k\phi''(S_q),
\]

which is \(O(T)\) because \(C_0=0\). No diagonal covariance inversion is used.

For the right derivatives, define

\[
u_k
=
\max_a
\sum_r
\left|
\frac{\partial U_k^a}{\partial Y_r}
\right|,
\qquad
\overline P_k=\frac1p\sum_a|P_{ka}|.
\]

Since

\[
\sum_r
\left|
\frac{\partial P_{ka}}{\partial Y_r}
\right|
\le
1+b(\mathcal B+\ell_D)u_k,
\]

differentiating the \(U\)-update gives

\[
u_{k+1}
\le
\left[
1+2hE_Tb\,\overline P_k
+4hE_Tb^2(\mathcal B+\ell_D)
\right]u_k
+
4hE_Tb.
\]

Define the pathwise response weight

\[
\Lambda_k
=
2E_Tb\,h\sum_{\ell<k}\overline P_\ell
+
4E_Tb^2(\mathcal B+\ell_D)kh.
\]

The preceding recurrence yields exactly

\[
\sup_{k\le K}e^{-\Lambda_k}u_k
\le4E_TbT.
\tag{1}
\]

This is the needed weighted response norm. It absorbs multiplication by \(P\) before expectation.

Furthermore,

\[
P_q=Y_q+B_q,
\qquad
|B_q|\le b(\mathcal B+\ell_D),
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(Y_q)\le d_T^2.
\]

Therefore, for every \(\lambda\ge0\),

\[
\mathbb E e^{\lambda|P_q|}
\le
2\exp\!\left(
\lambda b(\mathcal B+\ell_D)
+\frac{\lambda^2d_T^2}{2}
\right).
\tag{2}
\]

Jensen's inequality, with \(Kh\le T\), gives

\[
\mathbb E
\exp\!\left(
2E_Tb\,h\sum_{k<K}\overline P_k
\right)
\le
\sup_q
\mathbb E e^{2E_TbT|P_q|}.
\tag{3}
\]

Combining (1)–(3),

\[
\mathcal A
\le
4E_Tb^2T
e^{4E_Tb^2(\mathcal B+\ell_D)T}
\sup_q\mathbb E e^{2E_TbT|P_q|}
<\frac18
\]

for \(T\le T_*\). A first-exit induction closes the simultaneous bootstrap \(\mathcal A,\mathcal B\le1\).

Finally, the improved bounds imply

\[
|B_q|\le\frac b8,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(Y_q)\le\frac1{64^2}.
\]

Consequently

\[
\mathbb E\exp\!\left(\frac{P_q^2}{(4b)^2}\right)<2,
\]

which proves \(\|P_q\|_{\psi_2}\le4b\).

The proof used only one-dimensional Gaussian exponential moments, so arbitrary temporal correlations and singular Gram matrices cause no problem.

### Why the weighted norm closes response differences

Suppose two tangent recurrences have the form

\[
j_{k+1}\le(1+ha_k)j_k+hf_k,
\qquad
\widetilde j_{k+1}
\le(1+h\widetilde a_k)\widetilde j_k+h\widetilde f_k,
\]

where the difference forcing contains

\[
|f_k-\widetilde f_k|
\le C|\delta P_k|\,\widetilde j_k+\cdots.
\]

Put

\[
A_k=\sum_{\ell<k}
\log\!\bigl(1+h\max\{a_\ell,\widetilde a_\ell\}\bigr).
\]

Then

\[
e^{-A_k}|\delta j_k|
\le
h\sum_{\ell<k}
e^{-A_{\ell+1}}
|f_\ell-\widetilde f_\ell|.
\]

Since \(\widetilde j_\ell\le C e^{\widetilde A_\ell}\) and
\(A_{\ell+1}\ge\widetilde A_\ell\),

\[
e^{-A_{\ell+1}}
|\delta P_\ell|\,\widetilde j_\ell
\le C|\delta P_\ell|.
\]

Thus

\[
\left\|
\sup_k e^{-A_k}|\delta j_k|
\right\|_2
\le
CT^{1/2}
\left(
h\sum_k\|\delta P_k\|_2^2
\right)^{1/2}
+\cdots.
\]

There is no \(L^{2p}\to L^{4p}\to\cdots\) cascade. The random multiplier is removed pathwise before taking an \(L^2\) norm.

### Fixed-cutoff \(h\downarrow0\)

Let the current two-sorted action state be

\[
(U^1,\ldots,U^p,C,\mathcal B)
\in
\mathcal H_R^p\times\mathcal H_L
\times\mathcal L(\mathcal H_R,\mathcal H_L),
\]

with

\[
H^a=\phi(U^a),\qquad
S^a=\mathcal BH^a,\qquad
D^a=C\phi'(S^a),\qquad
P^a=\mathcal B^*D^a.
\]

The cutoff vector field is

\[
\dot U^a
=
-\gamma\sum_b
R_{ab}e_b\phi'(U^b)\tau_R(P^b),
\]

\[
\dot{\mathcal B}
=
-\gamma\sum_b e_bD^b\otimes H^b,
\qquad
\dot C
=
-\gamma\sum_b e_b\phi(S^b).
\tag{4}
\]

Do not invoke Picard–Lindelöf on an open \(L^2\) ball: the proof would be invalid because \(C\) is controlled in \(L^\infty\), while the metric is \(L^2\).

Instead, every Euler scheme satisfies the pointwise invariant envelope

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty\le c_T.
\]

On this invariant class, if
\(\|\mathcal B\|_{\rm op}\le\Lambda_T\), then direct estimates give

\[
\|\delta H\|_2\le b\|\delta U\|_2,
\]

\[
\|\delta S\|_2
\le
b\|\delta\mathcal B\|_{\rm op}
+\Lambda_Tb\|\delta U\|_2,
\]

\[
\|\delta D\|_2
\le
b\|\delta C\|_2+c_Tb\|\delta S\|_2,
\]

\[
\|\delta P\|_2
\le
d_T\|\delta\mathcal B\|_{\rm op}
+\Lambda_T\|\delta D\|_2.
\]

Because \(\tau_R\) is bounded and Lipschitz, the cutoff vector field is Lipschitz on this invariant class in the metric

\[
d^2
=
\sum_a\|\delta U^a\|_2^2
+\|\delta C\|_2^2
+\|\delta\mathcal B\|_{\rm op}^2,
\]

with an explicit constant \(L_R\le C_{\phi,p,T,\Lambda_T}(1+R)\). Its velocity is bounded there. Comparing two Euler interpolants of meshes \(h,h'\) gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}d(t)
\le
C_R(h+h')e^{L_RT}.
\tag{5}
\]

Thus the cutoff flow is defined as the Euler-Cauchy limit. Strong \(L^2\) convergence and an a.e. subsequence preserve \(|C|\le c_T\). Equation (4) follows by passage to the integral equations, and the same estimate proves uniqueness.

For state evolutions living on different probability spaces, apply (5) first to the two finite-width Euler schemes using the same initialization, and only then take \(n\to\infty\) jointly for the two fixed meshes. This proves that the fixed-mesh Gaussian action laws are Cauchy as \(h\downarrow0\), without comparing continuous response systems.

### Removing the cutoff

The required product estimate is the following. If

\[
\|X\|_{\psi_2}\le K,\qquad
\|q\|_\infty\le b,\qquad
\operatorname{Lip}(q)\le b,
\]

then, writing \(x=\|U-\widetilde U\|_2^2\),

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
X^2|q(U)-q(\widetilde U)|^2
\right]
\le
3b^2K^2x\log\frac{A}{x},
\tag{6}
\]

for any explicit \(A\ge8e(1+\|U\|_2^2+\|\widetilde U\|_2^2)\).

Indeed, split at

\[
r^2=K^2\log(A/x).
\]

On \(|X|\le r\), use
\(|q(U)-q(\widetilde U)|\le b|U-\widetilde U|\). On the complement use
\(|q(U)-q(\widetilde U)|\le2b\) and

\[
\mathbb E[X^2\mathbf1_{\{|X|>r\}}]
\le
2(r^2+K^2)e^{-r^2/K^2}.
\]

This proves (6).

Let two reachable action solutions have

\[
\sup_{t\le T,a}
\bigl(
\|P^a(t)\|_{\psi_2}
+
\|\widetilde P^a(t)\|_{\psi_2}
\bigr)
\le K_\phi.
\]

For their squared state distance \(E(t)\), all terms in (4) are linearly controlled by \(E(t)\), except

\[
\langle
\delta U,
[\phi'(U)-\phi'(\widetilde U)]\widetilde P
\rangle.
\]

By Cauchy–Schwarz and (6),

\[
D^+E(t)
\le
C E(t)
\sqrt{\log\frac{A}{E(t)}}.
\tag{7}
\]

Since

\[
\int_{0^+}
\frac{ds}{s\sqrt{\log(A/s)}}=\infty,
\]

(7) gives uniqueness.

For the cutoff comparison, use

\[
|x-\tau_R(x)|
\le2|x|\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R\}},
\]

and

\[
\delta_R
:=
8(R^2+K_\phi^2)e^{-R^2/K_\phi^2}.
\]

Then

\[
D^+E_R(t)
\le
C E_R(t)
\sqrt{\log\frac{A}{E_R(t)}}
+
C\delta_R.
\tag{8}
\]

Putting \(Z=E_R+\delta_R\) and integrating (8) yields

\[
\sup_{t\le T}E_R(t)
\le
A\exp\!\left[
-\left(
\sqrt{\log\frac{A}{\delta_R}}-\frac{CT}{2}
\right)_+^2
\right].
\tag{9}
\]

Hence the cutoff flows are Cauchy as \(R\to\infty\). Their limit solves the uncut action equations because the uniform \(\psi_2\) estimate makes

\[
\tau_R(P_R)\longrightarrow P
\]

in \(L^2\). The same Osgood inequality proves uniqueness.

The subgaussian estimate passes from the discrete canonical state evolutions to the cutoff continuum limit by lower semicontinuity of

\[
X\mapsto \mathbb E e^{X^2/K_\phi^2}
\]

under weak convergence, and then to the uncut limit using (9).

### Adversarial audit

The chain is valid with these qualifications:

- Well-posedness is in the explicit Gaussian-reachable action class with
  \(\|P\|_{\psi_2}\le4b\), not among every abstract bounded-operator \(L^2\) state. A bounded operator can send a bounded vector to an arbitrary \(L^2\) heavy-tailed vector, so unrestricted GNS uniqueness is not proved.
- No inverse covariance or nondegeneracy is used; singular Gaussian covariances are harmless.
- The current \(\beta\)-diagonal is included and is small only because \(C_0=0\). The vanishing normalized random initialization must be passed to \(C_0=0\) before applying the stated constants.
- Oracle overlaps, errors, and response coefficients are held fixed in source differentiation, exactly as in the canonical state-evolution definition.
- The response multiplier is controlled pathwise by the weight \(\Lambda_k\); no hidden Hölder iteration occurs.
- Fixed-cutoff existence is obtained by Euler-Cauchy convergence on the invariant \(L^\infty\)-bounded class, not by an invalid open-set \(L^2\) Picard theorem.
- Continuous response kernels are not needed for uniqueness or \(h\downarrow0\) identification. The current action state \((U,C,\mathcal B)\) is autonomous and contains no trajectory history.

Thus this part of the proof is not a remaining open bridge. The remaining uses of Han are only: identifying each fixed finite mesh with the displayed canonical state evolution and obtaining the quantitative growing-step diagonal approximation.

Independent audit result: the operator-action route is viable; I found no fatal gap after replacing growing-step Han by a fixed-mesh argument.

The essential rigorous chain is:

1. For each fixed cutoff \(R\), saturate \(C\) outside an invariant bound and replace \(P\) by a smooth \(\chi_R(P)\). The resulting vector field is dimension-independently Lipschitz in normalized \(L^2\oplus L^2\oplus\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}\).

2. Uniform Euler error reduces the cutoff flow to finitely many steps at any fixed mesh. Those steps serialize into Han’s asymmetric GFOM. Han is used only at a fixed finite iteration count; empirical scalars are handled by a finite oracle-coupling induction.

3. The GNS construction is legitimate: deterministic limits of a countable family of probes define two commutative \(L^2\) spaces, and
   \[
   \|\mathcal Bv\|_2\le 3\|v\|_2,\qquad
   \langle u,\mathcal Bv\rangle=\langle \mathcal B^*u,v\rangle
   \]
   pass from finite width. The limiting state \((w,C,\mathcal B)\) is current, autonomous, and does not retain \(B_0\) or the trajectory history.

4. The only genuinely difficult lemma is the uniform cavity-response bound. On every fixed-mesh state-evolution DAG, write
   \[
   S_k=\zeta_k+\sum_{r<k}\alpha_{kr}D_r+\text{trained memory},
   \qquad
   P_k=\xi_k+\sum_{r\le k}\beta_{kr}H_r+\text{trained memory},
   \]
   with
   \[
   \operatorname{Cov}\zeta=\mathbb E[HH],\quad
   \operatorname{Cov}\xi=\mathbb E[DD],\quad
   \alpha=\mathbb E[\partial_\xi H],\quad
   \beta=\mathbb E[\partial_\zeta D].
   \]
   If
   \[
   A=\sup_k\sum_r|\alpha_{kr}|,\qquad
   B=\sup_k\sum_r|\beta_{kr}|,\qquad
   K=\sup_k\|P_k\|_{\psi_2},
   \]
   direct differentiation gives
   \[
   K\le c(T+B),
   \]
   \[
   B\le cT\{1+(A+cT)B\},
   \]
   \[
   A\le cT(1+BA)\exp(cT+cT^2K^2).
   \]
   A first-crossing argument gives, for an explicit sufficiently small \(T\),
   \[
   A+B\le cT,\qquad K\le cT,
   \]
   uniformly in the mesh, cutoff, and covariance rank. No covariance inverse or Price differentiation is used, so singular Gaussian covariances cause no problem.

5. Consequently,
   \[
   \mathbb E\!\left[|P_R|^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_R|>R\}}\right]
   \le cT^2\exp\!\left(-\frac{R^2}{cT^2}\right).
   \]
   The exact uncut-versus-cutoff decomposition yields
   \[
   d_{R,n}'(t)
   \le C(1+R)d_{R,n}(t)
     +C\|P_{R,n}-\chi_R(P_{R,n})\|_2.
   \]
   Hence the cutoff error is bounded by an exponential linear in \(R\) times a Gaussian tail quadratic in \(R\), and therefore tends to zero.

6. The same decomposition proves pairwise cutoff Cauchy convergence. For \(Q\ge R\), insert \(P_Q\) and \(P_R\); the only sources are the two cutoff tails, while the coefficient on the state discrepancy is \(O(R)\). It also proves uniqueness of any uncut solution by comparison with the cutoff solutions. Thus an Osgood argument and an a priori tail assumption on the uncut trajectory are unnecessary.

The proof remains incomplete if the serialized GFOM induction or the three response inequalities above are only asserted. If they are derived node by node, as indicated, the current-action construction, cutoff removal, full-sequence convergence, autonomy, and uniqueness all pass the adversarial audit.

=== OPERATOR1 ROLLOUT LINE 31595 | 2026-08-27T18:31:29.472Z | /root/random_operator_limit ===

Task name: /root

Payload:
The \(L=2\) reused-operator proof extends to \(L=3\). The correct reduction is to combine the two independent Gaussian matrices into one typed rectangular Gaussian operator. No new external state-evolution theorem is needed.

## 1. Exact normalized \(L=3\) system

For fixed \(p,d\), let

\[
R_{ab}=d^{-1}x_a^\top x_b,\qquad \|x_a\|^2=d.
\]

Use

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(1)}_{ir})=d^{-1},\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(B^{(1)}_{ji})
=\operatorname{Var}(B^{(2)}_{kj})=n^{-1},
\]

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(4)}_k)=n^{-4},
\]

and mobilities

\[
\lambda_1=\frac nd,\qquad
\lambda_2=\lambda_3=1,\qquad
\lambda_4=\frac1n.
\]

Set \(C=nW^{(4)}\) and

\[
H^1_a=\phi(U_a),\qquad
S^2_a=B^{(1)}H^1_a,\qquad
H^2_a=\phi(S^2_a),
\]

\[
S^3_a=B^{(2)}H^2_a,\qquad
H^3_a=\phi(S^3_a),\qquad
f_a=\langle C,H^3_a\rangle_n.
\]

Define backward fields

\[
D^3_a=C\phi'(S^3_a),\qquad
P^2_a=(B^{(2)})^\top D^3_a,
\]

\[
D^2_a=\phi'(S^2_a)P^2_a,\qquad
P^1_a=(B^{(1)})^\top D^2_a.
\]

For \(e_a=f_a-y_a\), the exact normalized gradient ODE is

\[
\dot U_a
=
-\frac2p\sum_bR_{ab}e_b\phi'(U_b)P^1_b,
\]

\[
\dot B^{(1)}
=
-\frac2p\sum_be_bD^2_b\otimes_nH^1_b,
\]

\[
\dot B^{(2)}
=
-\frac2p\sum_be_bD^3_b\otimes_nH^2_b,
\]

\[
\dot C
=
-\frac2p\sum_be_bH^3_b.
\]

Exact GD is Euler discretization of these equations.

## 2. One-matrix block embedding

Let

\[
\mathcal H_R=\mathcal H_1\oplus\mathcal H_3,\qquad
\mathcal H_L=\mathcal H_2
\]

and define

\[
A=[B^{(1)},(B^{(2)})^\top]:
\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L.
\]

Thus

\[
A(v_1,v_3)=B^{(1)}v_1+(B^{(2)})^\top v_3,
\]

\[
A^\ast w=((B^{(1)})^\top w,B^{(2)}w).
\]

The \(n\times2n\) matrix \(A\) has independent \(N(0,1/n)\) entries. Therefore it satisfies exactly Han’s asymmetric GFOM assumption \(A=A_0/\sqrt m\) with \(m=n\), \(2n\) columns, and aspect ratio \(1/2\).

Equip the direct sum with

\[
\|(v_1,v_3)\|_R^2
=
\|v_1\|_n^2+\|v_3\|_n^2.
\]

Then \(A^\ast\) is precisely the physical transpose. Han’s \(1/(2n)\) right-population averages are multiplied by the fixed factor \(2\) whenever a physical block average \(1/n\) is required.

The learned operator is

\[
A_k=A_0-\frac{2h}{p}\sum_{\ell<k,b}e_{\ell b}
\left[
D^2_{\ell b}\otimes(H^1_{\ell b},0)
+
H^2_{\ell b}\otimes(0,D^3_{\ell b})
\right].
\]

Hence every learned-matrix multiplication reduces exactly to \(A_0,A_0^\ast\), block projections, and \(h\)-weighted scalar overlaps.

## 3. Exact two-stage serialization

For each sample \(a\) and physical step \(k\), use two Han stages.

Stage 1:

\[
F^1_{ka,1}=(H^1_{ka},0),
\]

so the \(A\)-query gives

\[
u_{ka,1}=S^2_{ka}.
\]

Set

\[
G^2_{ka,1}=H^2_{ka}.
\]

Then \(A^\ast H^2_{ka}\) contains \(S^3_{ka}\) in its layer-3 block.

Stage 2:

\[
F^1_{ka,2}=(0,D^3_{ka}),
\]

so the \(A\)-query gives

\[
u_{ka,2}=P^2_{ka}.
\]

Set

\[
G^2_{ka,2}=D^2_{ka}.
\]

Then \(A^\ast D^2_{ka}\) contains \(P^1_{ka}\) in its layer-1 block.

Thus the exact within-sample dependency chain is

\[
S^2\longrightarrow S^3\longrightarrow P^2
\longrightarrow D^2\longrightarrow P^1.
\]

All samples at physical time \(k\) use the same pre-update \(U_k,C_k,A_k\); the simultaneous update is performed only after the \(2p\) query stages.

Condition on the independent initial \(U_0,C_0\) marks. The GFOM row maps are then deterministic and coordinate-dependent, as permitted by Han. Empirical errors and overlaps must first be replaced by deterministic state-evolution oracle values; the adaptive/oracle coupling is identical to \(L=2\), and every scalar discrepancy remains \(h\)-weighted.

## 4. Exact canonical state evolution

Let \(r=(k,a,s)\), \(s\in\{1,2\}\), and write

\[
f_{ka,1}=(H^1_{ka},0),\qquad
f_{ka,2}=(0,D^3_{ka}),
\]

\[
g_{ka,1}=H^2_{ka},\qquad
g_{ka,2}=D^2_{ka}.
\]

Han’s canonical state evolution is exactly

\[
u_r
=
Z_r+G^1_r+\sum_{q<r}\alpha_{rq}g_q,
\]

\[
v_r
=
Y_r+F^2_r+\sum_{q\le r}\beta_{rq}f_q,
\]

with

\[
\alpha_{rq}
=
\mathbb E\,
\partial_{Y_q}(f_r\circ\Xi),
\qquad
\beta_{rq}
=
\mathbb E\,
\partial_{Z_q}(g_r\circ\Phi).
\]

There is no hidden aspect factor: an active block has \(n\) coordinates, each with variance \(1/n\).

The Gaussian covariances are

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_r,Z_q)
=
\mathbb E\langle f_r,f_q\rangle_R,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Y_r,Y_q)
=
\mathbb E[g_rg_q].
\]

In particular, the stage-1 and stage-2 left innovations are uncorrelated because \(f_{ka,1}\) and \(f_{\ell b,2}\) occupy disjoint right blocks.

## 5. Response and tail lemma

Two cutoffs are necessary:

\[
D^2_R
=
\phi'(S^2)\tau_R(P^2),
\]

and \(\tau_R(P^1)\) is used in the \(U\)-update. Cutting only \(P^1\) leaves the unbounded product
\(\phi'(S^2)P^2\) in the \(B^{(1)}\)-update.

Assume \(\phi\in C_b^4\). Within one sample cycle, the only \(O(1)\) response coefficients are:

\[
\beta_{1,1}
=
\mathbb E[\phi'(S^2)],
\]

\[
\alpha_{2,1}
=
\mathbb E[C\phi''(S^3)]
\]

on the layer-3 type,

\[
\beta_{2,2}
=
\mathbb E[
\phi'(S^2)\tau_R'(P^2)
],
\]

and

\[
\beta_{2,1}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi''(S^2)\tau_R(P^2)
+
\phi'(S^2)\tau_R'(P^2)
\alpha_{2,1}\phi'(S^2)
\right].
\]

All other same-cycle cross-sample derivatives vanish because the physical state is frozen across the \(p\) sample queries.

Every response from an earlier physical cycle contains one factor \(h\). More precisely, a lexicographic source differentiation gives

\[
|\partial_{\mathrm{source}}X_k|
\le
ch(1+|P^2_\ell|)
\exp\!\left[
ch\sum_{j=\ell}^{k-1}
\left(
1+\Gamma_0+
\sum_a(|P^1_{ja}|+|P^2_{ja}|)
\right)
\right]
\]

for every historical source. The finite within-cycle chain adds only a polynomial factor \(c(1+|P^2_k|)\).

The tail bootstrap must be triangular. Let \(K_2\) bound the \(\psi_2\)-norms of

\[
S^2,\ S^3,\ P^2,\ D^2,
\]

let \(K_1\) bound \(P^1\), let \(\Gamma_0\) be the fixed within-cycle response mass, and \(\Gamma_h\) the historical response mass. Then

\[
K_2
\le
c_0+c_0(T+\Gamma_h)K_2,
\]

\[
K_1\le c_0(1+K_2),
\]

\[
\Gamma_0\le c_0(1+K_2),
\]

and

\[
\Gamma_h
\le
c_0pT
\exp\!\left[
c_0T(1+\Gamma_0)
+c_0p^2T^2(K_1^2+K_2^2)
\right].
\]

The first inequality uses the crucial fact that every response or learned-rank coefficient multiplying an unbounded historical \(D^2\) is \(O(h)\). No current within-cycle response mean multiplies current \(D^2\).

For example, define

\[
c_0
=
2^{18}p^2
(1+E+\bar C+H+J+Q+D)^8,
\]

\[
\bar K_2=4c_0,\qquad
\bar K_1=4c_0(1+\bar K_2),
\]

\[
\bar\Gamma_0=2c_0(1+\bar K_2),
\]

\[
Q_0
=
c_0(1+\bar\Gamma_0)
+c_0p^2(\bar K_1^2+\bar K_2^2),
\]

and

\[
T_3
=
\min\left\{
T_b,\,
Q_0^{-1},\,
[16epc_0^2(1+J)]^{-1},\,
[4c_0(1+J)]^{-1}
\right\}.
\]

For \(T\le T_3\), these inequalities close with

\[
K_2\le2c_0,\qquad
K_1\le c_0(1+\bar K_2),\qquad
\Gamma_0\le\bar\Gamma_0,
\]

\[
\Gamma_h\le[4c_0(1+J)]^{-1}.
\]

Consequently, uniformly in \(h\), \(R\), time, and sample,

\[
\mathbb E[(P^1)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P^1|>x\}}]
+
\mathbb E[(P^2)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P^2|>x\}}]
\le
Ce^{-cx^2}.
\]

## 6. Cutoff removal

Use the current operator state

\[
X=(U^{1:p},C,A)
\in
\mathcal H_1^p
\times\mathcal H_3
\times\mathcal B(\mathcal H_1\oplus\mathcal H_3,\mathcal H_2).
\]

For fixed \(R\), its vector field is locally Lipschitz with constant

\[
L_R\le C(1+R).
\]

Indeed,

\[
\|\delta D^2_R\|_2
\le
C\bigl(
\|\delta P^2\|_2+R\|\delta S^2\|_2
\bigr),
\]

and all remaining fields are bounded-operator images or rank-one products. No derivative contains two cutoff factors.

For uncut and cutoff solutions starting together,

\[
d'(t)
\le
C(1+R)d(t)
+
C\sum_{\ell=1}^2
\|P^\ell_R
\mathbf1_{\{|P^\ell_R|>R/2\}}\|_2.
\]

The cutoff fields obey

\[
\|\dot P^1_R\|_2+\|\dot P^2_R\|_2
\le C(1+R).
\]

Han’s averaged theorem, the oracle coupling, and the response-tail lemma therefore give

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\sum_{\ell=1}^2
\|P^\ell_R(t)
\mathbf1_{\{|P^\ell_R(t)|>R/2\}}\|_2
\le
Ce^{-cR^2}+Ch(1+R)+o_{\mathbb P}(1).
\]

Hence

\[
d(T)
\le
Ce^{CRT}
\left[
e^{-cR^2}+h(1+R)+o_{\mathbb P}(1)
\right].
\]

Taking

\[
h_n=(\log\log n)^{-\gamma},\qquad
R_n=a\log\log\log n,
\]

with \(0<\gamma<1\) and \(CaT<\gamma\), makes the right-hand side vanish.

Uncut uniqueness follows from two copies of the same Osgood estimate, one for the \(P^1\)-gate and one for the \(P^2\)-gate:

\[
d'(t)
\le
C\,d(t)\sqrt{\log(K/d(t))}.
\]

## 7. Current GNS action state

Include the two fixed type projections in the probe algebra. Limiting probe Gram matrices define

\[
\mathcal H_1\oplus\mathcal H_3,\qquad
\mathcal H_2,
\]

and the finite identities

\[
\langle Av,w\rangle
=
\langle v,A^\ast w\rangle,
\qquad
\|Av\|\le M\|v\|
\]

pass to the completion. The limiting state contains the current bounded \(A\), not two finite arrays or their histories.

Fixed-cutoff cross-mesh convergence follows from finite-width dimension-independent Euler stability before taking \(n\to\infty\). The resulting flow is autonomous and restartable.

## Audit conclusion

No fatal obstruction appears. The following corrections are indispensable:

- use the single block operator \(A=[B^{(1)},(B^{(2)})^\top]\);
- account for the fixed factor \(2\) in right-block empirical averages;
- use two cutoffs, on \(P^2\) and \(P^1\);
- retain all finite within-cycle \(O(1)\) responses;
- separately prove that coefficients multiplying historical unbounded \(D^2\) have \(O(h)\) mass;
- use the triangular \(K_2\)-then-\(K_1\) tail bootstrap rather than one crude response norm.

With these points included, Han’s exact one-matrix theorem covers the two independently reused matrices, including singular Gaussian covariances and a growing auxiliary mesh horizon.
