Second-round verdict: the repaired source argument passes structurally after two explicit corrections. I found no omitted unbounded response term and no counterexample.

## Corrections required

### 1. Track deterministic response mass separately

The claimed pathwise estimate

\[
W_k\le C_p\Bigl(1+\sum_a|P_k^{2,a}|\Bigr)(1+S_k)
\]

is not justified as written. Han’s oracle coefficients \(\alpha,\beta\) are deterministic, so their source derivatives vanish, but their magnitudes cannot be bounded pathwise by the random \(S_k\).

Define the historical response mass

\[
\Gamma_k
=
\sum_{\substack{q<r\\\text{different physical cycles}}}
\bigl(|\alpha_{rq}|+|\beta_{rq}|\bigr).
\]

The correct local estimate is

\[
W_k
\le
C_p
\Bigl(1+\sum_a|P_k^{2,a}|\Bigr)
(1+S_k)(1+\Gamma_k).
\tag{1}
\]

The exact response definitions and the \(h\)-edge property imply

\[
\Gamma_k
\le
C_p\,
\mathbb E\!\left[
\Bigl(1+\sum_a|P_k^{2,a}|\Bigr)S_k
\right].
\tag{2}
\]

Use a simultaneous first-exit bootstrap

\[
K\le1,\qquad \Gamma\le1.
\]

Then \(1+\Gamma_k\) is absorbed into \(C_p\), yielding the intended estimate.

### 2. Include the missing \(H^3\) derivative

The local derivative table must also contain

\[
|DH^3|\le \|\phi'\|_\infty |DT|.
\tag{3}
\]

It is needed for the \(C\)-update, output probes, loss, and path-velocity probes.

## Source classification audit

With the physical state frozen while all \(2p\) matrix queries of a cycle are made, different samples in the same cycle do not create additional functional source paths. Their Gaussian innovations can be correlated, but correlation does not create a partial derivative in Han’s transformation variables.

The only \(O(1)\) within-cycle response band is therefore, with sample/species masks,

\[
\beta_{11}=\mathbb E\phi'(S),
\]

\[
\alpha_{21}=\mathbb E[C\phi''(T)],
\]

\[
\beta_{22}
=
\mathbb E[\phi'(S)\tau_R'(P^2)],
\]

\[
\beta_{21}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi''(S)\tau_R(P^2)
+
\phi'(S)\tau_R'(P^2)
\alpha_{21}\phi'(S)
\right].
\]

All cross-cycle source paths cross either:

- a slow update \(U_k,C_k\mapsto U_{k+1},C_{k+1}\), or
- a learned-rank memory term,

and therefore contain \(h\). Oracle outputs, errors, overlaps, and response coefficients are deterministic during source differentiation, so they contribute no derivative terms.

The corrected node induction gives

\[
S_{k+1}
\le
S_k+
Ch
\left(
1+\sum_a(|P_k^{1,a}|+|P_k^{2,a}|)
\right)(1+S_k).
\tag{4}
\]

Under \(K\le1\),

\[
1+S_k
\le
\exp\!\left[
Ch\sum_{r<k,a}
(1+|P_r^{1,a}|+|P_r^{2,a}|)
\right].
\]

The required Hölder estimate must use

\[
\log\mathbb E e^{\lambda |X|}
\le
C\lambda\|X\|_{\psi_2}
+
C\lambda^2\|X\|_{\psi_2}^2,
\tag{5}
\]

not the coarser bound with a fixed factor \(2\). Applying generalized Hölder to (5) yields

\[
\|S_k\|_2\le C_pT.
\tag{6}
\]

Equations (2) and (6) then give

\[
\Gamma\le C_pT.
\tag{7}
\]

## Orlicz closure audit

No term is missing from

\[
K_2
\le
C\left[
T+TK_2+\Gamma(1+K_2)+T^3
\right].
\tag{8}
\]

The terms correspond respectively to:

- the \(P^2\) Gaussian innovation and immediate \(\alpha_{21}H^2\) response;
- current bounded coefficients multiplying \(D^2\);
- all historical \(H^2\) and \(D^2\) response bases;
- the learned \(A\)-memory.

Likewise,

\[
K_1
\le
C\left[
K_2+T+\Gamma+TK_2^2
\right]
\tag{9}
\]

contains:

- the \(P^1\) Gaussian innovation of scale \(\|D^2\|_2\);
- the two current responses;
- all historical responses, whose bases are bounded \(H^1\) or \(O(T)\) fields \(D^3\);
- the learned \(B\)-memory.

There is no missing \(\Gamma K_2\) term in (9): historical right responses multiply \(F\)-nodes \(H^1\) and \(D^3\), not \(D^2\).

At a simultaneous first exit \(K=1\) or \(\Gamma=1\), equations (6)–(9) give

\[
K+\Gamma\le C_pT.
\]

Choosing \(T_*\) with \(C_pT_*<1/2\) closes the bootstrap and proves uniformly in \(h,R\),

\[
\|P^1\|_{\psi_2}+\|P^2\|_{\psi_2}\le C_pT,
\qquad
\Gamma\le C_pT.
\tag{10}
\]

## Cutoff and continuous-time bridge audit

With (10), the downstream bridge passes if organized in the following order:

\[
(R,h)\text{ fixed}
\;\longrightarrow\;
n\to\infty
\;\longrightarrow\;
h\downarrow0
\;\longrightarrow\;
R\to\infty.
\]

A growing-step version of Han is unnecessary.

On the \(R\)-independent energy region,

\[
\|C\|_\infty+\|P^1\|_2+\|P^2\|_2\le CT,
\qquad
\|A\|_{\rm op}+\|B\|_{\rm op}\le C,
\]

the cutoff vector field has reachable-set Lipschitz constant \(C(1+R)\). The comparison estimate is

\[
D_R'(t)
\le
C(1+R)D_R(t)
+
C\sum_{\ell=1}^2
\|P_R^\ell
\mathbf1_{\{|P_R^\ell|>R/2\}}\|_2.
\]

Han’s fixed-mesh averaged theorem, applied to smooth tail majorants, and (10) give

\[
D_R(T)
\le
Ce^{CRT-cR^2/T^2}\to0.
\]

Uniqueness must be stated in the tail-controlled solution class defined by the energy bounds and (10). The same truncation estimate proves uniqueness there, and time shifts remain in the class, giving restartability.

## Action and path-law audit

The action-state construction passes provided the proof explicitly:

1. uses a countable typed cylinder algebra;
2. proves projective consistency of finite probe laws;
3. passes the finite operator-norm bound to the \(L^2\) quotient;
4. passes the finite adjoint identity;
5. includes velocity probes in the algebra.

For each hidden path,

\[
\omega(z_i,\delta)^2
\le
\delta\int_0^T|\dot z_i(t)|^2\,dt.
\]

The velocity DAG contains no new feedback loop. Extending the source induction to its finitely many terminal nodes gives uniform fourth moments. This supplies both path tightness and uniform integrability of the squared path supremum, hence \(W_2(C([0,T];\mathbb R^p))\) convergence.

Final second-round assessment:

\[
\boxed{
\text{After adding (1)–(3), the source-tail and downstream bridge pass this audit.}
}
\]

The exact proof must retain the simultaneous \((K,\Gamma)\) bootstrap; omitting \(\Gamma\) again would reopen the circularity.

The second adversarial pass found a real circularity in my first version: the estimate labeled (M) omitted derivatives propagated through historical state-evolution response sums. A bound on \(K_1,K_2\) alone does not control those terms. The lemma is valid only after adding a simultaneous bootstrap on historical response mass and proving a per-source Volterra estimate.

Here is the corrected version.

## Corrected source-DAG lemma

Let

\[
\mathcal I_k
=
\{(\ell,a,s,\varepsilon):
0\le\ell<k,\ 1\le a\le p,\ s\in\{1,2\},
\ \varepsilon\in\{Z,Y\}\}.
\]

For every scalar decoder \(X\), define its formal old-source derivative norm by

\[
\|D_{<k}X\|_{\ell^1}
=
\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal I_k}|\partial_\iota X|.
\tag{1}
\]

The derivatives are derivatives of the deterministic Han decoder on its ambient Euclidean source space. They remain well-defined when the Gaussian source covariance is singular. Correlation of two source coordinates does not create a formal derivative edge between them.

For microstage \(r=(k,a,s)\), write the exact decoder as

\[
L_r
=
Z_r+\sum_{q<r}\alpha_{rq}G_q+\ell_r,
\]

\[
R_r
=
Y_r+\sum_{q\le r}\beta_{rq}F_q+r_r,
\tag{2}
\]

where

\[
\alpha_{rq}=\mathbb E[\partial_{Y_q}F_r],
\qquad
\beta_{rq}=\mathbb E[\partial_{Z_q}G_r].
\tag{3}
\]

All coefficients and all oracle overlaps are deterministic. Consequently,

\[
DL_r=dZ_r+\sum_{q<r}\alpha_{rq}DG_q+D\ell_r,
\]

\[
DR_r=dY_r+\sum_{q\le r}\beta_{rq}DF_q+Dr_r.
\tag{4}
\]

There are no missing derivatives of \(\alpha,\beta\) or of the oracle overlaps.

The physical node table is

\[
F_{ka1}=(H_k^{1,a},0),
\qquad
G_{ka1}=H_k^{2,a},
\]

\[
F_{ka2}=(0,D_k^{3,a}),
\qquad
G_{ka2}=D_k^{2,a},
\]

with

\[
H^{1,a}=\phi(U^a),\quad
H^{2,a}=\phi(S^a),\quad
H^{3,a}=\phi(T^a),
\]

\[
D^{3,a}=C\phi'(T^a),
\qquad
D^{2,a}=\phi'(S^a)\tau_R(P^{2,a}).
\]

The exact derivative inequalities are

\[
|DH^{1,a}|\le J|DU^a|,
\]

\[
|DH^{2,a}|\le J|DS^a|,
\qquad
|DH^{3,a}|\le J|DT^a|,
\]

\[
|DD^{3,a}|
\le
J|DC|+J_2|C|\,|DT^a|,
\]

\[
|DD^{2,a}|
\le
J|DP^{2,a}|+J_2|P^{2,a}|\,|DS^a|.
\tag{5}
\]

The last bound is uniform in \(R\).

### Per-source influence

For a source \(q\) born in physical cycle \(\ell\), let \(I_{kq}\) be the sum of the absolute derivatives, with respect to \(q\), of:

- \(U_k,C_k\);
- all nodes used in the cycle-\(k\) matrix queries;
- all earlier rank factors when multiplied by their actual \(h\)-weight.

Let

\[
X_k
=
1+\sum_a\bigl(|P_k^{1,a}|+|P_k^{2,a}|\bigr).
\tag{6}
\]

There is a finite constant \(C_{\rm dag}=C(p,\phi)\) with the following property. Suppose, through cycle \(k\),

\[
K_1\vee K_2\le1
\tag{7}
\]

and

\[
\Gamma_j\le\gamma_*:=(4C_{\rm dag})^{-1},
\qquad j\le k,
\tag{8}
\]

where

\[
\Gamma_j
=
\sum_{\substack{r\text{ in cycle }j\\
q\text{ in cycles }<j}}
\bigl(|\alpha_{rq}|+|\beta_{rq}|\bigr).
\tag{9}
\]

Then exact induction through the \(2p\) nodes of a cycle gives

\[
I_{\ell+1,q}\le ChX_\ell^2
\tag{10}
\]

and, for \(k>\ell\),

\[
I_{k+1,q}
\le
I_{kq}
+
ChX_k I_{kq}.
\tag{11}
\]

Why every inter-cycle term has \(h\):

- passage through the \(U\)- or \(C\)-update has its explicit Euler factor \(h\);
- passage through a learned \(A\)- or \(B\)-rank memory has its explicit factor \(h\);
- a historical response edge has coefficient
  \[
  \mathbb E[\partial_qF_r]
  \quad\text{or}\quad
  \mathbb E[\partial_qG_r].
  \]
  By the preceding microstage induction, the derivative on the right has already crossed one of the first two \(h\)-edges.

Repeated or perfectly correlated Gaussian innovations create covariance, but no additional formal derivative path.

The response terms in (4) give a gain at most \(C_{\rm dag}\Gamma_k\). Condition (8) allows this gain to be absorbed on the left. This is the step omitted in my previous proof.

Iterating (10)–(11),

\[
I_{kq}
\le
ChX_\ell^2
\exp\left(
Ch\sum_{m=\ell+1}^{k-1}X_m
\right).
\tag{12}
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal S_k=\sum_{q\in\mathcal I_k}I_{kq}.
\]

There are exactly \(4p\) new scalar sources per physical cycle, so

\[
\mathcal S_k
\le
Ch\sum_{\ell<k}X_\ell^2
\exp\left(
Ch\sum_{m=\ell+1}^{k-1}X_m
\right).
\tag{13}
\]

Under (7), generalized Hölder, with no independence assumption, gives

\[
\sup_{k\le N}\|\mathcal S_k\|_{L^2}
\le CT,
\qquad Nh\le T.
\tag{14}
\]

Indeed, the \(X_k\) have uniformly bounded \(L^q\) norms of order \(C\sqrt q\), and

\[
\mathbb E\exp\left(
qCh\sum_{m=r}^{k}X_m
\right)
\le e^{C_qT}.
\]

### Response mass

From (3), (5), and the definition of \(I_{kq}\),

\[
\Gamma_k
\le
C\,
\mathbb E
\left[
\left(1+\sum_a|P_k^{2,a}|\right)\mathcal S_k
\right].
\]

Cauchy–Schwarz and (14) give

\[
\Gamma_k\le CT.
\tag{15}
\]

Choose \(T\) so that

\[
CT\le\frac{\gamma_*}{2}.
\tag{16}
\]

Thus the response estimate strictly improves bootstrap condition (8).

### Field bootstrap

The deterministic oracle bounds give

\[
\|C_k\|_\infty\le CT,
\qquad
\max_a|e_{k,a}|\le2,
\qquad
|D_k^{3,a}|\le CT.
\tag{17}
\]

The centered Gaussian innovation of \(P^{2,a}\) has variance at most \(CT^2\). Same-cycle response terms are bounded by \(CT(1+K_2)\), historical responses by \(C\Gamma_k(1+K_2)\), and the learned \(A^\top\)-memory by \(CT^3\). Hence

\[
K_2
\le
C\left[
T+TK_2+\Gamma_k(1+K_2)+T^3
\right].
\tag{18}
\]

The centered innovation of \(P^{1,a}\) has scale at most \(CK_2\). The current response terms are bounded by \(C(K_2+T)\), the historical terms by \(C\Gamma_k\), and the learned \(B^\top\)-memory by \(CTK_2^2\). Thus

\[
K_1
\le
C\left[
K_2+T+\Gamma_k+TK_2^2
\right].
\tag{19}
\]

Using (15), and then choosing \(T_0\) smaller if necessary,

\[
K_1\vee K_2\le CT_0\le\frac12.
\tag{20}
\]

At the limiting initialization \(C=0\), hence \(P^1=P^2=0\), and there is no historical response mass. If a first discrete cycle violated any of (7)–(8), estimates (15) and (18)–(20), applied through the preceding cycle, would improve every threshold strictly. This contradiction proves the simultaneous bootstrap for all \(k\le T_0/h\).

Therefore

\[
\sup_{k,a,r}
\|P_k^{r,a}\|_{\psi_2}\le CT,
\qquad r=1,2.
\tag{21}
\]

Integration of the sub-Gaussian tail yields

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
|P_k^{r,a}|^2
\mathbf1_{\{|P_k^{r,a}|>u\}}
\right]
\le
CT^2
\left(1+\frac{u^2}{T^2}\right)
e^{-cu^2/T^2}.
\tag{22}
\]

This conclusion is uniform in \(h,R\).

## Tail transfer and cutoff comparison

The empirical transfer must not use a discontinuous indicator directly. For fixed \(u\), choose a pseudo-Lipschitz function \(\chi_u\) satisfying

\[
x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>u\}}
\le
\chi_u(x)
\le
x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>u/2\}}.
\]

At fixed \(h,R\), Han’s averaged theorem applies to \(\chi_u\). Higher polynomial test functions give uniform integrability. Consequently the empirical cutoff fields inherit (22) after \(n\to\infty\). The estimates are uniform in \(h\), so one may then take \(h\downarrow0\).

For uncut and cutoff limiting solutions, the exact decomposition

\[
\begin{aligned}
\phi'(S)P-\phi'(S_R)\tau_R(P_R)
={}&
\phi'(S)(P-P_R)\\
&+
[\phi'(S)-\phi'(S_R)]P_R\\
&+
\phi'(S_R)[P_R-\tau_R(P_R)]
\end{aligned}
\]

and splitting on \(\{|P_R|\le R/2\}\) give

\[
d_R'(t)
\le
C(1+R)d_R(t)
+
C\sum_{r=1}^2
\|P_R^r\mathbf1_{\{|P_R^r|>R/2\}}\|_2.
\]

By (22),

\[
d_R(T)
\le
C e^{C(1+R)T}
e^{-cR^2/T^2}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Only cutoff-solution tails are used, so this comparison is non-circular.

## Verdict

The original version of the lemma was not rigorous because it omitted the response-mass factor in the derivative recursion. With the simultaneous \((K_1,K_2,\Gamma)\) first-exit bootstrap and the per-source influence estimate (10)–(13), that circularity is removed.

The lemma itself now closes. It remains contingent on correctly embedding the oracle recursion into Han’s exact asymmetric decoder; the lemma does not independently prove that separate identification theorem.

## Exact four-map audit

All four physical projections and signs are correct:

\[
B_kH^1
=
B_0H^1
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}D_{\ell b}^2
\langle H_{\ell b}^1,H^1\rangle,
\]

\[
A_kH^2
=
A_0H^2
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}D_{\ell b}^3
\langle H_{\ell b}^2,H^2\rangle,
\]

\[
A_k^\top D^3
=
A_0^\top D^3
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}H_{\ell b}^2
\langle D_{\ell b}^3,D^3\rangle,
\]

\[
B_k^\top D^2
=
B_0^\top D^2
-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}H_{\ell b}^1
\langle D_{\ell b}^2,D^2\rangle.
\]

The full direct-sum Han maps also generate two unused components:

\[
B_k^\top H^2,
\qquad
A_kD^2.
\]

Their rank corrections involve

\[
\langle D_{\ell b}^2,H_{ka}^2\rangle,
\qquad
\langle H_{\ell b}^2,D_{ka}^2\rangle.
\]

These two overlaps must be retained in the oracle scalar ledger. They cause no new physical response paths because every future physical decoder is defined to have identically zero derivative with respect to:

- the type-1 output of the first microstage;
- the type-3 output of the second microstage.

They still remain in the full GFOM history and in Han’s Gaussian covariance structure. Marginalizing them is legitimate precisely because the subsequent decoders ignore them.

The additive maps \(G^{\langle1\rangle}\) and \(F^{\langle2\rangle}\) do not generate new Han response bases. The response coefficients are generated by derivatives of the query maps \(F^{\langle1\rangle}\) and \(G^{\langle2\rangle}\).

## All-source lemma audit

The earlier bulk argument was circular because it used

\[
\Gamma_k\le CT
\]

while deriving the current \(K_{2,k}\), even though its displayed bound for \(\Gamma_k\) contained the current \(K_{2,k}\).

The lexicographic repair closes this.

For the stage \(r=(k,a,2)\) producing \(P_{ka}^2\), only the \(\alpha\)-responses of

\[
F_r=(0,D_{ka}^3)
\]

enter. But \(D_{ka}^3=C_k\phi'(T_{ka})\) is completely formed before \(P_{ka}^2\). Therefore its derivatives involve only:

- earlier physical cycles;
- the current bounded \(H_{ka}^2,T_{ka}\) chain;
- already completed microstages.

They do not involve \(P_{ka}^2\). Consequently,

\[
\Gamma^{(2)}_{ka}
:=
\sum_{q<r}|\alpha_{rq}|
\le CT
\]

under the induction hypothesis for earlier microstages.

Since the centered innovation in \(P_{ka}^2\) has scale \(O(T)\), the same-cycle response is \(O(T)\), and the learned \(A^\top\)-memory is \(O(T^3)\),

\[
\|P_{ka}^2\|_{\psi_2}
\le C(T+T^3).
\]

This bounds \(P_{ka}^2\) before \(P_{ka}^1\) is considered.

Now

\[
D_{ka}^2
=
\phi'(S_{ka})\tau_R(P_{ka}^2)
\]

has sub-Gaussian scale \(O(T)\), uniformly in \(R\). Its derivative satisfies

\[
|DD_{ka}^2|
\le
\|\phi'\|_\infty |DP_{ka}^2|
+
\|\phi''\|_\infty |P_{ka}^2|\,|DS_{ka}|.
\]

Thus the \(\beta\)-response mass entering \(P_{ka}^1\) is \(O(T)\), and

\[
\|P_{ka}^1\|_{\psi_2}\le CT.
\]

Induction first over \(a\), then over \(k\), gives

\[
\sup_{k,a}
\left(
\|P_{ka}^1\|_{\psi_2}
+
\|P_{ka}^2\|_{\psi_2}
\right)
\le CT.
\]

The derivative table should additionally record

\[
|DH^{3,a}|\le\|\phi'\|_\infty|DT^a|,
\qquad
|D\tau_R(P^{1,a})|\le|DP^{1,a}|.
\]

With those additions, the slow-history recurrence is complete.

No covariance inverse or independence of source coordinates is used. Formal derivatives are derivatives of the ambient decoder functions; generalized Hölder controls the correlated Gaussian evaluations. Singular covariance therefore causes no gap.

## Fixed-\((h,R)\) Han audit

After conditioning on the independent \(w(0),C(0)\) marks and smoothly clipping the negligible \(C(0)\) tails:

- \(\mathbb A_0=[B_0,A_0^\top]\) is \(n\times2n\) with iid \(N(0,1/n)\) entries;
- the aspect ratio is \(1/2\);
- every map is deterministic and row-separate;
- every global empirical scalar has been replaced by a deterministic oracle scalar;
- for fixed \(h,R,T\), all map values, Lipschitz constants and derivatives through order three have a deterministic \(n\)-independent bound;
- products and smooth tail tests are pseudo-Lipschitz.

Han therefore applies to the fixed finite program.

The finite-stage state-evolution coefficients converge to deterministic population coefficients by induction and the law of large numbers over the independent side marks. Singular covariance is harmless because finite-dimensional positive-semidefinite square roots vary continuously.

The actual-oracle comparison closes only after including the learned rank memories in the error:

\[
\delta_k
=
\delta_k^{\mathrm{slow}}
+
\|R_k^A-\widehat R_k^A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+
\|R_k^B-\widehat R_k^B\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
\]

Then

\[
\delta_{k+1}
\le
(1+C_Rh)\delta_k+C_Rh\zeta_n,
\]

where \(\zeta_n\) contains all output and overlap discrepancies, including the two unused-component overlaps. Discrete Grönwall yields \(\sup_k\delta_k\to0\) in probability.

## Reachable-module and GNS audit

The correct object is a reachable typed module, not an algebra closed under arbitrary products.

Generate it from:

- current marks and constants;
- rational linear combinations;
- the bounded Lipschitz coordinate maps actually used;
- multiplication by bounded factors;
- \(\mathbb A_0,\mathbb A_0^\ast\) and the type projections.

Han gives convergence of every finite set of probe Gram matrices and joint coordinate laws. Quotienting by the limiting null seminorm and completing gives the three Hilbert spaces.

The high-probability operator bound

\[
\|\mathbb A_0\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le4
\]

passes to the quotient and makes the limiting operator bounded. The finite identity

\[
\langle\mathbb A_0v,u\rangle
=
\langle v,\mathbb A_0^\ast u\rangle
\]

passes to the limit, so the limiting transpose is genuinely the Hilbert adjoint.

For fixed \(R\), bounded multipliers make the vector field locally Lipschitz in

\[
(U,C,\mathbb A)
\in
(\mathcal H_1)^p
\times\mathcal H_3
\times
\mathcal B(\mathcal H_1\oplus\mathcal H_3,\mathcal H_2).
\]

Thus fixed-cutoff existence, uniqueness and restartability follow.

Ambient \(L^2\) Picard theory must not be claimed for the uncut vector field: the multiplier \(P^2\) is unbounded. The uncut flow is instead obtained as the \(R\to\infty\) limit, with uniqueness in the reachable uniform-sub-Gaussian class proved by the same cutoff comparison.

## Three-\(\varepsilon\) audit

The correct order is:

1. Fix \(R\) and \(h\).
2. Apply fixed-stage Han and pass \(n\to\infty\).
3. Use the dimension-uniform cutoff Euler estimate and send \(h\downarrow0\).
4. Use the uniform sub-Gaussian tail estimate and send \(R\uparrow\infty\).

Finite-width cutoff tails must be transferred before passing \(n\to\infty\). If \(X\) is a cutoff-ODE field and \(Y\) its Euler approximation, then

\[
\|X\mathbf1_{\{|X|>R\}}\|_2
\le
2\|X-Y\|_2
+
2\|Y\mathbf1_{\{|Y|>R/2\}}\|_2.
\]

The second term is controlled by Han’s smooth empirical tail tests, while the first is controlled by the uniform Euler estimate. This supplies the required

\[
\lim_{R\to\infty}
\limsup_{n\to\infty}
d(X_n^{\mathrm{uncut}},X_{n,R}^{\mathrm{cut}})
=0.
\]

Hence the three-\(\varepsilon\) passage really proves convergence along the full diagonal sequence. It is not merely a separate width limit followed by an assumed flow limit.

Therefore the repaired fixed-mesh proof passes this audit. The growing-stage Han argument remains unproved and should be removed.

Final audit verdict: the lexicographic source-DAG argument is clean, and the algebraic/activity part passes without a label-dependent exception.

### Lexicographic repair

Order microstages by

\[
(k,a,1)<(k,a,2)<(k,a+1,1).
\]

At \((k,a,1)\), the nodes are generated in the order

\[
S^a\longrightarrow H^{2,a}\longrightarrow T^a
\longrightarrow H^{3,a}\longrightarrow D^{3,a}.
\]

At \((k,a,2)\),

\[
P^{2,a}\longrightarrow D^{2,a}\longrightarrow P^{1,a}.
\]

This removes the apparent circularity:

- \(D^{3,a}=C\phi'(T^a)\) is completely determined before \(P^{2,a}\), and satisfies
  \[
  |D^{3,a}|\le CT.
  \]
- The centered innovation of \(P^{2,a}\) therefore has scale \(O(T)\).
- Every same-cycle response entering \(P^{2,a}\) differentiates \(D^{3,a}\), hence also carries \(C=O(T)\).
- Historical responses entering \(P^{2,a}\) multiply only lexicographically earlier \(H^2\) or \(D^2\) fields.
- Once \(P^{2,a}=O_{\psi_2}(T)\) is established, \(D^{2,a}=O_{\psi_2}(T)\).
- The innovation of \(P^{1,a}\) then has scale \(O(T)\); its current response coefficients contain only
  \[
  \phi'(S)\tau_R'(P^2)
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  \phi''(S)\tau_R(P^2),
  \]
  which are bounded by \(C\) and \(C|P^2|\), respectively.
- \(P^{1,a}\) never appears on its own right-hand side.

Thus induction over microstages gives directly

\[
\|P_k^{2,a}\|_{\psi_2}\le CT,
\qquad
\|P_k^{1,a}\|_{\psi_2}\le CT.
\]

No continuity argument or simultaneous first exit for \(K_1,K_2\) is needed.

For a source \(q\) born in cycle \(\ell\), its influence satisfies the discrete Volterra estimate

\[
I_{kq}
\le
ChX_\ell^2
+
Ch\sum_{m=\ell+1}^{k-1}X_mI_{mq},
\qquad
X_m=1+\sum_a(|P_m^{1,a}|+|P_m^{2,a}|).
\]

Hence

\[
I_{kq}
\le
ChX_\ell^2
\exp\left(
Ch\sum_{m=\ell+1}^{k-1}X_m
\right).
\]

The reason is exhaustive: an old source can reach a later physical cycle only through

1. an \(h\)-weighted \(U\)- or \(C\)-update;
2. an \(h\)-weighted learned-rank update; or
3. a response coefficient
   \[
   \mathbb E[\partial_qF_r]
   \quad\text{or}\quad
   \mathbb E[\partial_qG_r],
   \]
   whose derivative was already computed at an earlier microstage and has crossed one of the first two edges.

Singular covariance only correlates formal sources; it creates no derivative edge. Generalized Hölder therefore gives

\[
\left\|\sum_{q<k}I_{kq}\right\|_{L^2}\le CT,
\]

and consequently historical response mass is \(O(T)\). This proves the claimed sub-Gaussian and tail bounds uniformly in \(h,R\).

### Independent activity audit

The exact normalized dynamics and all four NTK blocks have the correct factors. In particular,

\[
\Theta^{(1)}_{ab}
=
R_{ab}
\langle\phi'(U^a)P^{1,a},
       \phi'(U^b)P^{1,b}\rangle_n
\]

contains neither a missing \(n\) nor a missing \(d\).

For \(\phi=\sin+\cos\),

\[
Q_1=e^{-1+R},\qquad
Q_2=e^{-1+Q_1},\qquad
Q_3=e^{-1+Q_2}
\]

are strictly positive definite whenever the inputs are pairwise distinct.

Let

\[
g(T)=\sum_ay_a\phi(T_a),
\qquad
(v_3)_a=y_ag(T)\phi'(T_a),
\qquad
M_3=\mathbb E[v_3v_3^\top].
\]

Full support of \(T\sim N(0,Q_2)\) and analyticity imply \(M_3>0\). The two exact Gaussian transpose-cavity decompositions then give

\[
M_2>0,\qquad M_1>0.
\]

Therefore

\[
E_A=\operatorname{tr}(Q_2M_3)>0,
\]

\[
E_B=\operatorname{tr}(Q_1M_2)>0,
\]

\[
E_w=\operatorname{tr}(RM_1)>0.
\]

The last remains positive even when \(R\) is singular because \(R\ge0\), \(R\neq0\), and \(M_1>0\).

The exact small-time coefficients are

\[
y^\top\Theta^{(3)}(t)y
=
\gamma^2E_A t^2+O(t^3),
\]

\[
y^\top\Theta^{(2)}(t)y
=
\gamma^2E_B t^2+O(t^3),
\]

\[
y^\top\Theta^{(1)}(t)y
=
\gamma^2E_w t^2+O(t^3),
\]

and

\[
y^\top[\Theta^{(4)}(t)-\Theta^{(4)}(0)]y
=
\gamma^2(E_w+E_B+E_A)t^2+O(t^3).
\]

Hence

\[
y^\top[\Theta(t)-\Theta(0)]y
=
2\gamma^2(E_w+E_B+E_A)t^2+O(t^3)>0.
\]

There is no possible cancellation.

Since \(e(t)=-y+O(t)\),

\[
e(t)^\top\Theta^{(\ell)}(t)e(t)
=
\gamma^2E_\ell t^2+O(t^3)>0,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3.
\]

The output block is active at initialization because

\[
y^\top Q_3y>0.
\]

Feature velocities satisfy

\[
D_U\Psi[V_U]=E_w>0,
\]

\[
D_S\Psi[V_S]=E_w+E_B>0,
\]

\[
D_T\Psi[V_T]=E_w+E_B+E_A>0,
\]

so all three hidden preactivations have nonzero \(L^2\) velocity coefficients and nonzero displacement.

Finally,

\[
\dot{\mathcal L}(0)
=
-\frac4{p^2}y^\top Q_3y<0,
\]

and the exact gradient-flow identity makes the loss nonincreasing thereafter. The nonlinear regression residual is initially positive for every hidden marginal \(N(0,1)\) and remains positive on a nonzero interval by \(W_2\) continuity.

No exceptional choice of distinct inputs or \(\{\pm1\}\)-labels invalidates these conclusions. The coefficients can become arbitrarily small as oppositely labelled inputs approach one another, but remain strictly positive for every fixed distinct dataset.

Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root

Payload:
Yes. At fixed \(h,R\), the omitted averaged theorem can be removed completely. Han’s entrywise asymmetric state-evolution theorem, combined with a self-contained Gaussian-concentration argument, yields every empirical convergence needed by the oracle coupling.

## 1. Normalized stability with respect to the Gaussian matrix

Write

\[
\mathbb A_0=\frac{G}{\sqrt n},
\qquad
G\in\mathbb R^{n\times2n}
\]

with iid standard Gaussian entries. Use

\[
\|u\|_L^2=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^nu_j^2,
\qquad
\|v\|_R^2=\frac1n\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n}v_\ell^2.
\]

For \(M>1+\sqrt2\), let \(\Pi_M\) be the metric projection, in Frobenius norm, onto the closed convex spectral-norm ball

\[
\{A:\|A\|_{\rm op}\le M\}.
\]

Metric projections onto closed convex subsets of Hilbert spaces are nonexpansive, so

\[
\left\|
\Pi_M(G/\sqrt n)-\Pi_M(\widetilde G/\sqrt n)
\right\|_{\rm op}
\le
\frac1{\sqrt n}\|G-\widetilde G\|_F.
\tag{1}
\]

Let \(A=\Pi_M(G/\sqrt n)\) and \(\widetilde A=\Pi_M(\widetilde G/\sqrt n)\). For fixed \(h,R,T\), all four oracle maps are bounded and globally Lipschitz. Let \(B\) bound their normalized output norms and let \(\Lambda\) bound their rowwise Lipschitz constants.

Define

\[
d_r^2
=
\sum_{q\le r}\|u^q-\widetilde u^q\|_L^2
+
\sum_{q\le r}\|v^q-\widetilde v^q\|_R^2.
\]

For the left update,

\[
u^r=AF_r^{\langle1\rangle}(v^{<r})
+G_r^{\langle1\rangle}(u^{<r}),
\]

we have

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|u^r-\widetilde u^r\|_L
&\le
\|(A-\widetilde A)F_r^{\langle1\rangle}(v^{<r})\|_L\\
&\quad+
\|\widetilde A[
F_r^{\langle1\rangle}(v^{<r})
-
F_r^{\langle1\rangle}(\widetilde v^{<r})
]\|_L\\
&\quad+
\|G_r^{\langle1\rangle}(u^{<r})
-G_r^{\langle1\rangle}(\widetilde u^{<r})\|_L\\
&\le
B\|A-\widetilde A\|_{\rm op}
+
\Lambda(M+1)d_{r-1}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

For the right update,

\[
v^r=A^\top G_r^{\langle2\rangle}(u^{\le r})
+F_r^{\langle2\rangle}(v^{<r}),
\]

the same calculation gives

\[
\|v^r-\widetilde v^r\|_R
\le
B\|A-\widetilde A\|_{\rm op}
+
\Lambda(M+1)
\bigl(d_{r-1}+\|u^r-\widetilde u^r\|_L\bigr).
\tag{3}
\]

Induction over the fixed number \(s=2p\lceil T/h\rceil\) of microstages yields

\[
d_s
\le
C_{s,M,\Lambda,B}
\|A-\widetilde A\|_{\rm op}.
\]

Using (1),

\[
\boxed{
d_s
\le
\frac{C_{s,M,\Lambda,B}}{\sqrt n}
\|G-\widetilde G\|_F.
}
\tag{4}
\]

All constants are deterministic and uniform in the conditioned side marks.

## 2. Concentration of bounded empirical observables

Let

\[
Z_n(G)
=
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi_i(\mathcal H_i(G)),
\]

where \(\mathcal H_i\) is the finite coordinate history on one physical species and every \(\psi_i\) is \(L_\psi\)-Lipschitz.

By Cauchy–Schwarz and (4),

\[
\begin{aligned}
|Z_n(G)-Z_n(\widetilde G)|
&\le
\frac{L_\psi}{n}
\sum_i
\|\mathcal H_i(G)-\mathcal H_i(\widetilde G)\|\\
&\le
L_\psi d_s\\
&\le
\frac{L_\psi C_s}{\sqrt n}
\|G-\widetilde G\|_F.
\end{aligned}
\tag{5}
\]

Gaussian concentration therefore gives, conditionally on the independent side marks,

\[
\Pr\left(
|Z_n-\mathbb E[Z_n\mid\mathcal S_n]|>u
\,\middle|\,\mathcal S_n
\right)
\le
2\exp\left(
-\frac{nu^2}{2L_\psi^2C_s^2}
\right).
\tag{6}
\]

This was proved for the spectrally clipped matrix. For an \(n\times2n\) Gaussian matrix,

\[
\Pr\left(
\left\|\frac G{\sqrt n}\right\|_{\rm op}>M
\right)
\le e^{-c_Mn}.
\]

On the complementary event, spectral clipping changes nothing. Hence for bounded \(Z_n\), its expectation and probability law differ from the unclipped observable by at most \(O(e^{-c_Mn})\). Thus (6) also gives concentration for the original Gaussian operator.

## 3. Identifying the expectation with entrywise state evolution

Condition on the side marks. Apply Han’s entrywise asymmetric theorem separately to each coordinate with \(S=\{i\}\).

For any bounded \(C^3\) test \(\psi_i\) with uniform derivative bounds,

\[
\left|
\mathbb E[
\psi_i(\mathcal H_i)
\mid\mathcal S_n]
-
\mathbb E[
\psi_i(\mathcal H_i^{\rm SE})
\mid\mathcal S_n]
\right|
\le\varepsilon_n,
\tag{7}
\]

uniformly in \(i\), where, for fixed \(s\),

\[
\varepsilon_n
\le
C
(\Lambda\Lambda_\psi\log n)^{Cs^5}
n^{-1/c_0^s}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Averaging (7),

\[
\left|
\mathbb E[Z_n\mid\mathcal S_n]
-
\frac1n\sum_i
\mathbb E[
\psi_i(\mathcal H_i^{\rm SE})
\mid\mathcal S_n]
\right|
\le\varepsilon_n.
\tag{8}
\]

The fixed-stage side-mark induction proves

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\mathbb E[
\psi_i(\mathcal H_i^{\rm SE})
\mid\mathcal S_n]
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\psi(\mathcal H^{\rm SE})
\tag{9}
\]

in probability, or almost surely after coupling the iid marks. This induction remains valid at singular covariances by continuity of the positive-semidefinite square root.

Combining (6), (8), and (9) proves convergence in probability of every bounded smooth empirical observable to its deterministic state-evolution value.

Coordinate-dependent tests caused by the side marks are allowed: after conditioning, they are deterministic, and the derivative bounds used above are uniform. The Gaussian \(w(0)\)-marks occur inside bounded activation maps, while the negligible \(C(0)\)-marks are clipped on an event whose complement has probability \(O(ne^{-n^2/2})\).

## 4. Pseudo-Lipschitz and unbounded observables

Let \(\psi\) be pseudo-Lipschitz of polynomial order. Choose a smooth cutoff

\[
\psi_M(x)
=
\psi(x)\,
\chi\!\left(\frac{\|x\|^2}{M^2}\right).
\]

For fixed \(M\), \(\psi_M\) is bounded, globally Lipschitz and \(C^3\), so the preceding argument applies.

Choose an even integer \(q\) larger than twice the growth order of \(\psi\). The polynomial test

\[
x\longmapsto \|x\|^q
\]

satisfies Han’s entrywise \(C^3\) polynomial-growth test condition. Fixed-stage Gaussian state evolution has finite \(q\)-th moments, so (7), averaged over coordinates, gives

\[
\sup_n
\mathbb E\left[
\frac1n\sum_i
\|\mathcal H_i\|^q
\right]
<\infty.
\tag{10}
\]

Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\left[
\frac1n\sum_i
|\psi(\mathcal H_i)-\psi_M(\mathcal H_i)|
\right]
&\le
C M^{-(q-r)}
\mathbb E\left[
\frac1n\sum_i
(1+\|\mathcal H_i\|^q)
\right]\\
&\le
C M^{-(q-r)},
\end{aligned}
\tag{11}
\]

where \(r\) is the growth order of \(\psi\). The same estimate holds for the state-evolution variables.

Letting first \(n\to\infty\) and then \(M\to\infty\) establishes empirical convergence for pseudo-Lipschitz tests.

In particular, smooth approximations to

\[
x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>u\}}
\]

are covered. Equation (10) supplies uniform integrability, while the state-evolution sub-Gaussian estimate supplies the sharp limiting tail bound needed for cutoff removal.

For the raw \(U\)-paths, one may either use the same truncation or write

\[
U(t)=U(0)+[U(t)-U(0)],
\]

where \(U(0)\) is an iid Gaussian mark and the cutoff displacement is bounded on compact time intervals.

## 5. What this establishes

The following empirical quantities now converge without invoking Han’s averaged theorem:

- every output and error;
- every same-type Gram and cross-Gram in the exact four-map scalar ledger;
- all smooth empirical tail observables;
- every finite family of reachable action probes;
- the terminal output, NTK blocks and loss.

Thus the fixed-\((h,R)\) oracle coupling and the reachable-module/GNS construction use only Han’s entrywise theorem plus the concentration proof above.

There is no internal gap in this replacement for fixed stage count.

Two limitations remain:

1. This argument does not justify \(h=h_n\downarrow0\) inside Han. Its constant \(C_s\) grows with the number of stages, and no quantitative growing-stage side-mark/concentration analysis has been supplied. The three-\(\varepsilon\), fixed-mesh route remains necessary.

2. The primary Han source itself describes the asymmetric entrywise AMP ingredient as an extension of another result rather than spelling out every detail. Therefore this repair eliminates reliance on the explicitly omitted averaged theorem, but it does not independently re-prove Han’s entrywise theorem.

Third-round verdict: fixed-stage empirical convergence can be derived from Han’s entrywise state-evolution theorem alone. The averaged theorem is not necessary.

## 1. Conditional one- and two-coordinate estimates

Fix \(h,R\), so the serialized recursion has a fixed finite number \(q\) of stages. Condition on the independent first/output-layer marks. After smooth clipping of \(P^1,P^2,C\), every oracle map is deterministic, row-separate, \(C^3\), and has globally bounded derivatives through order three. Han’s entrywise theorem therefore applies.

For a same-species empirical probe

\[
A_n=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\psi_i(X_i),
\]

let

\[
a_{i,n}
=
\mathbb E_{\rm SE}\psi_i(\widehat X_i),
\qquad
\bar a_n=\frac1n\sum_i a_{i,n}.
\]

The one-coordinate theorem gives, uniformly in \(i\),

\[
\left|
\mathbb E[A_n\mid\mathcal M]-\bar a_n
\right|
\le \varepsilon_n,
\tag{1}
\]

where \(\mathcal M\) denotes the side marks and \(\varepsilon_n\to0\).

Apply the same entrywise theorem with a two-coordinate set
\(S=\{i,j\}\) and test

\[
\Psi_{ij}(x_i,x_j)
=
\psi_i(x_i)\psi_j(x_j).
\]

The Gaussian state-evolution matrices have independent rows. This is explicit in the asymmetric AMP construction underlying Han’s theorem. Conditional on the marks, deterministic row transformations preserve that independence. Hence, for \(i\neq j\),

\[
\mathbb E_{\rm SE}
[\psi_i(\widehat X_i)\psi_j(\widehat X_j)]
=
a_{i,n}a_{j,n}.
\]

The two-coordinate theorem yields

\[
\left|
\operatorname{Cov}
(\psi_i(X_i),\psi_j(X_j)\mid\mathcal M)
\right|
\le C\varepsilon_n.
\tag{2}
\]

For the diagonal terms, apply the one-coordinate theorem to \(\psi_i^2\). Therefore

\[
\operatorname{Var}(A_n\mid\mathcal M)
\le
\frac Cn+C\varepsilon_n.
\tag{3}
\]

Equations (1)–(3) and conditional Chebyshev imply

\[
A_n-\bar a_n\longrightarrow0
\]

in conditional probability, uniformly on every good mark event. This proves concentration of every fixed empirical probe without invoking the averaged theorem.

Polynomial probes such as Gram entries are admissible: the one-coordinate test has fixed polynomial growth, and the two-coordinate product has another fixed polynomial order. The cutoff recursion supplies the necessary uniform moments.

## 2. Independent random marks

Conditional on the marks, the maps are deterministic. Uniform theorem constants hold because:

- \(U_0\) appears in dynamics only through
  \(\phi(U_0+\delta U)\) and its derivatives, all uniformly bounded;
- \(C_0\) is smoothly saturated;
- the formal Han initialization vectors can be zero.

The finite-\(n\) conditional state-evolution coefficients depend on empirical averages of iid marks. At each fixed stage, induction gives their convergence to deterministic population quantities:

1. assume all earlier covariance and response coefficients converge;
2. Gaussian expectations are continuous in covariance, including at singular covariances;
3. the integrands have uniform polynomial domination;
4. apply the iid law of large numbers to the local mark functions.

This proves

\[
\bar a_n\longrightarrow a
\]

in probability over the marks. Combining this with (3),

\[
\Pr(|A_n-a|>\epsilon)
=
\mathbb E_{\mathcal M}
\Pr(|A_n-a|>\epsilon\mid\mathcal M)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

No independence between probes at different times is required.

## 3. Unbounded first-layer paths

The unbounded Gaussian \(U_0\) does not violate the map hypotheses because only bounded functions of \(U_0+\delta U\) occur inside the GFOM maps.

For tests involving the raw path \(U_0+\delta U\), work on

\[
\max_i\|U_{0,i}\|\le C\sqrt{\log n},
\]

whose probability tends to one. A quadratic path test has theorem constant \(O(\log n)\), which is harmless for a fixed number of stages because Han’s error contains a negative power of \(n\).

Alternatively, bounded path tests follow immediately, while second moments follow from

\[
\sup_{t\le T}|U_i(t)|^2
\le
2|U_{0,i}|^2
+
2T\int_0^T|\dot U_i(t)|^2\,dt.
\]

Gaussian fourth moments of \(U_0\) and the repaired source-tail estimate for the velocity probes give uniform integrability. Thus the conditional concentration argument extends to raw \(U\)-path \(W_2\) convergence.

## 4. Lexicographic source repair

The final lexicographic version passes.

At a \(P_r^2\) microstage:

- its input \(D_r^3\) and every \(\alpha_{rq}\) depend only on earlier microstages;
- response bases are current bounded \(H^2\) or already constructed earlier \(D^2\).

Hence

\[
K_{2,r}
\le
C\left[
T+\Gamma_r^F(1+K_{2,<r})+T^3
\right]
\le CT.
\]

After \(P_r^2\) is known, so is \(D_r^2\). At the following \(P_r^1\) stage:

- the Gaussian innovation has scale at most \(CK_{2,r}\);
- every \(\beta\)-response base is bounded \(H^1\) or \(O(T)\) field \(D^3\);
- \(\beta_{22}=O(1)\) multiplies \(D^3=O(T)\);
- \(\beta_{21}=O(K_{2,r}+T)\) multiplies bounded \(H^1\).

Therefore

\[
K_{1,r}\le CT.
\]

The per-source Volterra recursion uses only earlier microstages, so induction first over microstages and then over physical cycles is noncircular. Retain the separately tracked deterministic historical response mass and the derivative bound for \(H^3\).

## 5. Three-\(\varepsilon\) quantifiers

For any finite collection of action probes and \(\varepsilon>0\):

1. Choose \(R\) so cutoff removal contributes less than \(\varepsilon\):
   \[
   \limsup_n
   \Pr\bigl(d(X_n,X_n^R)>\varepsilon\bigr)<\varepsilon.
   \]

2. With this \(R\) fixed, choose \(h\) so both finite-width and limiting cutoff Euler errors are below \(\varepsilon\).

3. With \(R,h\) fixed, choose \(n\) so the entrywise-derived empirical concentration, mark-law convergence, and oracle coupling errors are below \(\varepsilon\).

4. Increase \(n\) further so exact GD and finite-width ODE differ by less than \(\varepsilon\).

This yields convergence along the full sequence, not merely along a diagonal subsequence. For the countable action metric, first truncate its \(2^{-r}\)-weighted probe sum, then apply the preceding argument to the resulting finite collection.

Final third-round conclusion:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{gathered}
\text{The lexicographic all-source repair passes.}\\
\text{Fixed-stage empirical convergence follows from one- and two-coordinate}\\
\text{entrywise state evolution, independent of Han’s averaged theorem.}\\
\text{The conditional-to-unconditional and three-\(\varepsilon\) passages pass.}
\end{gathered}}
\]

Let

\[
U\sim N(0,R),\qquad
S\sim N(0,Q_1),\qquad
T\sim N(0,Q_2),
\]

with the independent copies used below explicitly indicated.

Define

\[
g(T)=\sum_{b=1}^p y_b\phi(T_b),
\]

\[
(v_3(T))_a
=
y_a g(T)\phi'(T_a),
\]

\[
M_3
=
\mathbb E_T[v_3(T)v_3(T)^\top],
\]

and

\[
(\Lambda_3)_{ac}
=
\mathbb E_T[\partial_{T_c}(v_3(T))_a].
\]

Explicitly,

\[
(\Lambda_3)_{ac}
=
\mathbb E_T\left[
y_ay_c\phi'(T_a)\phi'(T_c)
+
\mathbf1_{\{a=c\}}y_ag(T)\phi''(T_a)
\right].
\]

Now take \(S\sim N(0,Q_1)\) and

\[
\xi_3\sim N(0,M_3)
\]

independently, and define the first transpose-cavity field

\[
\Pi_3
=
\xi_3+\Lambda_3\phi(S),
\]

where \(\phi(S)\) is componentwise. Set

\[
v_2
=
\phi'(S)\odot\Pi_3,
\qquad
M_2
=
\mathbb E_{S,\xi_3}[v_2v_2^\top].
\tag{1}
\]

Define

\[
(\Lambda_2)_{ac}
=
\mathbb E_{S,\xi_3}
[\partial_{S_c}(v_2)_a].
\]

Since

\[
(v_2)_a
=
\phi'(S_a)
\left[
(\xi_3)_a+\sum_d(\Lambda_3)_{ad}\phi(S_d)
\right],
\]

this is explicitly

\[
\begin{aligned}
(\Lambda_2)_{ac}
=
\mathbb E\big[
&\mathbf1_{\{a=c\}}\phi''(S_a)(\Pi_3)_a\\
&+
\phi'(S_a)(\Lambda_3)_{ac}\phi'(S_c)
\big].
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

Next take \(U\sim N(0,R)\) and

\[
\xi_2\sim N(0,M_2)
\]

independently, and define the second transpose-cavity field

\[
\Pi_2
=
\xi_2+\Lambda_2\phi(U).
\]

Finally set

\[
v_1
=
\phi'(U)\odot\Pi_2,
\qquad
M_1
=
\mathbb E_{U,\xi_2}[v_1v_1^\top].
\tag{3}
\]

These are precisely the matrices in

\[
E_B=\operatorname{tr}(Q_1M_2),
\qquad
E_w=\operatorname{tr}(RM_1).
\]

For positive definiteness, condition first on \(S\). For every \(z\neq0\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
z^\top M_2z
&=
\mathbb E[(z^\top v_2)^2]\\
&\ge
\mathbb E\!\left[
(D_{\phi'(S)}z)^\top
M_3
(D_{\phi'(S)}z)
\right]>0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Here \(M_3>0\), and \(D_{\phi'(S)}\) is invertible almost surely because each \(S_a\sim N(0,1)\) and \(\phi'\) has only discrete zeros.

Similarly,

\[
z^\top M_1z
\ge
\mathbb E\!\left[
(D_{\phi'(U)}z)^\top
M_2
(D_{\phi'(U)}z)
\right]>0.
\]

This remains valid when \(R\) is singular: every marginal \(U_a\) is still \(N(0,1)\), so

\[
\mathbb P(\phi'(U_a)=0)=0.
\]

Since \(p<\infty\), \(D_{\phi'(U)}\) is invertible almost surely. Thus \(M_1>0\) without requiring \(R>0\).

Payload:
Verdict: the fixed-\((h,R)\) width identification can be made unconditional without Han’s theorem.

The clean route is a specialized, self-contained Gaussian-program theorem using the two independent square matrices \(A_0,B_0\). It avoids both Han’s full-history extension and the more delicate scalar extension of Tensor Programs.

### Finite Gaussian-program lemma

Let \(G_1,\dots,G_s\) be independent \(n\times n\) matrices with iid \(N(0,1/n)\) entries. Let the initial vector coordinates be iid copies of a finite, possibly singular, Gaussian vector. Consider a fixed finite program built from:

- \(G_rv\) and \(G_r^\top v\), with arbitrary finite reuse;
- fixed globally Lipschitz coordinate maps;
- deterministic scalar coefficients.

Then every empirical continuous polynomial-growth test converges in probability to the corresponding Gaussian response DAG. No covariance nonsingularity is required.

The proof is direct. Enumerate matrix-generated vectors. If the next one is \(g=Wh\), collect all earlier uses

\[
X=WY,\qquad U=W^\top V.
\]

Conditionally on the past,

\[
W\overset d=
E+P_V^\perp \widetilde W P_Y^\perp,
\]

where

\[
E=XY^++V^{+\top}U^\top
   -V^{+\top}U^\top YY^+.
\]

Consequently,

\[
g\overset d=
Eh+\sigma P_V^\perp z,
\qquad
\sigma^2=\frac1n\|P_Y^\perp h\|^2,
\]

with \(z\sim N(0,I_n)\) independent of the past.

Maintain a core family of preceding matrix-generated vectors whose limiting coordinate law is equivalent to Lebesgue measure and which exactly spans all preceding matrix-generated vectors.

If the limiting innovation variance is zero, then

\[
Z^h=\sum_i c_iZ^{Y_i}
\quad\text{in }L^2.
\]

The difference is a continuous function of the core variables. Since their law is equivalent to Lebesgue measure, a continuous function that vanishes almost everywhere vanishes everywhere. Hence the identical relation holds coordinatewise at finite width:

\[
h=\sum_i c_iY_i,
\qquad
g=\sum_i c_iX_i.
\]

This proves exact rank stability in the singular branch.

If the limiting innovation variance is positive, the relevant positive Gram eigenvalues remain bounded away from zero with probability tending to one. Pseudoinverses therefore converge on the nonzero subspace. The new limiting core coordinate has an independent nondegenerate conditional Gaussian component, so the enlarged core law remains equivalent to Lebesgue measure.

For conditional concentration, put \(P=P_V^\perp\). At most \(\operatorname{rank}V=O(1)\) diagonal entries of \(P\) vanish. On the remaining coordinates, writing

\[
\rho_{ij}=\frac{P_{ij}}{\sqrt{P_{ii}P_{jj}}},
\]

one has

\[
\sum_{i<j}\rho_{ij}^2
\le 2r^2+6r,
\qquad r=\operatorname{rank}V.
\]

Hermite expansion gives, for centered functions of two correlated Gaussian variables,

\[
|\operatorname{Cov}(f_i(Z_i),f_j(Z_j))|
\le
|\rho_{ij}|
\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}f_i\,\operatorname{Var}f_j}.
\]

Thus, for bounded coordinate tests,

\[
\operatorname{Var}\!\left[
\frac1n\sum_i f_i((Pz)_i)
\,\middle|\,\text{past}
\right]
\le \frac{C_r}{n}.
\]

The deterministic conditional parameters may be replaced by their limits because

\[
\frac1n\|\omega-\bar\omega\|^2\to0,\qquad
\sigma\to\bar\sigma,
\]

and

\[
\frac1n\sum_i(1-\sqrt{P_{ii}})
\le
\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{rank}V}{n}}.
\]

This closes bounded-Lipschitz convergence. Conditional Gaussian moment estimates and the finite-rank conditional mean give empirical moments of every order bounded in probability. Truncation then extends convergence to all polynomial-growth tests, including every Gram and kernel expression.

### Exact response identity

Suppose

\[
H=\Phi(\widehat U_1,\dots,\widehat U_m,\mathcal R),
\qquad
C_{ij}=\operatorname{Cov}(\widehat U_i,\widehat U_j)
      =\mathbb E[V_iV_j].
\]

Let

\[
\alpha_j=\mathbb E[\partial_j\Phi].
\]

Gaussian integration by parts, valid also for singular \(C\), gives

\[
b_i:=\mathbb E[\widehat U_iH]
=\sum_jC_{ij}\alpha_j.
\]

The conditional-regression response is therefore

\[
V^\top C^+b
=
V^\top C^+C\alpha
=
V^\top\alpha
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]

The omitted coefficient lies in \(\ker C\), and its corresponding linear combination of \(V\) has zero variance. Thus the total response is unique even though individual coefficients need not be unique at a singular covariance.

Applied to

\[
(B_0,H^1),\quad(B_0^\top,D^2),
\qquad
(A_0,H^2),\quad(A_0^\top,D^3),
\]

this is exactly the OMFP reused-adjoint rule. It includes:

- all previous \(B_0^\top D^2\to B_0H^1\) responses;
- the current and previous \(B_0H^1\to B_0^\top D^2\) responses;
- all analogous \(A_0/A_0^\top\) responses.

The learned rank-one terms remain explicit deterministic vector additions, so no response is lost.

### Oracle construction is causal

At cycle \(k\), the order is

\[
U_k,H_k^1
\to S_k,H_k^2
\to T_k,H_k^3,e_k
\to D_k^3,P_k^2,D_k^2,P_k^1
\to (U_{k+1},C_{k+1},A_{k+1},B_{k+1}).
\]

The only oracle overlaps are

\[
\langle H_\ell^1,H_k^1\rangle_n,\qquad
\langle D_\ell^2,D_k^2\rangle_n,\qquad
\langle H_\ell^2,H_k^2\rangle_n,\qquad
\langle D_\ell^3,D_k^3\rangle_n,
\]

together with the output residuals. Each is defined only after both participating nodes have been constructed. Hence there is no implicit fixed point or circular definition.

Keeping \(A_0\) and \(B_0\) separate also removes the two artificial cross-overlap families introduced by the combined rectangular Han serialization.

### Actual–oracle coupling

The physical normalized readout satisfies

\[
\|C_0\|_n=O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1}),
\]

so replacing it by the exact zero vector changes every fixed number of cutoff steps by \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\).

Track

\[
U,\quad C,\quad
R_k^A=-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}D_{\ell b}^3\otimes_nH_{\ell b}^2,
\quad
R_k^B=-\gamma h\sum_{\ell<k,b}
e_{\ell b}D_{\ell b}^2\otimes_nH_{\ell b}^1.
\]

Using

\[
\|u\otimes_n v\|_{\mathrm{op}}
=\|u\|_n\|v\|_n
\]

and the Gaussian operator-norm bound for \(A_0,B_0\), the actual-oracle error obeys, for fixed \(h,R,K\),

\[
\delta_{k+1}
\le C_{h,R,K}\delta_k+C_{h,R,K}\zeta_n,
\]

where \(\zeta_n\to0\) contains precisely the four overlap families and output errors above. Finite-step Grönwall gives \(\max_{k\le K}\delta_k\to0\) in probability.

### Compatibility with the uniform-in-\(h\) analysis

The deterministic source argument survives unchanged after reindexing the sources into four blocks per cycle:

\[
B\text{-forward},\quad
A\text{-forward},\quad
A\text{-adjoint},\quad
B\text{-adjoint}.
\]

Every source reaching a later cycle still crosses an \(h\)-weighted slow update or learned-rank update. Same-cycle forward-to-adjoint paths remain in the lexicographic induction. Splitting response mass between \(A\) and \(B\) changes only a numerical factor of at most two.

No state-evolution theorem uniform in \(h\) is required: width tends to infinity at each fixed \(h,R\), after which the deterministic source estimates are uniform in \(h,R\).

The cutoff should be a \(C^\infty\), \(1\)-Lipschitz saturation that equals the identity on \([-R,R]\), not \(R\tanh(x/R)\), because cutoff removal uses an error supported in the tail.

### Audit of the Tensor Programs fallback

[Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10685) formally contains an even more general parameterless theorem covering multiple matrices, transpose reuse, and singular initial Gaussian covariances. Its parameterless proof has several uniquely repairable notation or exponent slips but no structural defect relevant here.

I would not use its separate scalar no-rank-stability extension: that portion contains an apparent incorrect normalization in the rewritten-core construction and an overly compressed \(A\Delta h\) lemma. The deterministic-oracle reduction above avoids that extension entirely.

Therefore the external provenance issue can be closed: the fixed-\((h,R)\) OMFP identification has a direct proof, and the downstream continuous-time theorem need not depend on Han’s disputed full-history step.

Audit verdict:

- **Fixed \((h,R,K)\) OMFP identification: clean and unconditional.**
- **Full uncut continuous-time/GD theorem: not yet unconditional unless a uniform finite-width cutoff-removal estimate has already been proved.**

Tensor Programs III directly covers the deterministic-oracle construction:

- \(A_0,B_0\) are independent \(N(0,1/n)\) matrices.
- Arbitrary reuse of each matrix and its transpose is allowed.
- Singular Gaussian initial covariance is allowed.
- Parameterless polynomially bounded coordinate maps and polynomially bounded empirical tests are covered.
- Rank stability is proved internally by the CoreSet induction; it is not an assumption.
- The finite-rank formulas for learned \(A_k,B_k\) actions are exact.
- Population errors and overlaps are causal deterministic constants, so the oracle is a finite parameterless NetsorT program.
- Actual-to-oracle coupling closes by finite induction using the Gaussian operator-norm bound, TP convergence of the finitely many overlaps, and \(\|C_0\|_n=O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1})\).

At singular covariances, the OMFP response must be stated canonically. For \(W=A_0\) or \(B_0\), use

\[
Z^{Wx}
=
\widehat Z^{Wx}
+
\sum_i a_i Z^{y_i},
\qquad
a=C^+b,
\]

where

\[
C_{ij}=\mathbb E[Z^{y_i}Z^{y_j}],
\qquad
b_i=\mathbb E[\widehat Z^{W^\top y_i}Z^x].
\]

This is TP III’s Moore–Penrose rule and equals the derivative \(Z\)-dot formula when the derivative representation is valid. With this convention, the TP state evolution and the all-history OMFP response DAG agree exactly, including singular input Gram matrices.

The unresolved point is coordinatewise cutoff removal. Fixed-\(R\) Gaussian fields are not pointwise bounded, so one cannot claim the cutoff becomes inactive at finite width. To recover the original uncut dynamics one needs, for some \(\delta>0\), a dynamics-specific estimate such as

\[
\sup_n
\int_0^T
\frac1n\sum_i
\left(
|P_i^1(t)|^{2+\delta}
+
|P_i^2(t)|^{2+\delta}
\right)\,dt
<\infty
\]

in expectation or with suitable high probability, together with a stability estimate comparing clipped and unclipped trajectories. Gaussian tails only after taking \(n\to\infty\) do not suffice.

TP III can also identify the uncut system at every fixed mesh because polynomial-growth nonlinearities are allowed. It does not, however, provide estimates uniform as \(K=T/h\to\infty\). Thus fixed-mesh uncut identification does not by itself establish the joint continuous-time limit.

So the TP replacement completely repairs the previously missing fixed-step width-limit theorem. The remaining cutoff-removal estimate, if absent from the earlier bridge, is a substantive continuous-time gap rather than a consequence of TP III.

Yes. With the stated finite-width source estimate, cutoff removal is complete.

The crucial decomposition is

\[
\phi'(u)p-\phi'(u_R)\tau_R(p_R)
=
\phi'(u)(p-p_R)
+\phi'(u)\bigl(p_R-\tau_R(p_R)\bigr)
+\bigl(\phi'(u)-\phi'(u_R)\bigr)\tau_R(p_R).
\]

Thus only the clipped field appears with an \(L^\infty\) bound \(O(R)\); the uncut field never needs a tail estimate. Consequently,

\[
D_R'(t)
\le C_T(1+R)D_R(t)+C_TS_{n,R}(t),
\]

where \(S_{n,R}\) is the sum of the squared clipped-source tails. Grönwall and the claimed estimate give

\[
\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\mathbb E\sup_{s\le T}D_R(s)
\le
C_T\exp(C_TR-cR^2)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The valid order is:

\[
R,h\ \text{fixed},\ n\to\infty;
\qquad
R\ \text{fixed},\ h\to0;
\qquad
R\to\infty.
\]

No uniform-in-\(n\) tail estimate for the uncut trajectory and no TP theorem with \(K\to\infty\) are used. Compatible cutoffs similarly show that the limiting clipped flows are Cauchy. The discrete-GD comparison follows by the same decomposition and the Lipschitzness of \(I-\tau_R\).

The only necessary audit condition is that

\[
\limsup_n\mathbb E\int_0^T S_{n,R}(t)\,dt
\le C_Te^{-cR^2}
\]

is genuinely proved at finite width, or equivalently replaced by a sufficient convergence-in-probability statement. A sub-Gaussian estimate only after taking the width limit would not justify the displayed expectation bound.

Given the finite-width/source lemma as stated, the final verdict is **clean**: TP III resolves fixed-\((h,R)\) identification, and the clipped-source estimate closes cutoff removal and the continuous/GD bridges without changing the limit order.

### Fixed-mesh Gaussian action law

Write
\[
\langle u,v\rangle_n:=\frac1n u^\top v,\qquad
u\otimes_n v:=\frac1nuv^\top,\qquad
\eta:=h\gamma=\frac{2h}{p},
\]
and write \(r\) for the fixed cutoff radius. Thus \(\tau_r\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)\) is \(1\)-Lipschitz, agrees with the identity on \([-r,r]\), and satisfies \(|\tau_r|\le 2r\). Put
\[
\mathcal R_{ab}:=\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d}.
\]
No nonsingularity of \(\mathcal R\) is assumed.

At mesh time \(k\), the clipped Euler scheme is
\[
\begin{aligned}
&H_{1,k}^a=\phi(U_k^a),\qquad
S_k^a=B_kH_{1,k}^a,\qquad
H_{2,k}^a=\phi(S_k^a),\\
&T_k^a=A_kH_{2,k}^a,\qquad
H_{3,k}^a=\phi(T_k^a),\qquad
f_{k,a}=\langle C_k,H_{3,k}^a\rangle_n,\qquad e_{k,a}=f_{k,a}-y_a,\\
&D_{3,k}^a=C_k\odot\phi'(T_k^a),\qquad
P_{2,k}^a=A_k^\top D_{3,k}^a,\\
&D_{2,k}^a=\phi'(S_k^a)\odot\tau_r(P_{2,k}^a),\qquad
P_{1,k}^a=B_k^\top D_{2,k}^a,
\end{aligned}
\]
followed by
\[
\begin{aligned}
U_{k+1}^a
 &=U_k^a-\eta\sum_{b=1}^p
 \mathcal R_{ab}e_{k,b}\,
 \phi'(U_k^b)\odot\tau_r(P_{1,k}^b),\\
B_{k+1}
 &=B_k-\eta\sum_{b=1}^p e_{k,b}\,
 D_{2,k}^b\otimes_nH_{1,k}^b,\\
A_{k+1}
 &=A_k-\eta\sum_{b=1}^p e_{k,b}\,
 D_{3,k}^b\otimes_nH_{2,k}^b,\\
C_{k+1}
 &=C_k-\eta\sum_{b=1}^pe_{k,b}H_{3,k}^b .
\end{aligned}
\]

The conclusion below is meant pointwise in any fixed finite probe DAG: one may append finitely many applications of \(A_k,A_k^\top,B_k,B_k^\top\), deterministic linear combinations, and bounded Lipschitz coordinate maps to the history available at time \(k\).

#### Proposition

Fix \(r,h,K<\infty\). Under the natural product coupling in \(n\), the following hold almost surely as \(n\to\infty\).

1. Every fixed finite family of history vectors and current-action probes has a deterministic joint coordinate law given by the all-history Gaussian operator DAG constructed below. Empirical averages converge for bounded Lipschitz tests.

2. If \(X_n,Y_n\) are any two such vectors and \((\mathsf X,\mathsf Y)\) are their DAG variables, then
   \[
   \langle X_n,Y_n\rangle_n\longrightarrow
   \mathbb E[\mathsf X\mathsf Y].
   \]
   In particular,
   \[
   f_{k,a}\longrightarrow
   \bar f_{k,a}:=\mathbb E[\mathsf C_k\mathsf H_{3,k}^a].
   \]

3. There is a single probability-one event on which these assertions hold simultaneously for any prescribed countable probe grammar. Its positive quadratic kernel has a canonical GNS realization, described below.

#### The all-history base-matrix rule

For each \(M\in\{A_0,B_0\}\) and orientation \(s\in\{+,-\}\), with \(M^+=M\) and \(M^-=M^\top\), construct action nodes in their program order. Suppose the next request is \(M^sX\). Let
\[
M^{-s}Y_1,\ldots,M^{-s}Y_q
\]
be all preceding opposite-orientation calls. Introduce a centered Gaussian innovation
\(\widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,s}[X]\). For calls of the same orientation, these innovations are jointly Gaussian with
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
 \widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,s}[X]\,
 \widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,s}[X']
 \right]
 =\mathbb E[XX'].
\]
The innovation families belonging to different matrices or different orientations are mutually independent and are independent of the initial Gaussian variables.

Define
\[
G_{ij}:=\mathbb E[Y_iY_j],\qquad
b_i:=\mathbb E\!\left[
 \widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,-s}[Y_i]\,X
 \right],
\]
and set
\[
\mathsf M^{\,s}[X]
 :=
 \widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,s}[X]
 +\sum_{i=1}^q (G^+b)_iY_i,
 \tag{1}
\]
where \(G^+\) is the Moore–Penrose inverse; the sum is zero when \(q=0\).

This definition remains valid when \(G\) is singular. Indeed, if \(z\in\ker G\), then
\[
\sum_i z_iY_i=0
\quad\text{and}\quad
\sum_i z_i\widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,-s}[Y_i]=0
\qquad\text{a.s.}
\]
Consequently \(z^\top b=0\), so \(b\in\operatorname{ran}G\). The response in (1) is the unique element of
\(\operatorname{span}\{Y_1,\ldots,Y_q\}\) whose inner products with the \(Y_j\)'s equal \(b_j\); it is therefore unchanged by adding redundant opposite-orientation calls. Formula (1) is precisely the derivative-free singular-covariance form of the TP III \(Z\mathrm{MatMul}\) rule. When the symbolic derivatives exist, it equals the usual Onsager response
\[
\sum_iY_i\,\mathbb E
 \frac{\partial X}
 {\partial\widehat{\mathsf M}^{\,-s}[Y_i]}.
\]

#### Autonomous limiting recursion

Let
\[
(\mathsf U_0^1,\ldots,\mathsf U_0^p)
 \sim N(0,\mathcal R),\qquad \mathsf C_0=0.
\]
For a previously constructed variable \(X\), define the learned action laws
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf B_k[X]
 &=\mathsf B^{+}[X]
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k}\sum_b
 \bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \mathsf D_{2,\ell}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf H_{1,\ell}^bX],\\
\mathsf B_k^\dagger[X]
 &=\mathsf B^{-}[X]
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k}\sum_b
 \bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \mathsf H_{1,\ell}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf D_{2,\ell}^bX],\\
\mathsf A_k[X]
 &=\mathsf A^{+}[X]
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k}\sum_b
 \bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \mathsf D_{3,\ell}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf H_{2,\ell}^bX],\\
\mathsf A_k^\dagger[X]
 &=\mathsf A^{-}[X]
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k}\sum_b
 \bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \mathsf H_{2,\ell}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf D_{3,\ell}^bX].
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]
Recursively for \(0\le k<K\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf H_{1,k}^a&=\phi(\mathsf U_k^a),
&
\mathsf S_k^a&=\mathsf B_k[\mathsf H_{1,k}^a],
&
\mathsf H_{2,k}^a&=\phi(\mathsf S_k^a),\\
\mathsf T_k^a&=\mathsf A_k[\mathsf H_{2,k}^a],
&
\mathsf H_{3,k}^a&=\phi(\mathsf T_k^a),
&
\bar f_{k,a}&=\mathbb E[\mathsf C_k\mathsf H_{3,k}^a],\\
\bar e_{k,a}&=\bar f_{k,a}-y_a,
&
\mathsf D_{3,k}^a&=\mathsf C_k\phi'(\mathsf T_k^a),
&
\mathsf P_{2,k}^a&=\mathsf A_k^\dagger[\mathsf D_{3,k}^a],\\
\mathsf D_{2,k}^a
 &=\phi'(\mathsf S_k^a)\tau_r(\mathsf P_{2,k}^a),
&
\mathsf P_{1,k}^a
 &=\mathsf B_k^\dagger[\mathsf D_{2,k}^a],
\end{aligned}
\tag{3}
\]
and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf U_{k+1}^a
 &=\mathsf U_k^a-\eta\sum_b\mathcal R_{ab}\bar e_{k,b}
 \phi'(\mathsf U_k^b)\tau_r(\mathsf P_{1,k}^b),\\
\mathsf C_{k+1}
 &=\mathsf C_k-\eta\sum_b\bar e_{k,b}\mathsf H_{3,k}^b .
\end{aligned}
\tag{4}
\]
This is autonomous: at every line, all expectations involve already constructed variables. It is “all-history” both because (2) retains every earlier rank-one update and because (1) uses every earlier opposite-orientation application of the corresponding initial matrix. It would be incorrect to replace transpose calls by fresh independent Gaussian matrices.

All variables in (3)–(4) have moments of every order. Indeed,
\[
|\phi|,\ |\phi'|,\ |\phi''|\le\sqrt2,\qquad |\tau_r|\le2r.
\]
Thus \(\mathsf C_k,\mathsf D_{3,k}^a,\mathsf D_{2,k}^a\) are bounded for fixed \(k\); each base action is a Gaussian innovation plus a finite deterministic linear combination of earlier variables; and every increment of \(\mathsf U_k^a\) is bounded.

#### The parameterless finite-width oracle

For every DAG vector \(\mathsf X\), let \(\bar X_n\in\mathbb R^n\) denote its oracle realization. Start with
\[
\bar U_{0,n}^a=U_{0,n}^a,\qquad \bar C_{0,n}=0,
\]
and use the same \(A_0^n,B_0^n\) as the true dynamics. Replace each learned action in (2), for example, by
\[
\begin{aligned}
\overline{\mathcal A}_{k,n}[\bar X_n]
 &:=A_0^n\bar X_n
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}\bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \bar D_{3,\ell,n}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf H_{2,\ell}^b\mathsf X],\\
\overline{\mathcal A}_{k,n}^{\dagger}[\bar X_n]
 &:=(A_0^n)^\top\bar X_n
 -\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}\bar e_{\ell,b}\,
 \bar H_{2,\ell,n}^b\,
 \mathbb E[\mathsf D_{3,\ell}^b\mathsf X],
\end{aligned}
\tag{5}
\]
with the analogous two formulas for \(B_0^n\). Run (3)–(4) using (5) and the deterministic errors \(\bar e_{k,a}\).

This oracle is a parameterless \(\mathrm{NetsorT}\) program:

- its only matrix variables are \(A_0^n,B_0^n\), whose entries are independent \(N(0,1/n)\);
- its initial vector slices are iid copies of the possibly degenerate Gaussian
  \((\mathsf U_0^1,\ldots,\mathsf U_0^p,0)\);
- every coefficient in (5) is deterministic and independent of \(n\);
- every nonlinearity is polynomially bounded;
- \(A_0,B_0\) and their transposes may be reused arbitrarily.

The parameterless TP III Master Theorem therefore applies without any rank-stability assumption. Its exact conclusion is that for every finite oracle vector family
\(\bar X_n^1,\ldots,\bar X_n^m\) and every polynomially bounded test \(\Psi\),
\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
 \Psi(\bar X_{n,i}^1,\ldots,\bar X_{n,i}^m)
 \longrightarrow
 \mathbb E\Psi(\mathsf X^1,\ldots,\mathsf X^m)
 \qquad\text{a.s.}
\tag{6}
\]
The TP III symbolic rule identifying the right-hand side is exactly (1). In particular, neither singularity of \(\mathcal R\) nor singularity of any later action Gram matrix causes a missing hypothesis.

#### Coupling the true dynamics to the oracle

Unrolling the true matrix updates gives the exact identities
\[
\begin{aligned}
B_kv
 &=B_0v-\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}
 e_{\ell,b}D_{2,\ell}^b
 \langle H_{1,\ell}^b,v\rangle_n,\\
B_k^\top v
 &=B_0^\top v-\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}
 e_{\ell,b}H_{1,\ell}^b
 \langle D_{2,\ell}^b,v\rangle_n,\\
A_kv
 &=A_0v-\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}
 e_{\ell,b}D_{3,\ell}^b
 \langle H_{2,\ell}^b,v\rangle_n,\\
A_k^\top v
 &=A_0^\top v-\eta\sum_{\ell<k,b}
 e_{\ell,b}H_{2,\ell}^b
 \langle D_{3,\ell}^b,v\rangle_n.
\end{aligned}
\tag{7}
\]

We use \(\|v\|_n=\langle v,v\rangle_n^{1/2}\). The Gaussian operator-norm tail bound implies
\[
\sup_{n\ge n_0}\bigl(\|A_0^n\|_{\mathrm{op}}+
\|B_0^n\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)<\infty
\quad\text{a.s.}
\tag{8}
\]
Moreover, because \(C_0=nc\) and \(c_i\sim N(0,n^{-4})\),
\[
\mathbb E\|C_0\|_n^2=n^{-2}.
\]
Markov and Borel–Cantelli therefore give
\[
\|C_0-\bar C_0\|_n=\|C_0\|_n\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{a.s.}
\tag{9}
\]

We now induct over the finite, totally ordered list of computations through time \(K\). The only nontrivial induction step is a learned action. The following elementary estimate handles all four cases in (7). Suppose
\[
x_n-\bar x_n,\quad u_{j,n}-\bar u_{j,n},\quad
v_{j,n}-\bar v_{j,n}\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in }\|\cdot\|_n,
\]
\(\alpha_{j,n}\to\bar\alpha_j\), and (6) gives
\[
\langle\bar v_{j,n},\bar x_n\rangle_n
 \longrightarrow\mathbb E[\mathsf V_j\mathsf X].
\]
Then, using (8), Cauchy–Schwarz, and the eventual boundedness of all displayed norms and scalars,
\[
\begin{split}
&\left\|M_0x_n+\sum_j\alpha_{j,n}u_{j,n}
 \langle v_{j,n},x_n\rangle_n\right.\\
&\hspace{35mm}\left.
 -M_0\bar x_n-\sum_j\bar\alpha_j\bar u_{j,n}
 \mathbb E[\mathsf V_j\mathsf X]\right\|_n
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{split}
\tag{10}
\]
Indeed, expand each summand by adding and subtracting
\(\alpha_{j,n}\bar u_{j,n}
 \langle\bar v_{j,n},\bar x_n\rangle_n\) and
\(\bar\alpha_j\bar u_{j,n}
 \langle\bar v_{j,n},\bar x_n\rangle_n\).

All coordinatewise lines are stable under normalized \(L^2\). For example,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|D_{3,k}^a-\bar D_{3,k}^a\|_n
&\le\sqrt2\|C_k-\bar C_k\|_n
 +\sqrt2\|\bar C_k\|_\infty
   \|T_k^a-\bar T_k^a\|_n,\\
\|D_{2,k}^a-\bar D_{2,k}^a\|_n
&\le 2\sqrt2\,r\,
  \|S_k^a-\bar S_k^a\|_n
 +\sqrt2\|P_{2,k}^a-\bar P_{2,k}^a\|_n.
\end{aligned}
\tag{11}
\]
The same bound applies to
\(\phi'(U)\tau_r(P_1)\). Here \(\|\bar C_k\|_\infty<\infty\) because \(\bar C_0=0\) and \(\bar C_k\) is a finite linear combination of bounded \(H_3\)'s.

Finally,
\[
\begin{aligned}
|f_{k,a}-\bar f_{k,a}|
&\le
 \|C_k-\bar C_k\|_n\|H_{3,k}^a\|_n
 +\|\bar C_k\|_n
   \|H_{3,k}^a-\bar H_{3,k}^a\|_n\\
&\quad+
 \left|\langle\bar C_k,\bar H_{3,k}^a\rangle_n
 -\mathbb E[\mathsf C_k\mathsf H_{3,k}^a]\right|,
\end{aligned}
\tag{12}
\]
and the last term tends to zero by (6). Hence
\(e_{k,a}\to\bar e_{k,a}\), closing the induction. Thus, for every history or appended probe \(X_n\),
\[
\|X_n-\bar X_n\|_n\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{a.s.}
\tag{13}
\]

Equation (13), (6), and Cauchy–Schwarz imply all claimed quadratic limits. They also imply empirical convergence for bounded Lipschitz tests, since
\[
\frac1n\sum_i
 |\Psi(X_{n,i}^1,\ldots,X_{n,i}^m)
   -\Psi(\bar X_{n,i}^1,\ldots,\bar X_{n,i}^m)|
 \le L_\Psi\sum_j\|X_n^j-\bar X_n^j\|_n.
\]

#### Countable action law and GNS state

Fix a countable, topologically ordered probe grammar \(\mathscr Q\) containing the network history, the constant probe \(1\), rational linear combinations, bounded truncations, and the desired current actions. For every finite initial segment, the preceding construction gives joint variables \(\{\mathsf Z_q:q\in\mathscr Q\}\). Appending probes does not alter earlier nodes, so these finite-dimensional laws are consistent. Their quadratic kernel
\[
\Gamma(q,q'):=\mathbb E[\mathsf Z_q\mathsf Z_{q'}]
\]
is positive semidefinite:
\[
\sum_{i,j}\alpha_i\alpha_j\Gamma(q_i,q_j)
 =\mathbb E\left(\sum_i\alpha_i\mathsf Z_{q_i}\right)^2\ge0.
\]

Let \(\mathcal H_0\) be the rational linear span of \(\mathscr Q\), quotient by
\[
\mathcal N:=\{q:\Gamma(q,q)=0\},
\]
and complete it in the norm induced by \(\Gamma\). This gives a real Hilbert space \(\mathcal H\).

The action maps descend to the quotient. In fact, for every \(q,q'\),
\[
\Gamma(A_kq,q')=\Gamma(q,A_k^\dagger q'),\qquad
\Gamma(B_kq,q')=\Gamma(q,B_k^\dagger q'),
\tag{14}
\]
because these are limits of the exact finite-width transpose identities. They are bounded: Gaussian operator norms are eventually bounded, while
\[
\|u\otimes_n v\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 =\|u\|_n\|v\|_n,
\]
and only finitely many rank-one updates occur before \(K\). Hence \(A_k^\dagger\) and \(B_k^\dagger\) are the Hilbert adjoints of \(A_k\) and \(B_k\).

Adjoin multiplication operators by bounded coordinate probes. With
\(\Omega=[1]\), let \(\mathfrak A_{r,h,K}\) be the generated unital \(C^*\)-algebra on the cyclic closure of \(\Omega\), and define
\[
\omega_{r,h,K}(T):=\langle\Omega,T\Omega\rangle_{\mathcal H}.
\tag{15}
\]
Then \(\omega_{r,h,K}\) is normalized and positive:
\[
\omega_{r,h,K}(T^*T)=\|T\Omega\|_{\mathcal H}^2\ge0.
\]
Bounded truncations recover every \(L^2\) history probe by closure, so (15) contains all quadratic kernels and current-action laws. This is the precise autonomous action-law/GNS state associated with the clipped mesh.

#### Exact limitation

There is no remaining gap for fixed \(r,h,K\), any fixed finite probe DAG, quadratic observables, or a prescribed countable probe grammar. The argument does not prove simultaneous uniform convergence over an uncountable family of probes, probes chosen in a width-dependent manner after seeing the matrices, operator-norm convergence of the full trained matrices, or arbitrary discontinuous polynomial-growth tests of the true scalar-feedback dynamics. TP III supplies such tests for the parameterless oracle, but normalized-\(L^2\) coupling transfers bounded Lipschitz and quadratic observables only. Any theorem asserting one of those stronger conclusions has an unclosed gap without an additional uniform/high-moment argument.
